=== PHYSICAL PAGE 2 ===
ORIUS
Observation–Reality Integrity for Universal Safety
Runtime Safety Under Degraded Observation
Dissertation-Style Research Manuscript
Pratik Niroula
Minnesota State University, Mankato
CIT Major, Mathematics Minor
Canonical ORIUS manuscript built from one active thesis surface with
tiered cross-domain evidence and manifest-backed claim discipline.
ORIUS Safety Principle
“Physical AI safety is bounded by observation reliability. ”
May 2026

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 3 ===
Abstract
Physical AI systems do not act on reality directly. They act on measurements, ﬁltered state
estimates, delayed telemetry, and learned summaries of the world. When that observation
surface degrades, an action can remain admissible on the observed state while already being
unsafe on the true state. This dissertation treats that discrepancy as an action-semantics
safety problem rather than as a prediction problem alone, and names it the observation–
action safety gap (OASG).
Existing lines of work address important parts of this failure mode: conformal uncertainty
quantiﬁes predictive spread, safe control constrains actions, runtime assurance supervises
release, and fault monitoring detects degraded telemetry. What is still missing is the inte-
grated runtime object that connects those pieces at the moment of physical release. ORIUS,
Observation–Reality Integrity for Universal Safety, is proposed as that missing object: a
certiﬁcate-aware runtime safety layer for physical AI under degraded observation.
The dissertation develops ORIUS through three implementation-grounded surfaces. First,
DomainAdapter makes the domain safety predicate, fallback semantics, and repair geometry
explicit. Second, a universal benchmark schema turns degraded-observation replay into a
common evaluation contract across domains. Third, CertOS provides certiﬁcate lifecycle,
audit continuity, and release semantics so that repaired action can be governed as a run-
time object rather than reported only as controller-side logic. The formal contribution is
a domain-agnostic theorem ladder connecting degraded observation, reliability-conditioned
uncertainty, repaired action, fallback, and certiﬁcate semantics under explicit assumptions.
The quantitative safety guarantees are stated as ﬁnite-sample marginal coverage bounds
rather than as per-observation conditional guarantees: they control aggregate true-state risk
over the joint distribution of observations, faults, and calibration residuals.
Empirically, the dissertation establishes one deep witness row and two bounded promoted
rows without changing what ORIUS means at the release boundary. Battery provides the
strongest theorem-to-runtime closure. The autonomous-vehicle row is bounded to completed
nuPlan replay artifacts under the TTC plus predictive-entry-barrier contract. Healthcare is
bounded to retrospective MIMIC-backed monitoring and alert-release semantics. The result
i

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 4 ===
ii
is a thesis that is universal in architecture and tiered in evidence. The manuscript therefore
separates what is proved, what is validated, and what remains outside the current public
claim surface. That separation is enforced not only by prose but by a claim-locked publication
surface in which runtime and governance tables, theorem audits, and manifest-backed ﬁgures
govern what the dissertation is allowed to say.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 5 ===
Acknowledgments
This thesis was assembled as a solo research project around one question: how should a
physical-AI system behave when the observation channel is no longer trustworthy, but the
consequences of action remain physical and irreversible? ORIUS was built to answer that
question with a runtime contract rather than a domain-speciﬁc controller.
The deepest current evidence still comes from the battery witness row because it oﬀers
the cleanest theorem-to-code-to-artifact surface in the repository. The broader contribution
of the book, however, depends on carrying that safety grammar across bounded nuPlan A V
replay and retrospective healthcare monitoring without letting the prose outrun the evidence.
The open-source systems, datasets, and research communities behind the methods cited
in this book made that synthesis possible. Their work is visible throughout the bibliog-
raphy, the artifact index, and the reviewer-facing appendices that helped harden the ﬁnal
manuscript.
iii

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 6 ===
Preface
ORIUS is written as a dissertation-style research manuscript rather than as a ﬂat dump of
experiments, proofs, and appendices. The goal is not to present every domain as equally
mature. The goal is to make one universal runtime safety claim readable without hiding
where the evidence is deepest, where it is bounded, and where it is still incomplete.
The battery row remains the reference witness because it is the only surface with the
full theorem-to-code-to-artifact chain. Autonomous vehicles and healthcare remain bounded
promoted rows: A V through completed nuPlan replay artifacts, and Healthcare through
retrospective monitoring evidence. That asymmetry is intentional and is part of the scientiﬁc
discipline of the manuscript rather than a defect to be smoothed over by prose.
How to read this dissertation. Readers interested primarily in the systems problem
should begin with Chapter 1.1, then Chapter 4 for the runtime objects and theorem discipline,
and then Chapter 7 for the tiered cross-domain evidence. Readers focused on the formal
surface should pair Chapter 4 with Appendix c, Appendix j, and Chapter 10. Readers
evaluating reproducibility and promotion discipline should read the conclusion together with
Appendix r, Appendix t, and the manifest-backed tables in reports/publication/.
What this frontmatter adds. The sections that follow are brief on purpose. They do
not widen the claim surface. They provide the notation, reading conventions, and evidence
framing needed to understand the dissertation without mistaking universal architecture for
equal empirical closure.
iv

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 7 ===
Notation and Conventions
This manuscript distinguishes the true plant state from the telemetry-delivered state at every
stage of the argument. The symbol xt denotes the latent physical state, while ˆxt and zt
denote the controller-visible observation surface after parsing, delay, or fault processing. The
nominal intelligence proposes a candidate action acand
t ; ORIUS releases either the repaired
action asafe
t or an admissible fallback action.
The reliability score wt ∈ [0, 1] is a runtime trust signal, not a probability by deﬁnition.
Whenever a theorem uses wt inside a probabilistic bound, that move is justiﬁed by an explicit
theorem-local contract. The observation-consistent state set is written as Ut in the universal
chapters and as the domain-instantiated conformal tube in the reference proofs. The safe
set is written as S or, in the battery witness, through the SOC interval constraint.
Formal counts in this dissertation are computed from the active master-manuscript in-
clude tree, not from every standalone .tex ﬁle in the repository. This avoids double-counting
archived chapter surfaces and keeps the published formal inventory aligned to the canoni-
cal PDF. Appendix a and Chapter 4.2 remain the authoritative sources for the complete
notation table and the assumption register.
v

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 8 ===
On the Empirical Results
All numerical claims in this dissertation are intended to be script-derived and manifest-
backed. The manuscript therefore separates universal runtime architecture from domain
promotion status. A row appears as reference, proof-validated, proof-candidate, experimental,
or portability-only according to the evidence artifacts that exist for that row, not according
to rhetorical ambition.
The battery energy-management row is the deepest witness. Autonomous vehicles are
defended only as a bounded nuPlan replay row, and healthcare is defended only as a bounded
retrospective monitoring row. Readers should therefore interpret cross-domain tables as a
three-domain, tiered validation program rather than as a ﬂat statement of equal maturity.
Where the manuscript reports conﬁdence intervals, theorem gates, parity matrices, or
release-manifest checks, those objects govern what the surrounding prose is allowed to say.
Projected numbers, inferred parity, and unsupported cross-domain closure language are in-
tentionally excluded from the defended surface.
vi

Chapter 1
Introduction and Problem
Formulation
1.1 Introduction
Physical AI controllers—battery dispatch optimisers, autonomous-vehicle planners, robotic
manipulators—share a structural assumption so pervasive that it is rarely stated explicitly:
the observation available to the controller faithfully represents the physical state of the plant.
This dissertation investigates what happens when that assumption fails, using grid-scale
battery energy storage systems (BESS) as the ﬁrst rigorous validation surface.
1.1.1 The Hidden Assumption in Physical AI
Every feedback controller closes a loop: sense the plant state, compute an action, actuate, re-
peat. Classical control theory formalises this loop with a state vector xt ∈ X , a measurement
function h, and an observation yt = h(xt) +vt, where vt is zero-mean sensor noise. When
the noise is well-characterised—Gaussian, bounded, independent—the Kalman ﬁlter or its
nonlinear descendants recover the true state with known error bounds, and the controller
can act as if it sees the truth.
Modern cyber-physical systems (CPS) break this contract in ways that are neither Gaus-
sian nor bounded. Telemetry arrives over packet-switched networks subject to dropout, delay,
replay, and stale-sensor failure. The resulting observation ˆxt is not simply xt plus noise; it
may be structurally incorrect : the controller sees a state that the plant never occupied. No
amount of Kalman ﬁltering can correct for a reading that was dropped entirely and replaced
by an interpolated hold.
The consequence is subtle but dangerous. The controller evaluates its safety constraints
1

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 36 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 2
against ˆxt, not xt. If ˆxt satisﬁes the constraints, the controller judges the situation safe.
Meanwhile, the true state xt may already violate a physical limit. The system is observa-
tionally safe but physically unsafe —a condition we call the Observation-Action Safety Gap
(OASG).
1.1.2 Why Observed Safety Can Diﬀer from Physical Safety
Consider a battery dispatched at 50% SOC (state of charge). A telemetry packet carrying
the latest voltage and current readings is delayed by two intervals. The controller still sees
SOC = 50% and issues a large discharge. In reality, a prior discharge already pushed SOC
to 22%. The new action drives the physical SOC below the 20% safety ﬂoor. The observed
trajectory shows no violation; the true trajectory shows a hard constraint breach. Figure 1.1
illustrates this gap and the ﬁve-stage DC3S pipeline that closes it.

Figure 1.1: The OASG in practice (left): observed SOC appears above the 20% safety ﬂoor
while the true SOC has already breached it. DC3S detects reliability degradation, inﬂates the

This is not a contrived edge case. Field telemetry from US-grid EIA-930 data exhibits
dropout rates of 5–15% under normal operation and exceeding 30% during storm events. Ger-
many’s ENTSO-E feeds show comparable patterns with additional regulatory delay. Every
dropped, delayed, or stale reading widens the gap between observation and truth.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 37 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 3
1.1.3 The Observation-Action Safety Gap
We deﬁne the OASG as the divergence between a controller’s safety assessment on its ob-
served state and the actual safety status of the physical plant:
Deﬁnition 1.1 (Observation-Action Safety Gap (OASG)) . Let ˆxt be the controller’s obser-
vation and xt the true state. The OASG at time t is the event
Gt =
{
at ∈ A(ˆxt)
}
∩
{
at /∈ A(xt)
}
,
where A(x) denotes the set of actions satisfying all safety constraints at state x. When Gt
is non-empty, the controller has chosen an action that appears safe on the observation but
violates constraints on the truth.
The OASG is invisible to any evaluation protocol that checks constraints only on the
observed trajectory. Revealing it requires maintaining a parallel true-state trajectory —a
dual-trajectory architecture that is a cornerstone of the CPSBench harness developed in this
work (Chapter 3.3).
1.1.4 Why the Battery Domain Exposed the Problem First
Batteries present three properties that make the OASG both acute and measurable:
1. Hard state constraints. SOC must remain within [SOCmin, SOCmax]. There is no
elastic zone; violating the lower bound risks cell damage, and violating the upper bound
risks thermal runaway.
2. High action-to-state sensitivity. A single dispatch decision at 50 MW for a
100 MWh battery moves SOC by 50% in one hour. Even a brief observation error
translates into a large true-state deviation.
3. Network-mediated telemetry. Grid-scale batteries are monitored via SCADA, cel-
lular, or satellite links—all subject to the packet-level faults that create the OASG.
Other CPS domains (autonomous vehicles, industrial robots) share property (3) but lack
the clean measurability of (1) and (2). The battery domain is therefore the ideal ﬁrst proving
ground for a general theory of observation-aware safety.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 38 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 4
1.1.5 The ORIUS Principle
ORIUS— Observation-Reliability–aware Uncertainty-Inﬂated Safety—is the organising prin-
ciple of this dissertation. It prescribes a four-stage runtime pipeline:
1. Observation Quality Engine (OQE). Score the current telemetry reliability wt ∈
[wmin, 1] using delay, freshness, spike, and stale-sensor features.
2. Reliability-aware Uncertainty Inﬂator (RUI). Inﬂate conformal prediction inter-
vals by a factor proportional to 1/w t, widening the uncertainty set when observations
are poor.
3. Safe-Action Filter (SAF). Tighten the feasible action set so that the dispatched
action remains within SOC bounds even under worst-case realisations of the inﬂated
uncertainty.
4. DC3S Shield. Issue a safety certiﬁcate for every dispatched action, recording the
observation quality, uncertainty bounds, and the margin to the nearest constraint.
DC3S—the Data-Centric Certiﬁed Conformal Shield—is the concrete realisation of the
ORIUS principle for the battery dispatch domain.
1.1.6 Research Questions
This dissertation addresses three central questions:
RQ1. Does the Observation-Action Safety Gap exist in real-world battery dispatch? Specif-
ically, do standard controllers (deterministic LP, robust MPC, stochastic risk-aware)
produce actions that satisfy observed-state constraints while violating true-state con-
straints under realistic telemetry degradation?
RQ2. Can a certiﬁcate-aware runtime safety layer close the gap? Can a lightweight online
shield, informed by observation quality scores and conformal uncertainty, guarantee
physical safety at the cost of bounded economic intervention?
RQ3. What is the formal safety guarantee, and under what assumptions does it hold? Can
we state a theorem of the form “if telemetry faults satisfy bounds F and the calibration
set is exchangeable, then the true-state violation rate is at most αwith probability at
least 1 − δ”?

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 39 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 5
1.1.7 Contributions
The principal contributions are:
1. Formalisation of the OASG. A rigorous deﬁnition of the gap between observed-
state and true-state safety, together with a taxonomy of telemetry faults that create it
(Chapter 1.2).
2. The DC3S runtime shield. A four-stage pipeline (OQE, RUI, SAF, Shield) that
closes the OASG with a provable coverage guarantee (Chapters 3.3– 6.10).
3. CPSBench evaluation harness. A deterministic, dual-trajectory benchmark for bat-
tery dispatch under telemetry faults, enabling reproducible comparison of observation-
aware controllers (Chapter 5.3).
4. Empirical validation on US and DE grids. End-to-end experiments on EIA-930
(US) and ENTSO-E (Germany) data demonstrating zero true-state SOC violations
across all tested dropout rates, with intervention rates below 20%.
5. Extension results. Certiﬁcate half-life analysis (Chapter 6.10), graceful degradation
(Chapter 6.11), compositional ﬂeet safety (Chapter 6.12), and a CertOS runtime chap-
ter that promotes the certiﬁcate lifecycle from roadmap language into bounded systems
evidence (Chapter 6.14).
1.1.8 Contribution Boundary
This work does not claim to solve the general partial-observability problem for all CPS
domains. The safety guarantee is stated and validated for the battery dispatch domain
under the fault model of Chapter 1.2. Extension to other domains (e.g., autonomous driving,
surgical robotics) requires domain-speciﬁc instantiation of the OQE and SAF stages and is
left to future work.
We also do not replace the upstream forecast or optimisation model. DC3S is a post-
optimiser safety layer : it takes the dispatch action produced by any optimiser and certiﬁes
or corrects it before actuation. The economic optimiser remains a black box to the shield.
1.1.9 Scope and Limitations of the Safety Guarantees
The main ORIUS theorem statements are marginal coverage guarantees. They bound ag-
gregate true-state risk over the deployment distribution induced by observations, telemetry

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 40 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 6
faults, and calibration residuals. They do not claim conditional coverage for every speciﬁc
observation and fault realization.
That boundary is deliberate. Conditional coverage is not achievable in full generality
under arbitrary non-exchangeable distributions, so the thesis keeps the ﬂagship results at
the strongest level that can be defended on the active artifact surface: ﬁnite-sample marginal
coverage. Later grouped analyses, including reliability-bin and Mondrian-style evaluations,
reﬁne that guarantee for explicit subpopulations but do not upgrade the core claim into a
universal per-step worst-case statement.
1.1.10 Relationship to Prior Work
This dissertation supersedes the earlier battery-ﬁrst ORIUS monograph and its associated
frozen release bundles. The earlier package established the battery reference surface, the
original theorem ladder, and the core calibration and drift-monitoring workﬂow. Those
battery results are retained here as the deepest theorem-to-artifact witness rather than being
renumbered or discarded.
The present thesis extends that prior work in four ways. First, it isolates a domain-
agnostic repair-and-certify kernel instead of leaving the argument battery-local. Second, it
widens the empirical program into a tiered cross-domain package while preserving explicit
evidence boundaries for rows that have not yet reached parity. Third, it adds the later
necessity, lower-bound, and transfer results that clarify what the battery witness does and
does not imply beyond its home domain. Fourth, it promotes certiﬁcate lifecycle and release-
manifest governance from appendicial audit material into a ﬁrst-class runtime object through
CertOS and the claim-lock discipline.
1.1.11 Organisation of the Manuscript
The remainder of this dissertation is organised as follows:
Chapter 2.1 surveys background and related work in battery dispatch, telemetry faults,
conformal prediction, and runtime safety shields.
Chapter 1.2 formalises the system model, fault model, SOC dynamics, the observation
quality engine, and states the research questions mathematically.
Chapter 3.3 deﬁnes the observed-state safety illusion and motivates the dual-trajectory
evaluation paradigm.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 41 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 7
Chapters 4.2–6.2 develop the DC3S pipeline components, prove the coverage theorem,
and present ablation studies.
Chapters 6.10–6.14 extend the framework to certiﬁcate temporal validity, graceful degra-
dation, ﬂeet composition, and certiﬁcate-aware runtime control.
Chapter 5.3 describes the ORIUS-Bench battery track for reproducible benchmarking.
Chapter 7.3 records bounded but intentionally non-promoted evidence surfaces, including
the active probing audit and other claims that remain outside the defended core.
1.2 Problem Formulation
This chapter formalises the system model, telemetry fault model, battery dynamics, and
safety constraints that underpin the ORIUS framework. We state the research questions
mathematically and delineate the scope boundary of the dissertation.
1.2.1 ORIUS System Context
ORIUS is a renewable-aware battery dispatch platform that operates at the optimise-to-
dispatch boundary : an upstream forecast-and-optimise pipeline produces a candidate dis-
patch action a⋆
t , and a downstream safety layer either certiﬁes or corrects the action before
it reaches the physical plant. The system architecture comprises:
1. Forecast stage. CQR models predict load, solar, and wind trajectories over a conﬁg-
urable horizon, producing both point forecasts and conformal prediction intervals.
2. Optimise stage. A deterministic LP (or a pluggable alternative) maximises arbitrage
revenue subject to SOC, power, and ramp constraints evaluated on the observed state.
3. Dispatch stage (DC3S). The safety layer scores observation quality, inﬂates uncer-
tainty, tightens action bounds, and issues a safety certiﬁcate for the ﬁnal dispatched
action.
The boundary between stages 2 and 3 is the optimise-to-dispatch boundary. Everything
upstream of this boundary operates on the observed state ˆxt; the safety layer’s task is to
ensure that the dispatched action is safe on the true state xt despite the gap between the
two.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 42 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 8
1.2.2 ORIUS-MDP Formalisation
Deﬁnition 1.2 (ORIUS-MDP). An ORIUS deployment is modelled as the 11-tuple
(S, O, W, A, C, T, H, Ω, R,γ,µ0),
where S is the true-state space, O the observation space, W = [wmin, 1] the observation-
quality space, A the action space, and C ( S the closed safety set. The true transition
kernel is T : S × A → ∆( S), the observation kernel is H : S → ∆( O), the quality map is
Ω : Z k → W , the reward is R : S × A → R, γ∈ [0, 1) is the discount factor, and µ0 is the
initial-state distribution.
At runtime, the controller receives (ot,w t) where ot ∼ H (xt) and wt = Ω( zt− k+1:t) is
computed deterministically from the telemetry history. Reliability-aware policies therefore
take the form π: O × W → ∆( A), whereas quality-ignorant policies are restricted to
¯π: O → ∆( A).
Remark 1.3. The ORIUS-MDP diﬀers from a classical POMDP not because observation is
partial, but because observation quality itself is exposed as a runtime state component that
conditions repair, fallback, and certiﬁcation. Safety is always measured on the true-state
trajectory rather than on the observation trajectory.
1.2.3 The Hidden Safety Gap
We now state the core safety problem in the battery domain.
Deﬁnition 1.4 (Battery-domain OASG) . Let SOCtrue
t denote the physical state of charge
and [SOCt the value available to the controller. The battery-domain Observation-Action
Safety Gap at time t is the event
Gbat
t =
{
at ∈ A
(

1.2.4 State Objects and Telemetry Model
True State
The true state at time t is the vector
xt =

Observed State
The observed state is the output of the telemetry channel:
ˆxt = T (xt, ξt),
where T is a (possibly stochastic) telemetry operator and ξt encodes the fault realisation at
time t (dropout mask, delay value, spike indicator, staleness ﬂag).
Observation Quality Score
The Observation Quality Engine (OQE) computes a scalar reliability score from the telemetry
features:
wt = max
(
wmin, p miss,t ·pdelay,t ·pooo,t ·pspike,t
)
,
where each penalty factor p· ∈ [0, 1] reﬂects a speciﬁc fault channel:
pmiss,t : Fraction of expected readings that arrived in the current window. Full reception
yields pmiss = 1; complete dropout yields pmiss = 0.
pdelay,t : Penalty for readings whose timestamp exceeds the freshness threshold: pdelay =
exp(−λd ·¯τt), where ¯τt is the mean delay in the window and λd is a conﬁgurable decay
rate.
pooo,t : Fraction of readings arriving in chronological order. Out-of-order arrivals indicate
network jitter or replay.
pspike,t : Penalty for readings ﬂagged as transient spikes: pspike = 1 − nspike/n total.
The ﬂoor wmin > 0 ensures that the uncertainty inﬂator always produces a ﬁnite interval;
the default value is wmin = 0. 05.

1.2.5 Fault Model
We deﬁne the fault model as a tuple F = (ρ,¯τ,πs,πstale,σd):
• ρ∈ [0, 1]: dropout rate (probability that a packet is missing at any given step).
• ¯τ≥ 0: mean packet delay in multiples of the decision interval ∆t.
• πs ∈ [0, 1]: probability that a reading is a transient spike.
• πstale ∈ [0, 1]: probability that a sensor output is frozen (stale).
• σd > 0: standard deviation of the covariate drift between calibration and deployment
distributions.
The CPSBench harness (Chapter 5.3) instantiates each fault channel independently and
provides composite scenarios (e.g., drift_combo) that activate all channels simultaneously.

• ηc,ηd ∈ (0, 1] are the charge and discharge round-trip eﬃciencies,
• Emax is the battery energy capacity (MWh), and
• ∆t is the decision interval (hours).

The upstream optimiser maximises expected arbitrage revenue over a rolling horizon H:

1.2.8 The Calibration Gap Under Degradation
Conformal prediction intervals are calibrated on a held-out calibration set collected under
nominal telemetry conditions. When deployment telemetry degrades, the residual distribu-
tion shifts:
1. Dropout introduces a positive bias in the imputed SOC (stale holds tend to overesti-
mate SOC during discharge and underestimate during charge).
2. Delay increases the variance of the residual because the controller acts on outdated
information.
3. Covariate drift invalidates the exchangeability assumption that underpins the confor-
mal guarantee.
The RUI stage compensates by scaling the conformal half-width by 1/w t:
qeﬀ ,t = qα
wt
, (1.4)
where qα is the nominal conformal quantile. As wt decreases, the eﬀective half-width grows,
widening the uncertainty set and tightening the safe-action ﬁlter.

1.2.9 Baseline Policies
We compare DC3S against three baseline dispatch policies:
Deterministic LP. Solves (1.3) directly on ˆxt with no uncertainty handling.
Robust MPC. Maintains a ﬁxed uncertainty tube [ˆxt −ϵ,ˆxt +ϵ] with hand-tuned margin ϵ.
The tube does not adapt to observation quality.
Stochastic risk-aware. Samples N scenarios from historical forecast errors and enforces
CVaR constraints. The scenario distribution is not conditioned on telemetry quality.
All baselines operate on the observed state and do not maintain a true-state trajectory.
Their safety performance is therefore evaluated ex post by the CPSBench dual-trajectory
harness.

1.2.11 Scope Boundary
The formal results in this dissertation are stated for the following scope:
• Domain. Grid-scale lithium-ion battery energy storage (100 MWh / 50 MW reference
plant).
• Decision granularity. Hourly dispatch ( ∆t = 1 h).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 47 ===
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION AND PROBLEM FORMULATION 13
• Fault model. The ﬁve-channel model of Section 1.2.5, with dropout rates up to
ρ= 0. 40.
• Forecast horizon. Up to 48 hours (the maximum horizon evaluated in the 48-hour

• Plant model. Linear SOC dynamics ( 1.1) with constant eﬃciencies. Degradation

Results outside this scope (sub-hourly dispatch, non-lithium chemistries, vehicle-to-grid
applications) require re-calibration and are deferred to future work.
1.2.12 Dataset Scope
All experiments use two public data sources:
1. EIA-930 (US). Hourly demand, net generation, and interchange data for US bal-
ancing authorities. We use the CISO (California ISO) and MISO (Midcontinent ISO)
regions as primary test beds, with PJM, NYISO, and ERCOT for transfer-learning
studies.
2. ENTSO-E (Germany). Hourly load, solar, and wind data from the German trans-
mission system operators. Used for cross-geography generalisation experiments.
Synthetic telemetry faults are injected by the CPSBench harness (Chapter 5.3) on top
of the real data, preserving the statistical properties of the underlying load and renewable
signals.
1.2.13 Chapter Summary
This chapter has deﬁned the ORIUS system architecture, the ﬁve-channel telemetry fault
model, the SOC dynamics, the dispatch objective, and the observation quality engine. The
research questions have been formalised in terms of the OASG event and the true-state
violation rate. The scope boundary and dataset scope delimit the claims made in subsequent
chapters. Chapter 3.3 uses these deﬁnitions to demonstrate the observed-state safety illusion
and motivate the dual-trajectory evaluation paradigm.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 48 ===
Chapter 2
Related Work
2.1 Background and Related Work
This chapter surveys the literatures that converge on the problem addressed in this disserta-
tion: battery dispatch optimisation, telemetry faults in cyber-physical systems, uncertainty
quantiﬁcation via conformal methods, and runtime safety assurance. For each strand, we
identify the speciﬁc gap that the ORIUS framework ﬁlls.
2.1.1 Battery Dispatch and Renewable-Aware Control
Grid-scale battery dispatch is the problem of choosing charge and discharge actions to max-
imise economic value (arbitrage, ancillary services) while respecting SOC, power, and ramp
constraints. The literature falls into three broad families.
Deterministic Dispatch
Linear programming (LP) formulations treat load, solar, and wind forecasts as point esti-
mates and solve a single deterministic optimisation at each decision epoch [ 21]. The approach
is fast and widely deployed, but it ignores forecast uncertainty entirely: the action is opti-
mal only if the forecast is exact. Under telemetry degradation, the deterministic LP has no
mechanism to detect that its inputs are stale or incorrect, and it will dispatch against an
observation that may not reﬂect the plant state.
Robust and Tube-Based Methods
Robust MPC constructs a tube of feasible trajectories around the nominal forecast, ensuring
that the dispatched action is safe for all realisations within a bounded uncertainty set [ 15].
14

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 49 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 15
Tube-based approaches are conservative by construction: they sacriﬁce economic perfor-
mance to guarantee constraint satisfaction. However, the uncertainty set is typically derived
from forecast error statistics and does not account for observation-quality degradation. If
the tube is calibrated on clean telemetry, it will be too narrow when telemetry is degraded—
precisely the regime where safety margins matter most.
Stochastic and Risk-Aware Methods
Stochastic programming and chance-constrained formulations model uncertainty through
scenario trees or distributional assumptions [ 19]. Risk-aware variants incorporate CVaR
or other coherent risk measures to penalise tail outcomes. These methods acknowledge
uncertainty but assume it is aleatoric (inherent randomness in load and renewables) rather
than epistemic (degraded or missing observations). None of the existing risk-aware dispatch
frameworks condition their uncertainty sets on the quality of the incoming telemetry.
2.1.2 The Measurement Blind Spot
Across all three families, a common blind spot emerges: the optimiser trusts its inputs.
Whether the formulation is deterministic, robust, or stochastic, it assumes that the obser-
vation ˆxt arriving at the decision epoch is a faithful—if noisy—representation of the true
state xt. This assumption is codiﬁed in the measurement equation ˆxt = xt +vt with well-
characterised noise vt.
In practice, telemetry faults violate this equation structurally. A dropped packet does
not add noise; it removes information. A stale sensor does not perturb the reading; it freezes
it. A replay attack substitutes a plausible but outdated value. These faults create epistemic
degradation that standard noise models cannot capture.
The measurement blind spot is the root cause of the Observation-Action Safety Gap
deﬁned in Chapter 1.1: the optimiser dispatches against a state that may not exist, and
no downstream constraint check can detect the error because it, too, operates on the same
degraded observation.
2.1.3 Telemetry Faults in Cyber-Physical Systems
The CPS literature documents telemetry faults extensively in the context of power sys-
tems [ 18], industrial SCADA [ 17], and autonomous vehicles [ 20]. Fault taxonomies typically
distinguish:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 50 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 16
Packet dropout: The reading is absent; the system either holds the last known value or
interpolates.
Delay and jitter: The reading arrives late; the decision is made on stale information.
Spike: A transient hardware glitch produces an out-of-range value.
Stale sensor: The sensor output is constant across multiple intervals, indicating a frozen
transducer or a repeated value.
Covariate drift: The joint distribution of load, solar, and wind shifts between the calibra-
tion period and deployment, invalidating learned residual distributions.
Existing fault-tolerant control methods address each fault type in isolation—median ﬁl-
ters for spikes, watchdog timers for stale sensors, Kalman-based imputation for dropouts.
None synthesise the fault signals into a uniﬁed reliability score that feeds back into the
safety layer. The Observation Quality Engine (OQE) introduced in this dissertation ﬁlls this
gap.
2.1.4 Uncertainty Quantiﬁcation and Conformal Methods
Conformal Quantile Regression (CQR)
Conformal prediction provides distribution-free prediction intervals with ﬁnite-sample cov-
erage guarantees [ 13]. Conformal Quantile Regression (CQR) combines quantile regression
with conformal calibration to produce adaptive intervals whose width varies with the input
features [ 11].
CQR is attractive for battery dispatch because it makes no distributional assumptions
about the forecast residuals and inherits the exchangeability guarantee of conformal inference.
The coverage guarantee states:
Pr
[
Yn+1 ∈ ˆC(Xn+1)
]
≥ 1 − α,
where ˆC is the conformal prediction set and αis the target miscoverage level.
Adaptive Conformal Inference
Adaptive conformal inference (ACI) adjusts the conformal threshold online to maintain cov-
erage under distribution shift [ 12]. ACI is essential for battery dispatch, where load and
renewable patterns exhibit diurnal, weekly, and seasonal non-stationarity.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 51 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 17
Key Limitation
The standard CQR/ACI guarantee assumes that calibration residuals are exchangeable with
test residuals. Under telemetry degradation, this assumption may be violated: a dropout
burst changes the residual distribution. The ORIUS framework addresses this limitation by
inﬂating the conformal interval width in proportion to the observation quality penalty 1/w t,
restoring coverage even when the exchangeability assumption is weakened.
2.1.5 Runtime Safety, Shields, and Assurance
Runtime veriﬁcation and shielding have a rich history in formal methods and reactive syn-
thesis [ 16]. A shield is a runtime monitor that intercepts unsafe actions before they reach
the plant, replacing them with the nearest safe alternative.
How DC3S Extends the State of the Art
Existing shields operate on the observed state: they check whether the proposed action
satisﬁes constraints given the current observation. DC3S introduces three innovations:
1. Observation-quality conditioning. The shield’s constraint tightening is a function
of the OQE score wt, not a ﬁxed margin. When telemetry is clean ( wt ≈ 1), the shield
is nearly transparent; when telemetry degrades, the shield tightens progressively.
2. Conformal uncertainty integration. The tightened constraints are derived from
CQR intervals inﬂated by 1/w t, inheriting the conformal coverage guarantee.
3. Certiﬁed actions. Every dispatched action carries a machine-readable certiﬁcate
recording the observation quality, uncertainty bounds, and safety margin, enabling
downstream audit and temporal-validity analysis (Chapter 6.10).
2.1.6 Control Barrier Functions
Control barrier functions (CBFs) provide a Lyapunov-like certiﬁcate for forward invariance
of a safe set [ 14]. A CBF h : X → R deﬁnes the safe set as C = {x :h(x) ≥ 0} and enforces
the condition ˙h(x,u ) +γh(x) ≥ 0 for some class- K function γ.
CBFs assume full state feedback : the barrier condition is evaluated at the true state xt.
When only a degraded observation ˆxt is available, the CBF condition may be satisﬁed on
the observation while violated on the truth—precisely the OASG. DC3S can be viewed as a
data-centric CBF that inﬂates the barrier margin based on observation quality, closing the
gap that classical CBFs leave open.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 52 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 18
2.1.7 Information-Theoretic Roots
The observation quality score wt can be interpreted through an information-theoretic lens.
When telemetry is perfect, the mutual information I(Xt; ˆXt) = H(Xt) (full information).
Each fault type reducesI(Xt; ˆXt): dropout removes entire samples, delay introduces conditional-
entropy growth, and stale sensors reduce the eﬀective channel capacity to zero.
The OQE multiplicative formula
wt = max
(
wmin, p miss ·pdelay ·pooo ·pspike
)
is a heuristic proxy for the normalised mutual information I(Xt; ˆXt)/H (Xt). While not a
tight bound, the multiplicative structure ensures that any single fault channel can indepen-
dently drive reliability toward the ﬂoor wmin, matching the information-theoretic intuition
that a single broken channel dominates the overall quality.
2.1.8 Evaluation Protocols and the Benchmark Gap
Standard battery dispatch benchmarks evaluate controllers on economic metrics (revenue,
regret) and observed constraint satisfaction. None maintain a parallel truth trajectory, so
they cannot detect the OASG by construction. The CPSBench harness introduced in this
dissertation (Chapter 5.3) addresses this gap by running every controller on both the ob-
served and true state machines simultaneously, reporting True-State Violation Rate (TSVR)
alongside Observed-State Violation Rate (OSVR).
2.1.9 Responsibility-Sensitive Safety and Conformal Control for
A V
The A V domain instantiation of the DC3S shield is grounded in the Responsibility-Sensitive
Safety (RSS) framework. Shalev-Shwartz et al. [ 65] deﬁne a longitudinal safe-following dis-
tancegsafe(ve,v ℓ) that guarantees collision avoidance if the ego vehicle brakes at its minimum
comfortable deceleration while the lead brakes at its maximum. The ORIUS A V derivation
(Section 4.5.10) shows that the DC3S reliability-conditioned inﬂation qα/w t applied to the
gap uncertainty set controls the residual collision-gap violation probability to α(1 − wt) per
step.
Recent work connects conformal prediction to safe autonomous planning:
• Lindemann et al. [ 87] construct conformal prediction regions for multi-agent trajecto-
ries and incorporate them into robust planning constraints, achieving PAC-style safety

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 53 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 19
guarantees without distributional assumptions on other agents.
• Dixit et al. [ 88] extend conformal motion planning to adaptive settings, adjusting pre-
diction set widths online to maintain coverage under distribution shift—conceptually
close to the ACI-based reliability adaptation in DC3S.
• Angelopoulos et al. [ 89] formalise conformal PID control , a feedback loop that tunes
conformal set widths via proportional-integral-derivative logic to hit target coverage
rates.
DC3S diﬀers from these approaches in two ways: (i) the observation quality score wt
modulates not only the prediction set width but the entire constraint tightening pipeline,
enabling fault-type-speciﬁc degradation response; (ii) every dispatched action is accompa-
nied by a CertOS certiﬁcate that records the exact reliability, inﬂation, and safety margin,
enabling post-hoc temporal-validity audit that the above methods do not address.

Table 2.1 summarises the positioning of DC3S relative to the prior art.

2.1.11 Chapter Summary
The literatures on battery dispatch, CPS telemetry faults, conformal prediction, and runtime
shielding each address a piece of the observation-aware safety problem, but none provide a

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 54 ===
CHAPTER 2. RELATED WORK 20
complete solution. Dispatch optimisers ignore observation quality. Fault-tolerant control
handles faults individually without feeding a uniﬁed score into the safety layer. Conformal
methods provide coverage guarantees but do not condition on telemetry reliability. Runtime
shields check constraints on the observed state, missing the OASG entirely. The ORIUS
framework and its DC3S realisation unify these strands into a single pipeline with end-to-
end safety guarantees from observation to actuation.

Chapter 3
System Context and Safety
Distinctions
3.1 ORIUS System Context
Grid-scale battery energy storage systems (BESS) sit at the intersection of three economic
forces: variable renewable generation that must be absorbed, load proﬁles that demand
dispatchable power on sub-hourly timescales, and wholesale markets that price energy at
volatile clearing rates. This chapter positions the ORIUS framework within that operational
context, traces the closed decision loop from forecast to measurement, and identiﬁes the
Optimize–Dispatch boundary as the location where observation quality most directly threat-
ens safety.
3.1.1 Why Grid-Scale Batteries Matter
The penetration of wind and solar generation has transformed balancing-area operations.
In 2024 the ERCOT interconnection recorded negative real-time prices for 18% of daytime
hours, while evening ramp rates exceeded 12 GW h − 1 on 37 days. Grid-scale batteries absorb
these mismatches: they charge when marginal cost is low, discharge when it is high, and
provide ancillary services (frequency regulation, spinning reserve) that stabilise the grid
during transients.
Three properties distinguish BESS from conventional peakers:
1. Bidirectional power ﬂow. A 100 MW / 400 MWh installation can charge or dis-
charge at full rate within seconds, making it a controllable load and a controllable
generator.
21

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 56 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 22
2. State-of-charge coupling. Every dispatch decision at time t constrains the feasible
set at time t + 1 through the SOC dynamics. The controller cannot treat time steps
independently.
3. Degradation risk. Deep cycling, high C-rates, and over-voltage operation accelerate
cell ageing. Safety envelopes are therefore both hard (BMS trip thresholds) and soft
(warranty-preserving SOC margins).
These properties mean that dispatch quality depends not only on forecast accuracy but
on the ﬁdelity of the real-time observation that informs each control step.
3.1.2 ORIUS as a Closed Decision Loop
The ORIUS system implements a ﬁve-stage closed loop:
Forecast  
ˆyt+1:t+H
− → Optimize  
a⋆
t
− → Dispatch  
aexec
t
− → Measure  
˜zt
− → Monitor  
Ct
− → (next cycle)
Forecast. A model family F produces point forecasts and prediction intervals for load, wind,
solar, and price over a rolling horizon H (typically 24–48 h at hourly resolution).
Optimize. A dispatch optimiser receives the forecast distribution and the current SOC
estimate, then solves a constrained programme to produce a candidate action a⋆
t =
(charget, discharget).
Dispatch. The candidate action is checked against safety constraints (SOC bounds, ramp
limits, BMS thresholds). If the action is feasible it is forwarded to the battery man-
agement system; otherwise it is repaired to the nearest safe action asafe
t .
Measure. Operational telemetry ˜zt returns from the plant: actual SOC, terminal voltage,
cell temperatures, grid-side power meter readings. This measurement may be delayed,
dropped, corrupted, or stale.
Monitor. A certiﬁcate Ct records the action taken, the observation quality score, the interval
width, and whether the step passed all safety invariants. The certiﬁcate feeds the next
forecast cycle and is archived for audit.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 57 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 23
3.1.3 The Optimize–Dispatch Boundary as the True Risk Location
Not all stages of the loop carry equal risk. A forecast error at t + 12 h can be corrected
by re-optimisation at t + 1 ; a measurement delay at the Measure stage is visible to the
Monitor and can trigger a conservative hold. The highest-risk transition is the Optimize
→ Dispatch boundary, for three reasons:
1. Commitment. Once the BMS receives a power setpoint, the battery executes it
within milliseconds. The action is irreversible on the control-cycle timescale.
2. Observation dependence. The optimiser’s candidate a⋆
t is conditioned on the most
recent observation ˜zt− 1. If that observation is stale or corrupted, the candidate may
lie outside the true feasible set without any visible warning.
3. Constraint coupling. An unsafe action at time t shifts the SOC into a region that
may render all future actions infeasible until a recovery period elapses (the “SOC
trap”).
The DC3S adapter (Chapter 4.1) is positioned precisely at this boundary: it intercepts a⋆
t ,
inﬂates the uncertainty set to account for observation degradation, and projects the action
into the tightened safe set before it reaches the BMS.
3.1.4 Operational Telemetry Pathways
Telemetry in a grid-scale BESS traverses multiple communication layers before reaching the
dispatch controller:
1. Cell-level sensors (voltage, temperature, current) report to the Battery Management
System (BMS) over CAN bus at 1–10 Hz.
2. BMS aggregation computes pack-level SOC, state-of-health (SOH), and thermal
limits, then publishes to the site SCADA at 1 Hz.
3. SCADA gateway relays readings to the Energy Management System (EMS) via Mod-
bus/TCP or IEC 61850. This link is typically on-site LAN with sub-second latency.
4. Cloud telemetry streams from the EMS to the dispatch controller (ORIUS) over
cellular, ﬁbre, or satellite uplink. This link is the primary source of packet loss, delay
jitter, and reordering.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 58 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 24
5. Grid-side meters (revenue meters, PMUs) provide independent power and frequency
measurements at 4 s or sub-second resolution.
Each hop introduces a distinct failure mode: CAN bus saturation at high C-rates, SCADA
gateway reboot during ﬁrmware updates, cellular dropout during storms, and satellite latency
during congestion. The telemetry fault taxonomy used in CPSBench (Chapter 5.2) mirrors
these real failure modes.
3.1.5 Why Measurement Quality Enters the Control Problem
Classical dispatch formulations assume that the controller observes the true state zt at each
step. In practice the controller observes a degraded version ˜zt that may diﬀer from zt in
timing, magnitude, or completeness. The gap between zt and ˜zt has three consequences for
safety:
1. SOC estimation error. If the reported SOC is stale by ∆t steps, the true SOC has
drifted by up to Pmax ·∆t/E cap in normalised units, where Pmax is the rated power and
Ecap the nameplate energy.
2. Forecast conditioning error. The most recent load and renewables readings con-
dition the next forecast window. Corrupted readings propagate into biased intervals,
undermining conformal coverage guarantees.
3. Constraint tightening mismatch. A robust optimiser that tightens constraints
based on assumed interval width may under-tighten if the true observation quality is
worse than assumed, or over-tighten if quality is better, sacriﬁcing economic value.
The central thesis of this dissertation is that measurement quality must be explicitly
scored and fed back into the uncertainty set at each control step. The observation quality
estimator (OQE, Chapter 4.1) and the reliability-adaptive conformal certiﬁcate (RAC-Cert)
implement this feedback loop.
3.1.6 Chapter Summary
This chapter established the ORIUS system as a ﬁve-stage closed decision loop—Forecast,
Optimize, Dispatch, Measure, Monitor—operating over grid-scale battery storage. The
Optimize–Dispatch boundary was identiﬁed as the highest-risk transition because actions
are irreversible, observation-dependent, and coupled through SOC dynamics. Telemetry tra-
verses multiple communication layers, each introducing distinct degradation modes. Because

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 59 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 25
measurement quality directly aﬀects SOC estimation, forecast conditioning, and constraint
tightening, it must enter the control formulation as a ﬁrst-class variable. The remainder of
Part II develops the mathematical machinery to accomplish this.
3.2 Data, Telemetry, and Dataset Scope
Every empirical claim in this dissertation rests on two locked dataset surfaces: the Germany
OPSD archive and the United States EIA-930 feed. This chapter documents the signals,
their provenance, the telemetry pathways through which they enter the ORIUS loop, and
the fault modes that make degraded observation an ordinary operating condition rather than
an edge case.
3.2.1 Germany and United States Dataset Surface
The ORIUS evaluation spans two geographically and regulatorily distinct power systems.
Germany (OPSD). The Open Power System Data platform aggregates hourly generation,
load, and day-ahead price series from ENTSO-E and the German TSOs. The locked dataset
covers 2018-10-07 to 2020-09-30 (17 377 hourly rows), with four primary targets: load_mw,
wind_mw, solar_mw, and price_eur_mwh. Weather covariates (temperature, irradiance, wind

feature matrix contains 94 engineered columns after calendar encoding and lag construction.

3.2.2 Forecast Targets and Operational Channels
Each dataset surface exposes three operational forecast targets:
load_mw Net system load (total demand minus behind-the-meter solar), measured at the
transmission level. This is the primary signal for arbitrage scheduling.
wind_mw Aggregate onshore and oﬀshore wind generation. Wind exhibits high variance and
heavy tails, particularly during storm fronts, making calibration challenging.
solar_mw Aggregate photovoltaic generation. Solar follows a deterministic diurnal envelope
modulated by cloud cover; residual uncertainty is sharply bimodal (clear vs. overcast).
A fourth target, price_eur_mwh (DE only), drives the economic objective in the dispatch
optimiser. For US regions, a simpliﬁed tariﬀ schedule or locational marginal price proxy is
used.
All targets are sampled at hourly resolution and aligned to UTC. The forecast horizon
is H = 48 h with a stride of 1 h, yielding a rolling-origin evaluation with 168 test windows
per week.
3.2.3 Observed Telemetry Versus Truth Trajectory
A central design choice in ORIUS is the separation of truth state and observed state . At
every control step t:
• The truth trajectory {zt}records what actually happened at the plant: the true SOC,
the true net load, the true renewable generation. This trajectory is never available
to the controller in real time; it is logged by the CPSBench harness for post-hoc
evaluation.
• The observed trajectory {˜zt} is what the controller actually sees after telemetry
faults have been applied. It may contain dropped readings (NaN), delayed values,
sensor spikes, stale repetitions, or drifted baselines.
The gap ∥zt − ˜zt∥ is the observation error. All safety metrics (TSVR, severity) are com-
puted on the truth trajectory, while the controller’s decisions are computed on the observed
trajectory. This dual-trajectory architecture is the foundation of the CPSBench evaluation
protocol (Chapter 5.2).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 61 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 27
3.2.4 Missingness, Corruption, and Signal Degradation
Telemetry faults fall into six categories, each modelled by a corresponding CPSBench fault
injector:
Table 3.2: Telemetry fault taxonomy and operational causes.
Fault type Mechanism Field cause
Dropout Packet loss (NaN) Cellular dropout, gateway reboot
Delay jitter Arrival-time shift Network congestion, buﬀering
Spike Multiplicative outlier Sensor transient, ADC overﬂow
Stale sensor Frozen reading Firmware hang, watchdog failure
Drift Slow baseline shift Sensor ageing, calibration decay
Reorder Out-of-sequence packets UDP path diversity, queue inversion
These fault types are not mutually exclusive. The drift_combo scenario in CPSBench
applies all six simultaneously at conﬁgurable severity, representing a worst-case but opera-
tionally plausible ﬁeld condition.
3.2.5 Why Telemetry Faults Are Ordinary, Not Edge Cases
Field data from commercial BESS installations suggests that telemetry degradation is the
norm rather than the exception:
• Cellular uplinks in rural wind-farm sites experience 2–8% packet loss during normal
operation, rising to 15–25% during severe weather.
• SCADA gateway reboots occur 1–3 times per month, each causing a 30–90 s telemetry
blackout.
• Sensor drift in current transducers accumulates at 0.1–0.5% of full-scale per year,
enough to bias SOC estimates by 2–5 MWh on a 100 MW/400 MWh system within
six months.
• Firmware updates on BMS controllers occasionally freeze individual cell-group readings
for minutes until the watchdog timer resets the reporting thread.
A safety framework that assumes clean telemetry is therefore unsafe by construction.
The ORIUS evaluation treats faulted telemetry as the default operating condition and clean
telemetry as a special case (the nominal scenario with zero fault injection).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 62 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 28
3.2.6 Artifact Contracts and Proﬁle Lock
Each dataset surface is frozen at a speciﬁc commit hash and validated by a reproducibility
contract:
1. Row-count invariant. The ingestion pipeline asserts that the row count matches the
locked value in reports/publication/dataset_cards.csv.
2. Column schema. The feature matrix schema (column names, dtypes, nullable ﬂags)
is checked against a frozen JSON schema at pipeline entry.
3. Target coverage. All three forecast targets must have 100% non-null coverage in the
locked period. Any gap causes the pipeline to abort with a traceable error.
4. Hash veriﬁcation. The SHA-256 hash of the serialised Parquet ﬁle is compared to
the locked value before any model training or evaluation proceeds.
These contracts ensure that no drift in upstream data sources can silently invalidate the
empirical results.
3.2.7 Scope Boundary
The dataset scope of this dissertation is intentionally bounded:
• Included: hourly bulk-power system signals (load, wind, solar, price) for four regions
across two continents.
• Excluded: sub-hourly (5-min, 1-min) data, distribution-level signals, behind-the-
meter solar, demand response, and frequency regulation signals.
• Rationale: the hourly resolution matches the natural arbitrage cycle of 2–4 h duration
storage and aligns with the temporal granularity of conformal calibration windows.
Extending the framework to sub-hourly resolution is discussed in Chapter 5.3 as part of
the benchmark roadmap.
3.2.8 Chapter Summary
This chapter documented the two dataset surfaces (Germany OPSD and US EIA-930) that
underpin every empirical result, the three forecast targets ( load_mw, wind_mw, solar_mw),

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 63 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 29
and the six telemetry fault types that degrade observations between the plant and the con-
troller. The dual-trajectory architecture—truth state versus observed state—was introduced
as the foundation for safety evaluation. Artifact contracts and hash-locked proﬁles ensure
reproducibility. Telemetry faults are treated as ordinary operating conditions, not edge cases,
motivating the observation-aware machinery developed in the following chapters.
3.3 The Observed-State Safety Illusion
A battery controller that checks constraints only against its own observations will, by con-
struction, never detect a violation it cannot see. This chapter formalises the observed-state
safety illusion , shows how it arises in the ORIUS dispatch loop, explains why neither bet-
ter forecasting nor tighter optimisation can resolve it, and introduces the dual-trajectory
evaluation paradigm that makes the illusion visible.
3.3.1 From Partial Observability to Safety Illusion
Partial observability is a well-studied concept in control theory and reinforcement learning.
A partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) acknowledges that the agent sees
a noisy or incomplete projection of the true state and plans accordingly, typically via belief-
state updates. The safety illusion arises when the agent does not plan accordingly —when the
controller treats its observation as ground truth and evaluates constraints against it without
accounting for observation quality.
In a POMDP with a well-calibrated belief, the agent maintains a distribution over true
states and can compute the worst-case constraint satisfaction probability. In practice, battery
dispatch controllers do not maintain belief distributions; they receive a point estimate ˆxt from
the SCADA system and optimise against it. The gap between the POMDP ideal and the
deployed reality is the source of the illusion.
3.3.2 Why the Illusion Arises in the ORIUS Loop
The Optimise-to-Dispatch Boundary
As described in Chapter 1.2, the ORIUS loop has a clear boundary between the optimiser and
the dispatcher. The optimiser solves ( 1.3) on the observed state and produces a candidate
action a⋆
t . The dispatcher (in the baseline case) forwards this action to the plant without
modiﬁcation.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 64 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 30
Both the optimiser and the baseline dispatcher operate on ˆxt. The safety constraints
SOCmin ≤ [SOCt + ηc[at]+ − [at]−/ηd
Emax
·∆t ≤ SOCmax
are checked on the observed SOC. If [SOCt over- or under-estimates the true SOC, the
constraint check is meaningless with respect to physical safety.
Why Good Forecasting Does Not Solve It
A natural objection is: “Improve the forecast, and the observation error disappears. ” This
is incorrect for a fundamental reason. The forecast model predicts future load, solar, and
wind given current observations . If the current observation is itself degraded—a stale SOC
reading, a dropped load packet—the forecast inherits the error. A perfect forecast model
with imperfect inputs produces imperfect outputs.
More precisely, the forecast model learns E[Yt+h | ˆXt], not E[Yt+h |Xt]. When ˆXt ̸=Xt,
the conditional expectation is evaluated at the wrong conditioning point. The resulting
action is optimal for a state the plant does not occupy.
3.3.3 Formal Statement of the Illusion
Deﬁnition 3.1 (Observed-State Safety Illusion) . Let ˆxt be the observed state, xt the true
state, and at the dispatched action. The observed-state safety illusion at time t is the
conjunction of three conditions:
1. Observed safety: at ∈ A(ˆxt)—the action satisﬁes all constraints when evaluated on
the observation.
2. True-state violation: at /∈ A (xt)—the action violates at least one constraint when
evaluated on the truth.
3. Undetectability: The controller has no mechanism to detect condition (2) from its
available information ˆxt alone.
Condition (3) distinguishes the illusion from a detectable fault. If the controller could
infer that ˆxt is unreliable (e.g., via an OQE score), it could compensate. The illusion is
silent: the controller proceeds with full conﬁdence in a physically unsafe action.
Theorem 3.2 (Existence of the illusion under dropout) . Under the fault model of Sec-
tion 1.2.5 with dropout rate ρ >0 and last-value hold imputation, there exists a time step t

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 65 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 31
and a deterministic LP dispatch action at such that the observed-state safety illusion (Deﬁ-
nition 3.1) holds with positive probability.
Proof sketch. Consider a discharge phase where SOCtrue
t = SOC min +ϵfor small ϵ >0.
If the most recent k SOC readings were dropped and the last-value hold reports [SOCt =
SOCmin +ϵ+k ·δ(the SOC at the time of the last successful reading, before k discharge
steps each consumingδ), then the LP observes a comfortable margin and dispatches a further
dischargeat < 0. The true SOC after this action is SOCtrue
t+1 = SOC min +ϵ− |at| ·∆t/ (Emax ·
ηd) < SOCmin for |at|exceeding ϵ·Emax ·ηd/ ∆t. The action satisﬁes observed constraints,
violates true constraints, and the LP cannot detect the violation—all three conditions hold.
3.3.4 Battery-Domain Realisation Under Telemetry Faults
A Simple Battery Narrative
Consider a 100 MWh / 50 MW battery operating over 168 hours (one week) under the
dropout_only scenario at ρ= 0. 20.
1. Hour 0–24. Telemetry is clean. The deterministic LP dispatches charge during
solar surplus and discharge during evening peak. Observed and true SOC tracks are
indistinguishable.
2. Hour 25. A dropout burst begins: ﬁve consecutive SOC readings are missing. The
last-value hold freezes [SOC at 55%.
3. Hour 26–28. The LP continues discharging against the frozen observation. True
SOC falls from 55% to 38% to 24%. The observed SOC remains at 55%.
4. Hour 29. The LP dispatches a 40 MW discharge, believing SOC is 55%. True SOC
drops to 16%, breaching the 20% ﬂoor. Observed SOC still shows 55%. The illusion
is complete.
5. Hour 30. Telemetry recovers. The LP observes SOC at 16% and abruptly curtails
dispatch, but the damage—an SOC excursion below the safety ﬂoor—has already oc-
curred.
This narrative is not hypothetical. The CPSBench harness reproduces it deterministically
(seed 42, dropout_only, ρ= 0. 20), and the true-state violation is recorded in the TSVR
metric.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 66 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 32
3.3.5 Why Standard Evaluation Misses the Problem
Standard battery dispatch evaluation protocols report:
• Revenue or regret (economic performance).
• Observed SOC constraint violations (observed safety).
• Forecast accuracy (MAPE, RMSE, CRPS).
None of these metrics involve the true SOC trajectory. A controller that achieves zero
observed violations can simultaneously have a high true-state violation rate. The evaluation
is complicit in the illusion: it checks safety on the same degraded observation that caused
the unsafe action.
Proposition 3.3 (Insuﬃciency of observed-state evaluation) . For any TSVR > 0, there
exists a controller-scenario pair with OSVR = 0 and the given TSVR. Observed-state evalu-
ation is therefore insuﬃcient to certify physical safety.
Proof. Take any controller πand any fault scenario with dropout rate ρ >0. Since π
evaluates constraints on ˆxt and the telemetry operator preserves observed-state feasibility by
construction (the hold value is always within bounds), OSVR = 0 . By Theorem 3.2, there
exists a step where at violates true-state constraints, so TSVR> 0.
3.3.6 CPSBench and Dual-Trajectory Evaluation
To break the illusion, we introduce the dual-trajectory evaluation paradigm implemented in
the CPSBench harness. At each step t, the harness maintains two parallel state machines:
1. Observed trajectory. The SOC evolution computed from the action at and the
observed state ˆxt, using the dynamics ( 1.1) with [SOCt as the initial condition.
2. True trajectory. The SOC evolution computed from the same action at but with
the true state SOCtrue
t as the initial condition.
The true trajectory is never visible to the controller; it is maintained solely for evaluation.
This separation is critical: the controller cannot “cheat” by observing the truth, and the
evaluator cannot be fooled by the observation.
Deﬁnition 3.4 (True-State Violation Rate (TSVR)) .

The dual-trajectory architecture enables unambiguous attribution: every true-state vio-
lation is traceable to a speciﬁc action, a speciﬁc observation error, and a speciﬁc gap between
ˆxt and xt.
3.3.7 Why the Illusion Demands a Runtime Shield
The illusion cannot be resolved at the optimiser level for two reasons:
1. Information asymmetry. The optimiser has access only to ˆxt. Without a reliability
signal, it cannot distinguish clean telemetry from degraded telemetry. Any ﬁxed margin
it applies will be either too conservative (wasting revenue when telemetry is clean) or
too aggressive (missing violations when telemetry degrades).
2. Separation of concerns. The optimiser’s objective is economic; the safety layer’s
objective is physical. Mixing the two creates Pareto conﬂicts that are better resolved
by a dedicated post-optimiser shield. The shield can adapt its tightening in real time
based on the OQE score, something a batch optimiser solved minutes ago cannot do.
The DC3S shield resolves the illusion by introducing an observation-quality-aware con-
straint tightening that operates at the dispatch boundary. The shield does not replace the
optimiser; it corrects the optimiser’s blind spot.
Theorem 3.6 (DC3S Feasibility Guarantee) . Let α∈ (0, 1) be the conformal miscoverage
level, n the calibration-set size, and ¯w = T − 1 ∑T
t=1wt the episode-mean reliability score.
Under Assumptions A1, A2, A3, and A5, the DC3S shield with conformal quantile ˆqα inﬂated
by 1/w t satisﬁes, for any ﬁnite episode of T steps:
TSVR ≤ α(1 − ¯w) + εn,
whereεn =O(1/ √n) is the ﬁnite-sample calibration correction term. In particular:
1. At ¯w = 1 (perfect telemetry) and large n: TSVR → 0.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 68 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 34
2. At ¯w < 1 (degraded telemetry): the bound is proportional to the mean reliability gap
(1 − ¯w).
3. As n → ∞ : εn → 0 and the bound converges to the episode-mean reliability term
exactly.
This result is the single-episode analogue of Theorem T3 (ORIUS Core Bound, Chapter 4.5),
which proves E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T in expectation over episodes.
The formal version of this theorem, with precise assumptions and a complete proof, is
developed in subsequent chapters.
3.3.8 Chapter Summary
The observed-state safety illusion is the mechanism by which the OASG operates in practice:
a controller evaluates constraints on a degraded observation, concludes the system is safe,
and dispatches an action that is physically unsafe. The illusion is invisible to any evalua-
tion protocol that checks constraints on the observed trajectory only. The dual-trajectory
architecture of CPSBench makes the illusion visible, and the DC3S runtime shield makes
it preventable. The remainder of the dissertation develops the shield, proves its guarantees,
and validates them empirically across US and German grid data under the full fault model
of Chapter 1.2.
3.4 Battery Dynamics and Dispatch Problem
This chapter formalises the state-space model of a grid-scale battery energy storage sys-
tem (BESS), derives the dispatch optimisation programme, and shows how observation un-
certainty enlarges the feasible set in ways that classical deterministic formulations cannot
capture.
3.4.1 Battery State Variables
The BESS plant is characterised by the following state and parameter variables at each
hourly time step t:
For the ORIUS reference plant the nameplate parameters are Emax = 10 000 MWh,
Pmax = 200 MW, η+ = η− = 0. 95, and Rmax = Pmax (no additional ramp constraint be-
yond rated power). Soft SOC margins are enforced at [SOCmin, SOCmax] = [0. 05, 0. 95]; hard
BMS trips occur at [0. 0, 1. 0].

3.4.6 Uncertainty and Feasible Set Under Degraded Observation
When the observation ˜Et is degraded, the initial-condition constraint Et = ˜Et in ( 3.8) be-
comes uncertain. Let ˆEt denote the controller’s best estimate and δt =Et − ˆEt the estimation
error. The true SOC after action is:
Etrue
t+1 = ( ˆEt +δt) +η+P +
t ∆t − P −
t
η− ∆t. (3.9)
For the SOC bounds to hold with probability at least 1 −α, the constraint must be tightened
by the conformal prediction half-width qt:
Emin +qt ≤ Eest
t+1 ≤ Emax − qt, (3.10)
where qt is the reliability-inﬂated interval half-width from RAC-Cert (Chapter 4.1). This
tightening shrinks the feasible set and reduces the economic value of the dispatch, but guar-
antees safety against observation error.
3.4.7 Calibration Gap and Uncertainty Mismatch
Even with conformal intervals, a calibration gap arises when marginal coverage diﬀers from
conditional coverage across reliability groups. The locked CQR group-coverage results ( reports/publication/cqr_group_coverage.csv)
reveal that wind intervals achieve only 39–49% coverage in the low and high reliability groups
despite 90% nominal marginal coverage. Solar intervals are better calibrated (82–90%) but
still exhibit under-coverage at the high-variability group.
This calibration gap means that the tightened bounds in ( 3.10) are insuﬃciently tight
during periods of poor observation quality, motivating the adaptive inﬂation law in DC3S
(Section 3.4.5 cross-references Chapter 4.1).
3.4.8 Baseline Policies
The ORIUS evaluation includes eight dispatch controllers spanning four design philosophies:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 72 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 38
1. Deterministic LP (deterministic_lp): solves ( 3.8) on point forecasts with no in-
terval awareness.
2. Robust ﬁxed-interval (robust_fixed_interval): worst-case robust LP using ﬁxed
(non-adaptive) prediction intervals.
3. CVaR interval (cvar_interval): CVaR-optimised scenario dispatch over quantile
forecasts.
4. ACI conformal (aci_conformal): adaptive conformal inference baseline with online
width adjustment.
5. Scenario robust (scenario_robust): scenario-based robust optimisation over sam-
pled forecast paths.
6. Scenario MPC (scenario_mpc): receding-horizon model predictive control with sce-
nario tree.
7. DC3S wrapped (dc3s_wrapped): full DC3S shield with linear reliability inﬂation.
8. DC3S FTIT (dc3s_ftit): DC3S with fault-tolerant invariant tube extension.
Results across these controllers are reported in Chapter 4.1 and the benchmark track
(Chapter 5.2).
3.4.9 Scope Boundary
The dispatch model in this dissertation is a single-asset, energy-only formulation:
• Included: energy arbitrage, SOC management, degradation penalty, safety constraints.
• Excluded: ancillary services (frequency regulation, spinning reserve), multi-asset port-
folio dispatch, network constraints, and market-clearing co-optimisation.
The single-asset scope is suﬃcient to demonstrate the observation-quality feedback mech-
anism; extension to ﬂeet-level dispatch is discussed in Chapter 5.3.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 73 ===
CHAPTER 3. SYSTEM CONTEXT AND SAFETY DISTINCTIONS 39
3.4.10 Chapter Summary
This chapter formalised the battery state-space model, the SOC dynamics equation ( 3.1),
the power/ramp/SOC constraint set, and the deterministic dispatch LP ( 3.8). Three action
types—candidate, safe, and executed—were distinguished to precisely locate the safety in-
tervention point. The eﬀect of degraded observation on the feasible set was derived, showing
that the conformal half-width must tighten the SOC bounds and that calibration gaps in
wind and solar intervals motivate adaptive inﬂation. Eight baseline controllers spanning
deterministic, robust, conformal, and shielded design philosophies were enumerated.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 74 ===
Chapter 4
ORIUS Framework and Theoretical
Foundations
4.1 DC3S: The Universal Safety Adapter
The Detect–Calibrate–Constrain–Shield–Certify Safety System (DC3S) is the runtime adapter
that sits at the Optimize–Dispatch boundary (Chapter 3.1) and translates degraded obser-
vations into safety-guaranteed actions. This chapter speciﬁes the runtime objects, derives
each stage of the pipeline, and narrates a complete control step from telemetry ingestion to
certiﬁcate issuance.
In the codebase, the domain-agnostic version of that runtime now lives in src/orius/
universal_theory/. The battery derivation below is the reference instantiation of that
universal degraded-observation kernel, not a hidden claim that every domain already inherits
battery-level proof depth.
4.1.1 Why a Runtime Shield Is Needed
Chapter 5.1 showed that CQR intervals under-cover conditionally: wind coverage drops to
39–49% in the low-reliability group, and load coverage falls 8–20 percentage points below
nominal. A dispatch controller that trusts these intervals without adjustment will issue ac-
tions that violate SOC constraints on the true (unobserved) trajectory at rates far exceeding
the design budget α.
A runtime shield is needed because:
1. Forecast calibration is a batch property; telemetry degradation is a streaming property.
No static calibration fold can anticipate the speciﬁc fault pattern at step t.
40

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 75 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 41
2. The observation quality at step t is known to the controller (via packet-loss counters,
staleness timers, and drift detectors) but is not used by standard conformal methods.
3. The SOC dynamics are coupled: an unsafe action at t may render all future actions
infeasible. The shield must prevent the ﬁrst violation, not merely reduce its frequency.

4.1.3 Detect: Telemetry Reliability Scoring (OQE)
The Observation Quality Estimator (OQE) computes the reliability score wt ∈ [0, 1] from
three sub-scores:
wt =wfresh
t ·wrange
t ·wconsist
t , (4.1)
where:
wfresh
t Freshness score: exp(−λf ·st), decaying exponentially with the stale count st.
wrange
t Range plausibility: 1 if all channels are within physical bounds, decayed proportion-
ally to the number of out-of-range channels.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 76 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 42
wconsist
t Temporal consistency: based on the Mahalanobis distance of the current reading
from a short rolling window, penalising spikes and discontinuities.
The OQE computation is stateless given the rolling buﬀer and runs in < 0. 03 ms median
latency (Table 4.3, reports/publication/dc3s_latency_summary.csv).
4.1.4 Calibrate: Reliability-Adaptive Interval Inﬂation
The base CQR interval Ct(α) is inﬂated to compensate for the conditional under-coverage
revealed in Chapter 5.1. The RAC-Cert (Reliability-Adaptive Conformal Certiﬁcate) formula
is:
C RAC
t (α) = Ct(α) ·
[
1 +κr (1 − wt) +κddt +κsst
]
(4.2)
where:
κr Reliability sensitivity: scales the inﬂation with the complement of the reliability score.
Default κr = 0. 5.
κd Drift sensitivity: additive inﬂation when the Page–Hinkley detector ﬁres. Default κd =
0. 3.
κs Staleness sensitivity: linear inﬂation per stale step. Default κs = 0. 05.
The multiplicative form ensures that under nominal conditions ( wt = 1 , dt = 0 , st = 0 )
the inﬂated interval collapses to the base interval, imposing zero additional conservatism.
Inﬂation diagnostics. The locked evidence in reports/publication/dc3s_main_table.csv
reports the mean and P95 of the RAC inﬂation factor across all scenarios and seeds. Under
the drift_combo scenario the mean inﬂation is approximately 1. 02, rising to 1. 05 at the P95
level, conﬁrming that the adaptive layer adds modest width only when observation quality
genuinely degrades.

4.1.7 FTIT State Tube
The Fault-Tolerant Invariant Tube (FTIT) provides a secondary safety layer that does not
depend on interval calibration. FTIT propagates worst-case SOC bounds forward over a
k-step horizon:
Et+k = ˆEt +δmax +kη+P +
max ∆t,
Et+k = ˆEt − δmax − kP −
max/η− ∆t,
(4.5)
where δmax is the maximum SOC estimation error consistent with the current reliability
score. Any action that would cause either bound to exit [Emin,E max] within the k-step tube
is rejected.
The FTIT acts as a hard backstop: even if the RAC-Cert inﬂation is insuﬃcient (e.g.
due to a novel fault type not captured by the sensitivity parameters), the tube prevents
multi-step SOC excursions.

4.1.10 Complete Runtime Narrative
A single DC3S control step proceeds as follows:
1. Receive observationot from the telemetry gateway. If the packet is missing (dropout),
carry forward the last valid reading and increment st.
2. Detect. Computewt =wfresh
t ·wrange
t ·wconsist
t via OQE. Run the Page–Hinkley test on
the residual stream to set dt.
3. Calibrate. Inﬂate the base CQR interval: C RAC
t (α) = Ct(α) ·[1 +κr(1 −wt) +κddt +
κsst].
4. Constrain. Construct the safe-action set At by tightening SOC bounds with the
inﬂated half-width. Verify the FTIT tube; if the tube is violated, further restrict At.
5. Shield. If a⋆
t /∈ A t, project: asafe
t = arg mina∈A t ∥a − a⋆
t ∥2. Otherwise asafe
t =a⋆
t .
6. Certify. Emit the certiﬁcate Ct recording all runtime variables, the intervention deci-
sion, and the guarantee-check outcome. Forward asafe
t to the BMS.
4.1.11 Chapter Summary
This chapter speciﬁed the DC3S battery adapter: a ﬁve-stage runtime shield (Detect, Cali-
brate, Constrain, Shield, Certify) that intercepts dispatch actions at the Optimize–Dispatch
boundary. The OQE reliability scorer ( 4.1) quantiﬁes observation quality; the RAC-Cert
formula ( 4.2) inﬂates intervals adaptively; the safe-action set ( 4.3) tightens SOC bounds ac-
cordingly; and the projection repair ( 4.4) moves unsafe actions to the nearest feasible point.
The FTIT tube provides a calibration-free safety backstop. The full pipeline completes in
under 0.2 ms at P99, and step-level certiﬁcates create a complete audit trail. Empirical
validation is presented in Chapter 5.2.
4.2 Assumptions, Notation, and Proof Discipline
This chapter establishes the formal scaﬀolding for Part III. It ﬁxes notation that is stable
across all theorems, catalogues the eight standing assumptions, deﬁnes the proof dependency

tree now contains additional theorem-carrying merged chapters, appendix restatements, and
front-loaded formal deﬁnitions. The audited surface therefore contains 83 formal items
