CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 47
4.2.2 Assumption IDs (A1–A13)
Each assumption is assigned a permanent identiﬁer and stated in both domain-general and
battery-speciﬁc form.
Assumption 1 (A1: Bounded model error) . The one-step prediction error ∥xt+1 −f (xt,a t)∥
is bounded by a known constant ϵmodel. Battery: |xt+1 − (xt +ηPt ∆t)| ≤ϵmodel.
Assumption 2 (A2: Bounded telemetry error) . The observation error ∥ˆxt −xt∥ is bounded
by a reliability-dependent function: ∥ˆxt − xt∥ ≤g(wt), where g is monotone decreasing.
Assumption 3 (A3: Feasible safe repair) . For every state xt satisfying ϕ(xt) = 1 , there
exists at least one action at ∈ A t such that ϕ(xt+1) = 1 .
Assumption 4 (A4: Known dynamics) . The transition function f (xt,a t) is known to the
controller up to the model error bounded in A1. Battery: the SOC transition xt+1 =
xt +ηPt ∆t is known with eﬃciency ηidentiﬁed oﬄine.
Assumption 5 (A5: Monotone bounded uncertainty inﬂation) . The inﬂation function
ι(wt) = qt/ (wt +ϵ) is monotone decreasing in wt and bounded: ι(wt) ≤ ¯ιfor all wt ∈ [0, 1].
Assumption 6 (A6: Bounded detector lag) . The OQE reliability score wt reﬂects each
telemetry fault within a channel-speciﬁc lag budget. If a fault begins at step tf , then the
runtime detector ﬁres or the reliability score crosses the alert threshold within at most τmax(f )
steps, with
τmax(dropout) = 1, τmax(stale) = 3, τmax(delay) = 2, τmax(out-of-order) = 10, τmax(spike) = 1, τmax(drift) = 48.
These constants are mirrored by the runtime diagnostics in src/orius/dc3s/quality.py
and are intended as the defended detector contract for the locked replay surfaces.
Assumption 7 (A7: Causal certiﬁcate update) . The certiﬁcate Ct depends only on infor-
mation available at or before step t: Ct =h(ˆx1:t,a 1:t− 1).
Assumption 8 (A8: Admissible fallback policy) . There exists a fallback policy πfb such
that, starting from a safe state, the runtime can either safely hold the current state in place
or apply a boundary-aware recovery action that moves the state inward. If neither case
can be certiﬁed, the runtime fails closed instead of claiming a fallback guarantee. Battery:
zero dispatch is the defended hold action on the interior, and safe landing is the defended
boundary-recovery action near the SOC limits.

Reading note. The ﬁrst ten columns record standing assumptions. The ﬁnal column records

4.2.4 Marginal Versus Conditional Coverage
A critical distinction throughout Part III:
Marginal coverage. Pr[xt ∈ ˆCt ] ≥ 1 −αaveraged over the randomness of calibration and
test points. This is what CQR provides.
Conditional coverage. Pr[xt ∈ ˆCt |ˆxt ] ≥ 1 − αfor every observed value. Conditional
coverage is strictly stronger and is not guaranteed by CQR without additional distri-
butional assumptions.
The ORIUS theorems are stated under marginal coverage unless a stronger conditioning
statement is written explicitly into the theorem assumptions. The reference proof ladder
uses monotone inﬂation to preserve the base coverage surface. The transfer theorem is kept
at the one-step level for exactly this reason: it does not silently upgrade marginal coverage
into a stronger episode-level conditioning claim.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 83 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 49
4.2.5 Theorem-Local Calibration Contracts
Two theorem-local contracts are used repeatedly in the tightened theory surface:
1. Risk-envelope contract. A chapter may introduce a predictable sequence rt satis-
fying P[xt+1 /∈ S | H t] ≤ rt. The reference-row envelope rt ≤ α(1 − wt) is one such
contract, but it is not claimed to follow from vanilla conformal exchangeability alone.
2. Boundary-indistinguishability contract. A chapter may model degraded observa-
tion through pairs of latent hypotheses whose observation laws remain close in total
variation or another explicit channel metric. This is the extra ingredient used in the
stylized lower-bound theorem (T10); it is not inherited automatically from the deﬁni-
tion of wt.
4.2.6 One-Step Versus Multi-Step Claims
The theorems in Chapters 4.3– 4.5 operate on a per-step basis: each step’s safety is analysed
conditionally on the current state. The core bound (Chapter 4.5) aggregates per-step risks
into an episode-level expectation via linearity of expectation.
Multi-step claims (certiﬁcate horizon, graceful degradation) require additional assump-
tions about temporal dependence; these are stated explicitly in Chapter 4.7.
4.2.7 Main Result Arc and Traceability IDs
The manuscript presents the theory as four ﬂagship theorem roles plus supporting deﬁnitions,
propositions, and corollaries, while retaining the permanent T1–T11 identiﬁers for auditabil-
ity, appendix mapping, and code traceability. This distinction is deliberate: reviewers should
read a compact argument, while the repository keeps the ﬁner-grained theorem IDs needed
for tests, generated registers, and proof-to-code evidence.
1. Observation-aware safety gap. T1 establishes that degraded observation can make
observed-state safety disagree with true-state safety.
2. Observation necessity. T4 establishes that ﬁxed-margin, quality-ignorant release
cannot guarantee true-state safety on the defended ambiguity witness class.
3. ORIUS covered release preservation. T2, with T3 as a supporting risk-envelope
corollary, shows that under coverage and a stated risk-envelope contract, the runtime
shield preserves one-step safety and yields a quantitative episode-level bound.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 84 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 50
4. Temporal certiﬁcate and fallback discipline. T5 is retained as the certiﬁcate-
horizon deﬁnition, while T6–T8 provide expiration, fallback, and graceful-degradation
support for operational release. These are operational support surfaces, not additional
main-paper novelty claims.
5. Typed transfer and covered ambiguity optimality. T11 states the typed cross-
domain transfer obligations; the supporting T10/T11 observation-ambiguity corollary
gives the Bayes lower bound for observation-only mandatory release and the ORIUS
α-covered upper bound. T9 and T10 remain scoped extension surfaces unless their
additional promotion gates are discharged.

4.2.8 Theorem-Writing Discipline
Every theorem in this dissertation follows a ﬁxed template:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 85 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 51
1. Statement: formal claim with quantiﬁers.
2. Assumptions used : list of A-IDs.
3. Proof : complete derivation.
4. Artifact link : pointer to the empirical evidence that instantiates the theorem on the
reference row.
5. Scope disclaimer : “What this theorem does not prove. ”
4.2.9 Common Theorem Failure Modes
To inoculate against reviewer objections, we catalogue failure modes that a careless proof
might exhibit:
1. Vacuous truth. If the assumption set is never satisﬁed, the theorem is true but useless.
We verify non-vacuousness by exhibiting the reference-row instantiation.
2. Circular dependence. Theorem X assumes the conclusion of Theorem Y , and vice
versa. The dependency matrix (Table 4.4) prevents this.
3. Hidden conditional. A marginal claim is stated as if it were conditional. We use the
marginal/conditional distinction explicitly.
4. Inﬁnite-sample limit. A result that holds only asymptotically is presented as ﬁnite-
sample. All ORIUS bounds are stated for ﬁnite T .

include tree contains 26 theorem environments and 83 formal items overall. The defended
unique theorem program comprises the two precursor results S1–S2, the reference ladder
T1–T8, and the universal extension T9–T11. Separate from that full inventory, the older
hard gate still audits the precursor plus reference-ladder theorem rows and their Appendix C
restatements for source, code, artifact, and mapping integrity.

4.2.11 Chapter Summary
This chapter provides the stable notational, assumptive, and methodological foundation for
every proof in Part III. The canonical assumption register (A1–A13) is individually justiﬁed
in both domain-general and witness-domain terms, the proof dependency matrix prevents
circular reasoning, and the theorem-writing discipline ensures that every claim is bounded
by explicit scope disclaimers. The integrated theorem surface of 83 formal items—spanning
26 theorems, 5 lemmas, 9 propositions, 12 corollaries, 15 deﬁnitions, and 16 assumptions—is
the formal depth this dissertation contributes to the CPS safety literature.
4.3 Theorem 1: OASG Existence
This chapter proves the Observed-safe / Actually-unsafe State Gap (OASG) existence the-
orem in the battery domain. The theorem establishes that telemetry degradation creates
episodes where an action is safe according to the observed state but unsafe according to the
true state—the foundational phenomenon that motivates the entire ORIUS safety layer.
4.3.1 Domain-Agnostic Statement
We ﬁrst state the OASG existence result for an arbitrary cyber-physical system; the battery
domain provides the concrete instantiation that follows.
Deﬁnition 4.1 (Abstract CPS) . A cyber-physical system is a tuple (X, U,f, S, ˆx) where
X ⊆ Rn is the state space, U ⊆ Rm is the action space, f : X × U → X is the (possibly
stochastic) dynamics, S ⊆ X is the safe set with non-empty boundary ∂S ̸= ∅, and ˆxt =
xt +ξt is the observed state under telemetry error ξt with ∥ξt∥ ≤g(wt) (Assumption A2).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 87 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 53
Theorem 4.2 (OASG Existence — General CPS) . Let (X, U,f, S, ˆx) be a CPS satisfying
Assumptions A1 (bounded dynamics error), A2 (bounded telemetry error with g(wt) > 0
under degraded quality), and A4 (known dynamics). Let π: X → U be any quality-ignorant
controller, i.e. one whose action depends only on ˆxt and not on wt.
If the controller drives the state within distance δ >0 of ∂S with positive frequency, and
the telemetry error satisﬁes Pr[∥ξt∥>δ]> 0, then there exists a time t∗ such that
ϕobs(π(ˆxt∗)) = 1 and ϕtrue(π(ˆxt∗)) = 0,
whereϕobs and ϕtrue are the observed-safe and truly-safe predicates respectively.
The proof constructs the witness t∗ by intersecting the boundary-proximity set (from op-
timal control) with the observation-error tail (from degraded telemetry). We now instantiate
this in the battery domain.
4.3.2 Battery-Domain Instantiation
Consider a battery energy storage system with:
• True state xt = SOCt ∈ [0,E max] (MWh).
• Observed state ˆxt =xt +ξt, where ξt is the telemetry error induced by packet dropout,
delay, drift, or stale sensors.
• Actionat = (P chg
t ,P dis
t ) ∈ R2
+ subject to power and ramp constraints.
• Dynamics: xt+1 =xt + (ηcP chg
t −η− 1
d P dis
t ) ∆t +ϵt, where |ϵt| ≤ϵmodel (Assumption A1).
4.3.3 Observed and True Safety Predicates
Deﬁnition 4.3 (Observed safety). An action at is observed-safe if the predicted next state
under the observed SOC satisﬁes the constraints:
ϕobs(at) = 1
[
SOCmin ≤ ˆxt + ∆(at) ≤ SOCmax
]
,
where ∆(at) = (ηcP chg
t − η− 1
d P dis
t ) ∆t.
Deﬁnition 4.4 (True safety). An action at is truly safe if the actual next state satisﬁes the
constraints:
ϕtrue(at) = 1
[
SOCmin ≤ xt + ∆(at) +ϵt ≤ SOCmax
]
.
The OASG phenomenon occurs when ϕobs(at) = 1 but ϕtrue(at) = 0 .

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 88 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 54
4.3.4 Assumptions Used
The OASG existence theorem requires:
• A1 (bounded model error): ensures the dynamics are predictable up to ϵmodel.
• A2 (bounded telemetry error): ensures the observation gap |ξt| ≤g(wt) is ﬁnite but
non-zero under degraded telemetry.
• A4 (known dynamics): ensures the controller can compute ∆(at) correctly.
4.3.5 Auxiliary Lemmas
Lemma 4.5 (Observation gap under dropout) . Under packet dropout with rate d >0, the
observation error satisﬁes
E
[
|ξt|
]
≥ d ·σdrift ·
√
∆t,
whereσdrift is the standard deviation of the one-step state drift.
Proof. When a packet is dropped (probability d), the observer uses the last received state,
which drifts by at least σdrift
√
∆t in expectation under the sub-Gaussian drift model. The
result follows by the law of total expectation.
Lemma 4.6 (Boundary proximity under arbitrage) . An optimal arbitrage controller drives
SOC within δbnd =Pmax ∆t of at least one constraint bound in at least a fraction fbnd > 0 of
time steps.
Proof. Optimal arbitrage charges to near-full when prices are low and discharges to near-
empty when prices are high. The fraction fbnd depends on the price distribution and is
strictly positive whenever price variance is non-zero.
4.3.6 Main Theorem: OASG Existence
Theorem 4.7 (OASG Existence). Under Assumptions A1, A2, A4, A11, and A12, consider
an optimal arbitrage controller whose safe-state trajectory reaches the boundary-proximity set
Tbnd with positive frequency. For any telemetry degradation model with nonzero admissible
error tail, there exists an admissible degraded-observation realization and a time step t∗ such
that
ϕobs(at∗) = 1 and ϕtrue(at∗) = 0.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 89 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 55
That is, the controller selects an action that appears safe in the observed state but violates
a constraint in the true state. Equivalently, OASG is not a numerical artifact of a particu-
lar experiment: once boundary reachability and a nonzero observation-error tail coexist, an
observed-safe / true-unsafe witness can be constructed.
Proof. By Lemma 4.6 and Assumption A11, there exists a set Tbnd ⊂ {1,...,T }with |Tbnd| ≥
fbndT such that for t ∈ T bnd, the true SOC is within δbnd of a constraint bound.
By Lemma 4.5, Assumption A2, and the controller-fault independence condition in
Assumption A12, the degraded-observation model has an admissible realization for which
|ξt|> δbnd at some t ∈ T bnd. This is the witness realization used in the existence theorem;
the locked empirical artifacts then measure how often such events occur under the evaluation
distributions.
At such a step t∗, the observed state ˆxt∗ = xt∗ +ξt∗ is displaced from the true state
by more than the distance to the constraint boundary. The controller, which sees only ˆxt∗,
selects an action that is feasible under the observed state but pushes the true state across
the bound:
xt∗ + ∆(at∗)< SOCmin while ˆxt∗ + ∆(at∗) ≥ SOCmin.
Hence ϕobs(at∗) = 1 and ϕtrue(at∗) = 0 .
4.3.7 Interpretive Table
Table 4.7 maps the theorem’s formal elements to battery observables.
Table 4.7: Mapping OASG Existence theorem to battery domain.
Formal element Battery meaning
xt True SOC (MWh)
ˆxt BMS-reported SOC after telemetry degradation
ξt Telemetry error from dropout / drift / delay
ϕobs = 1,ϕtrue = 0 Controller thinks SOC is in bounds; it is not
δbnd Distance from SOC to nearest rated limit

4.3.9 Corollaries
Corollary 4.8 (OASG rate lower bound). Under the conditions of Theorem 4.7, the expected
OASG rate satisﬁes E[TSVR] ≥ d ·fbnd ·Pr[|ξt|>δbnd]> 0.
Corollary 4.9 (OASG severity) . The expected severity of an OASG violation is at least
E[|ξt| −δbnd | |ξt|>δbnd]> 0.
4.3.10 What This Theorem Does Not Prove
1. It does not quantify the exact TSVR for a speciﬁc controller; that requires empirical
measurement (Chapter 6.1).
2. It does not claim that every step has a violation, only that at least one exists.
3. It does not address whether the violation can be prevented—that is the subject of
Theorem 4.14.
4.3.11 Chapter Summary
The OASG Existence theorem is the ﬁrst rung of the theorem ladder. It proves that the gap
between observed and true safety is not a theoretical curiosity but an inevitable consequence

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 91 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 57
of telemetry degradation in optimising controllers. The battery domain provides a concrete
witness: a TSVR of 3.9% for the deterministic LP under drift_combo at 20% dropout.
4.4 Theorem 2: Safety Preservation Under the Shield
This chapter proves the Safety Preservation theorem: given the OASG phenomenon estab-
lished in Chapter 4.3, the DC3S shield restores true-state safety by constructing a tightened
action set that accounts for the gap between observed and true state. The theorem state-
ments below use energy management notation; the analogous result for any domain satisfying
the shared adapter contract follows by substituting domain-speciﬁc state and constraint def-
initions (see Chapter 7.1 for all six instantiations).
4.4.1 Domain-Agnostic Statement
We ﬁrst state the Safety Preservation result for an arbitrary CPS satisfying the ORIUS
adapter contract.
Theorem 4.10 (Safety Preservation — General CPS) . Let (X, U,f, S, ˆx) be a CPS satisfying
Assumptions A1–A5 and A7. Deﬁne the tightened safe action set
At =
{
a ∈ U : f (ˆxt,a ) ⊕ B(mt) ⊆ S
}
,
where mt =qt/ (wt +ϵ) is the reliability-inﬂated conformal margin and B(mt) is the ball of
radiusmt.
If the current true state lies inside the inﬂated observation-consistent set and the DC3S
shield releases an action at ∈ A t, then the resulting one-step successor remains safe:
xt ∈Ut and at ∈ A t =⇒ f (xt,a t) ∈ S.
The theorem is therefore a conditional one-step shield theorem. Any probabilistic statement
about how often xt ∈Ut occurs is supplied separately by the relevant coverage or risk-envelope
hypothesis.
The battery domain provides the scalar instantiation below, where S = [SOCmin, SOCmax]
and At reduces to a power-clipping interval.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 92 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 58
4.4.2 Battery-Domain Instantiation: Tightened Safe Action Set
Deﬁnition 4.11 (Tightened safe-action set) . Given observed state ˆxt, reliability score wt,
and conformal quantile qt, the tightened safe-action set is
At =
{
a ∈ U : SOCmin +mt ≤ ˆxt + ∆(a) ≤ SOCmax − mt
}
,
where the margin mt = qt/ (wt +ϵ) is the reliability-inﬂated conformal half-width and U is
the power/ramp feasible set.
The margin mt grows as reliability wt decreases, shrinking At and forcing the controller
toward more conservative actions.
4.4.3 Assumptions Used
The Safety Preservation theorem requires:
• A1 (bounded model error): one-step prediction error |ϵt| ≤ϵmodel.
• A2 (bounded telemetry error): |ˆxt − xt| ≤g(wt).
• A3 (feasible safe repair): At ̸= ∅ wheneverϕ(xt) = 1 .
• A4 (known dynamics): the controller can compute ∆(a).
• A5 (monotone bounded inﬂation): mt is monotone in wt and bounded above.
• A7 (causal certiﬁcate): no future information is used.
4.4.4 Auxiliary Propositions
Proposition 4.12 (Inﬂated set contains the current state) . Under Assumptions A2 and
A5, if the current state belongs to the reliability-adjusted observation-consistent set Ut =
[ˆxt − mt, ˆxt +mt], then |xt − ˆxt| ≤mt.
Proof. This is the deﬁning containment property of the inﬂated observation set. Membership
xt ∈Ut means exactly that the latent state lies in the interval centered at ˆxt with half-width
mt, which is equivalent to |xt − ˆxt| ≤mt. Assumptions A2 and A5 justify the construction
of mt and its monotone inﬂation under degraded reliability; the proposition uses only the
resulting set-membership statement.
Proposition 4.13 (Tightened feasibility implies true feasibility) . If at ∈ A t and |xt − ˆxt| ≤
mt, then ϕtrue(at) = 1 .

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 93 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 59
Proof. By Deﬁnition 4.11, ˆxt + ∆(at) ∈ [SOCmin +mt, SOCmax − mt]. Since |xt − ˆxt| ≤mt,
xt + ∆(at) ∈ [ˆxt − mt + ∆(at), ˆxt +mt + ∆(at)] ⊆ [SOCmin, SOCmax].
Adding the model error |ϵt| ≤ϵmodel from A1 and using the fact that the tightened set is
built with an explicit model-error buﬀer yields the claimed true-state feasibility.
4.4.5 Main Theorem: Safety Preservation
Theorem 4.14 (Safety Preservation) . Under Assumptions A1–A5 and A7, if the DC3S
shield selects at ∈ A t and the current true state satisﬁes xt ∈Ut, then the released action is
one-step safe:
xt ∈Ut and at ∈ A t =⇒ ϕtrue(at) = 1.
No episode-level expectation claim is made in this theorem; those are derived later from an
explicit risk-envelope contract.
Proof. Fix a step t and assume the theorem hypotheses. Because xt ∈Ut, Proposition 4.12
gives the deterministic bound |xt − ˆxt| ≤mt. The released action lies in At, so Deﬁnition 4.11
ensures that the observed-state forward prediction remains at least mt away from the SOC
boundaries after the one-step update. Proposition 4.13 then converts that tightened observed-
state feasibility statement into true-state safety.
The proof is therefore entirely conditional and one-step: once the latent state is covered
by the inﬂated observation-consistent set and the repair map returns an element of the
tightened safe set, the released action preserves safety. Probabilistic statements about the
event xt ∈ Ut are handled in later chapters through theorem-local coverage or risk-budget
assumptions.
4.4.6 Scalar SOC Derivation
In the battery domain, the safety preservation condition reduces to a scalar inequality. Let
st = SOCt and ˆst = SOCobs

st+1 =st +ηcP chg

t ∆t − η− 1
d P dis

≥ SOCmin +mt − mt − ϵmodel = SOCmin − ϵmodel. (4.6)
An analogous upper bound holds for SOC max. The ϵmodel residual is absorbed by the model-
error buﬀer included in the margin computation.

4.4.9 Corollaries
Corollary 4.15 (Zero-violation regime) . If the coverage event xt ∈ Ut holds at every step
of a ﬁnite episode and the margin includes the required model-error buﬀer, then the shielded
trajectory incurs no one-step true-state violations on that episode.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 95 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 61
Corollary 4.16 (Intervention-safety tradeoﬀ) . The intervention rate IR = |{t : aLP
t ̸=
asafe
t }|/T is bounded below by the fraction of steps where the LP action lies outside At, and
bounded above by the fraction of steps where wt <w alert.
4.4.10 What This Theorem Does Not Prove
1. It does not prove that At is the largest possible safe set; it may be overly conservative.
2. It does not prove that the intervention rate is minimal; a tighter conformal method
could reduce IR without sacriﬁcing safety.
3. It does not by itself prove how frequently the event xt ∈Ut occurs. That step requires
a separate coverage or risk-budget argument, and later chapters keep that dependence
explicit.
4.4.11 Chapter Summary
The Safety Preservation theorem is the second rung of the theorem ladder. It proves a nar-
rower but more durable statement than the earlier draft: once the latent state is covered by
the inﬂated observation-consistent set, any action released from the tightened safe set pre-
serves one-step true-state safety. The battery-domain scalar derivation shows the mechanism
concretely: the reliability-inﬂated margin absorbs the observation gap, and the projection
ensures constraint satisfaction. Episode-level probability and expectation statements are de-
ferred to the later risk-envelope chapter, where the additional calibration assumptions are
written explicitly.
4.4.12 Three-Domain Per-Step Coverage Veriﬁcation
The per-step risk lemma (Lemma 4.18) states that Pr[Zt = 1] ≤ α(1 − wt) for each step t,
given marginal conformal coverage at level 1−αand the monotone inﬂation assumption (A5).
The current defended paper uses this lemma only across the active Battery, A V, and Health-
care claim surface. Earlier exploratory rows are not promoted in this proof check.
Battery energy storage. The battery witness row uses the locked CPSBench replay sur-
face and its state-of-charge conformal certiﬁcate. The calibration residuals and runtime trace
are drawn from the same replay program under the documented fault schedule, and the OQE
scorewt is monotone decreasing in telemetry degradation. Under the stated exchangeability
and boundedness assumptions, Lemma 4.18 applies to the battery row, and the Core Bound
E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T is the paper’s deepest theorem-facing witness.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 96 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 62
Autonomous vehicles. The A V row is now bounded to the completed nuPlan replay sur-
face. Its defended state targets are ego speed and relative gap under the TTC plus predictive-
entry barrier contract. The balanced bounded split keeps train, validation, calibration, and
test surfaces non-empty, and source identity is retained in the scenario identiﬁers so multiple
nuPlan archives cannot collide. The per-step risk lemma applies only within this replay
denominator; the relative-gap watch state and spike weakness remain explicit limitations
rather than evidence of full autonomy closure.
Healthcare monitoring. The healthcare row is bounded to retrospective monitoring and
alert-release semantics over the MIMIC-backed evidence lane. ICU vital-sign observations
are physiologically bounded, and the stale/spike fault schedule is evaluated through the
same reliability-weighted certiﬁcate grammar as the other active rows. Lemma 4.18 applies
only to this retrospective monitoring surface. The row does not claim prospective clinical
deployment, treatment recommendation, or regulatory approval.
Summary. The defended per-step coverage argument is a three-domain argument: Battery
supplies the theorem-grade witness, A V supplies a bounded nuPlan replay transfer check,
and Healthcare supplies a bounded retrospective monitoring check. No additional domain is
used as a promoted empirical premise in the current public manuscript.
4.5 The ORIUS Risk-Envelope Bound
This chapter derives the central quantitative result of the ORIUS framework: a conservative
upper bound on the expected number of true-state violations over an episode of length T
once a domain supplies an explicit per-step residual-risk budget. The bound links the mis-
coverage level α, the average observation reliability ¯w, and the horizon T into a single enve-
lope, but it does so only under a theorem-local calibration contract. The runtime module
src/orius/dc3s/coverage_theorem.py exposes helper functions that compute and inspect
this bound across the locked runtime rows; the strength of the manuscript claim remains
governed by the tier matrix in Chapter 7.
4.5.1 Domain-Agnostic Statement
The Core Bound is stated entirely in domain-agnostic quantities.
Theorem 4.17 (ORIUS Core Bound — General CPS) . Let (X, U,f, S, ˆx) be a CPS satis-
fying Assumptions A1, A2, A4–A7. Suppose the instantiated domain supplies a predictable

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 97 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 63
residual-risk budget sequence rt such that
P[xt+1 /∈ S | Ht] ≤ rt for all t,
and suppose further that the domain justiﬁes the reference-row envelope rt ≤ α(1−wt). Then
the expected total violation count over horizon T satisﬁes
E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T,
whereαis the conformal miscoverage level and ¯w =T − 1 ∑T
t=1wt is the episode-average reli-
ability. The theorem is therefore an aggregation result for an explicit risk-envelope contract;
it is not presented as a direct consequence of vanilla conformal exchangeability alone.
Remark. The bound depends on α, ¯w, and T once the per-step budget has already been
justiﬁed. That makes it a useful design tool, but only after the theorem-local budget contract
has been stated. The battery-domain derivation below provides exactly that extra step; it
does not silently identify wt with a probability by deﬁnition.

4.5.8 Corollaries
Corollary 4.20 (Perfect-telemetry collapse) . When ¯w = 1 for all t, the core bound gives
E[V ] = 0 on any episode satisfying the battery risk-envelope contract. The corollary should
therefore be read as a statement about the instantiated budget, not as a universal theorem for
all observation models.
Corollary 4.21 (Reliability-proportional safety) . For ﬁxed αand T , the expected violation
count is linear in the average unreliability (1 − ¯w). Improving average reliability by ∆w
reduces the violation bound by α∆wT .
Corollary 4.22 (Intervention suﬃciency) . If the shield’s intervention rate satisﬁes IR ≥
1 − ¯w, then the runtime policy is operating in a regime consistent with the battery-side
budget interpretation: intervention pressure tracks the average reliability deﬁcit. This is an
interpretive corollary, not a converse optimality theorem.
4.5.9 What This Theorem Does Not Prove
1. The bound is on expected violations, not on worst-case or high-probability violations.
A concentration inequality (e.g., via Hoeﬀding) would tighten the result to a PAC-style
guarantee at the cost of a
√
T ln(1/δ) term.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 101 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 67
2. The bound does not follow from conformal exchangeability alone. The extra step is
the explicit predictable risk budget P[Zt = 1 | Ht] ≤ α(1 −wt) supplied by the battery
witness analysis.
3. The bound does not account for the shield’s ability to prevent violations proactively;
it only envelopes the residual risk once the per-step budget has been speciﬁed.

4.6 Observation Necessity / No Free Safety Theorem
This chapter proves the Observation Necessity / No Free Safety theorem: any controller
that ignores observation quality—that is, treats all telemetry as equally reliable—admits
admissible degraded-observation sequences on which observed-state legality and true-state
safety separate. The result is constructive and class-scoped: it shows that reliability-aware
repair is necessary within the class of controllers that keep a ﬁxed safety margin while the
observation channel degrades.
4.6.1 Domain-Agnostic Statement
We ﬁrst state the necessity result for an arbitrary CPS.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 104 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 70
Theorem 4.23 (Observation Necessity / No Free Safety — General CPS) . Let (X, U,f, S, ˆx)
be a CPS satisfying Assumption A2 with g(wt)> 0 under degraded quality. For any quality-
ignorant controller π: X → U (one whose action depends on ˆxt but not on wt), there exists
an admissible degraded-observation sequence {ξt}T
t=1 and a time index t⋆ such that
ϕobs(π(ˆxt⋆)) = 1 and ϕtrue(π(ˆxt⋆)) = 0.
Thus no ﬁxed-margin, quality-ignorant controller can rule out observation-action safety gaps
across all admissible degraded-observation sequences in this class. The theorem is a construc-
tive separation result, not a blanket statement about every possible safe controller architecture.
The proof is constructive: we exhibit a three-phase fault sequence (baiting → injection
→ violation) that exploits the controller’s ﬁxed margin. The battery domain provides the
concrete construction below.
4.6.2 Battery-Domain Instantiation: Quality-Ignorant Controller
Class
Deﬁnition 4.24 (Quality-ignorant controller). A controllerπis quality-ignorant if its action
depends only on the observed state and not on the reliability score:
at =π(ˆxt), ∂π
∂wt
= 0 for all t.
Equivalently,πuses the same decision rule regardless of whether wt = 1. 0 (perfect telemetry)
or wt = 0. 01 (near-blackout).
The deterministic LP and the CQR-only controller are both quality-ignorant: neither ad-
justs its dispatch based on telemetry reliability. By contrast, DC3S is quality-aware through
the RUI inﬂation module.
4.6.3 Auxiliary Lemmas
Lemma 4.25 (Admissible fault sequence existence) . For any quality-ignorant controller π
operating on the battery plant with dropout rate d >0, there exists a fault sequence {ξt}T
t=1
satisfying Assumption A2 such that:
1. Each ξt is realisable under the dropout/drift model.
2. The fault sequence is temporally concentrated: faults cluster in a window of length
ℓf ≤ T/ 4.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 105 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 71
3. Within the fault window, |ξt|>δbnd for at least fbnd ·ℓf steps.
Proof. The dropout model generates i.i.d. Bernoulli (d) packet losses. By the coupon-collector
argument, a run of k consecutive dropouts occurs with probability dk > 0 for any ﬁnite k.
Choosek large enough that the accumulated drift exceeds δbnd. The resulting fault sequence
satisﬁes all three conditions.
Lemma 4.26 (No margin compensation for quality-ignorant controllers) . A quality-ignorant
controller πuses a ﬁxed margin (or no margin) that does not grow when wt decreases. For-
mally, the eﬀective margin mπ
t satisﬁes mπ
t = mπ for a constant mπ ≥ 0 independent of
wt.
Proof. By Deﬁnition 4.24, πdoes not depend on wt. Any margin implicit in π’s decision
rule is therefore constant with respect to reliability.
4.6.4 Constructive Fault-Sequence Logic
The Observation Necessity / No Free Safety proof is constructive: we exhibit a speciﬁc fault
sequence that causes the quality-ignorant controller to violate constraints. The construction
proceeds in three phases:
1. Baiting phase (steps 1 to t0): Telemetry is perfect ( wt = 1 , ξt = 0 ). The con-
troller optimises freely and drives SOC near a constraint boundary (Lemma 4.6 from
Chapter 4.3).
2. Fault injection phase (steps t0 + 1 to t0 +ℓf ): A burst of packet dropouts and
covariate drift is injected. The observation error accumulates: |ξt|> δbnd. Because π
ignores wt, it does not widen its margin.
3. Violation realisation (step t∗): The controller selects an action based on the stale/-
drifted observation that pushes the true SOC across the constraint boundary.
4.6.5 Main Theorem: Observation Necessity / No Free Safety
Theorem 4.27 (Observation Necessity / No Free Safety) . Under Assumptions A2 and A11,
for any ﬁxed-margin quality-ignorant controller πand any dropout rate d >0, there exists
an admissible fault sequence such that πincurs at least one true-state violation:
∃t∗ ∈ {1,...,T } : ϕobs(π(ˆxt∗)) = 1 and ϕtrue(π(ˆxt∗)) = 0.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 106 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 72
The theorem is qualitative and witness-based. The rate at which OASG grows with degradation
budget is supplied separately by the boundary- indistinguishability lower bound in T10.
Proof. By Lemma 4.25, there exists a fault sequence with a concentrated fault window in
which |ξt|>δbnd.
By Lemma 4.26, the controller’s margin mπ does not increase during the fault window.
During the baiting phase, the controller drives SOC within δbnd of a constraint boundary
(Lemma 4.6).
At step t∗ in the fault window where |ξt∗|> δbnd +mπ, the observation error exceeds
the controller’s ﬁxed margin. The controller sees ˆxt∗ as safely within bounds and selects a
dispatch action that is feasible under ˆxt∗ but pushes the true state xt∗ across the boundary:
xt∗ + ∆(π(ˆxt∗)) = ˆxt∗ − ξt∗ + ∆(π(ˆxt∗)) < SOCmin.
Hence ϕtrue(π(ˆxt∗)) = 0 while ϕobs(π(ˆxt∗)) = 1 .

Table 4.10: Mapping the Observation Necessity / No Free Safety theorem to the battery
domain.
Formal element Battery meaning
Quality-ignorantπ Deterministic LP; CQR-only controller
Fixed margin mπ LP: m = 0; CQR: m =qt (not inﬂated by wt)

δbnd SOC distance to rated min/max
Violation True SOC below SOC min or above SOC max

4.6.8 Corollary
Corollary 4.28 (Reliability awareness is necessary) . For the violation bound E[V ] ≤ α(1 −
¯w)T (Theorem 4.19) to hold, the controller must be quality-aware: it must use wt (or an
equivalent reliability signal) to modulate its safety margin on the witness class used in this
chapter. No ﬁxed-margin controller can inherit the battery-style risk-envelope argument un-
changed under persistent admissible faults with ¯w <1.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 107 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 73
Figure 4.4: P95 violation severity for quality-ignorant controllers (LP, CQR-only) versus
DC3S across dropout rates. Quality-ignorant controllers exhibit non-zero severity at all
d> 0, consistent with the constructive witness used in Theorem 4.27.
Proof. Theorem 4.27 shows that a quality-ignorant controller incurs at least one violation
under an admissible fault sequence. By repeating the construction across ⌊T/ (2ℓf )⌋ non-
overlapping fault windows, the expected violation count grows as Ω(T ), exceeding any o(T )
bound.
4.6.9 What This Theorem Does Not Prove
1. It does not claim that quality-ignorant controllers fail on every fault sequence—only
that there exists at least one admissible sequence that causes failure.
2. It does not specify the optimal form of quality awareness; DC3S’s inﬂation rule mt =
qt/ (wt +ϵ) is one admissible design, not necessarily the unique best.
3. It does not address adversarial manipulation of wt itself; if the adversary can spoof the
reliability score, the theorem’s assumptions are violated.
4.6.10 Chapter Summary
The Observation Necessity / No Free Safety theorem is the fourth rung of the theorem lad-
der. It proves that ignoring observation quality is not merely sub-optimal on the defended
witness class: any ﬁxed-margin quality-ignorant controller can be driven to violation by an

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 108 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 74
admissible fault sequence. The constructive proof identiﬁes the exact mechanism (baiting +
fault injection + boundary proximity) and the empirical ablation results (Chapter 6.2) con-
ﬁrm the theorem’s prediction: removing reliability-aware inﬂation from DC3S re-introduces
violations.
Bridge to Comparative Baselines
The controlled comparison against Tube MPC, Control Barrier Functions, and Lagrangian
Safe RL is retained in the dedicated standalone chapter, Chapter 9, rather than duplicated
inside the live merged theory surface. That split is intentional. The merged chapter is the
authoritative thesis path for theorem statements, assumption discipline, and proof-facing
runtime objects. The baseline chapter is the authoritative empirical path for head-to-head
controller comparisons. Keeping those roles separate reduces maintenance drift and keeps
the theory chapter from quietly turning into a second empirical-results chapter.
4.7 Theorems T5–T8: Temporal and Behavioral Ex-
tensions
This chapter extends the per-step safety results of Chapters 4.4– 4.6 into the temporal and
behavioral dimensions. Within the ﬂagship battery theorem ladder, this chapter contributes
Theorems T5–T8: certiﬁcate validity horizon, certiﬁcate expiration bound, feasible fallback
existence, and graceful degradation dominance. These results are integrated with a broader
theorem-like surface that also includes precursor statements, chapter-local supporting results,
and appendix restatements catalogued in Appendix r. The certiﬁcate half-life result remains
a corollary, and safe-budget monotonicity remains a proposition.

4.7.5 Fallback and Graceful Degradation
When the reliability score drops below the minimum threshold wmin, the DC3S shield tran-
sitions from normal operation to a fallback policy . This section formalises the fallback’s
existence and its dominance over uncontrolled degradation.
Feasible Fallback Existence
Theorem 4.34 (Feasible fallback existence) . Under Assumptions A1, A4, and A8, for any
safe battery state xt, the fallback policy πfb obeys a piecewise certiﬁed rule:
either afb
t = (0, 0) preserves safety on the interior,
or afb
t is a boundary-aware safe-landing action with ϕ(xt+1) = 1.
If neither case can be certiﬁed under the active ramp, power, and model-error constraints,
the theorem surface fails closed rather than asserting a false fallback guarantee.
Proof. If the current SOC has enough interior slack, zero dispatch gives xt+1 = xt +ϵt
with |ϵt| ≤ϵmodel, so the next state remains inside the safe interval. If the current SOC is
safe but too close to a boundary for zero dispatch to be certiﬁed, the safe-landing controller
computes a charge or discharge action that moves the SOC toward the interior safe zone while
respecting the active battery limits. The promoted runtime validator certiﬁes exactly these
two cases and returns infeasible rather than over-claiming when neither case is safe. Hence
the defended T7 surface is the piecewise hold-or-safe-landing rule and nothing broader.
Graceful Degradation Dominance
Theorem 4.35 (Graceful degradation dominance) . Under Assumptions A1–A5 and A8,
consider a paired graceful and uncontrolled rollout evaluated on the same admissible fault
trace. If the graceful controller releases a violation sequence zgraceful
1:T that is stepwise no worse

V graceful ≤ V uncontrolled
with strict inequality whenever the graceful sequence is strictly smaller on at least one step.
This sequence-level comparison is the full defended T8 surface; the stronger absorbing-landing
interpretation remains supporting-only.
Proof. Summing the paired stepwise inequality zgraceful

strictly smaller on at least one step. The executable helper evaluate_graceful_degradation_dominance()
implements precisely this paired-sequence comparison and does not claim a standalone
absorbing-landing dynamics proof.

Proof. During landing, the reliability score is below wmin and the uncertainty interval widens

4.7.8 What This Chapter Does Not Prove
1. The certiﬁcate validity horizon is a lower bound on the true safe horizon; the actual
safe horizon may be longer because drift is typically sub-maximal.
2. The half-life corollary assumes sub-Gaussian drift; heavy-tailed drift distributions
would require a diﬀerent tail bound and would yield a shorter half-life.
3. The graceful degradation dominance result compares to an uncontrolled baseline. A
more sophisticated adaptive controller that is not quality-ignorant but uses a diﬀerent
inﬂation rule might also achieve zero violations with lower revenue loss.
4. The safe-budget monotonicity holds only during the landing phase; during normal
operation, the budget ﬂuctuates with wt.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 114 ===
CHAPTER 4. ORIUS FRAMEWORK AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS 80
5. Multi-asset (ﬂeet) temporal certiﬁcates are not covered here; compositional temporal
extension is deferred to Chapter 6.12.
4.7.9 Chapter Summary
This chapter extends the ORIUS safety guarantee from per-step claims to temporal and
behavioral horizons. Three temporal results—certiﬁcate validity horizon (Theorem 4.31),
expiration bound (Theorem 4.32), and half-life corollary (Corollary 4.33)—quantify how
long a safety certiﬁcate remains valid after issuance. Three behavioral results—fallback
existence (Theorem 4.34), graceful degradation dominance (Theorem 4.35), and safe-budget
monotonicity (Proposition 4.36)—formalise the landing protocol and prove it is strictly safer
than uncontrolled dispatch. Together, these extensions complete the theorem ladder initiated
in Chapter 4.3.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 115 ===
Chapter 5
System Deployment and Testbench
Architecture
5.1 Forecasting and Calibration
Dispatch safety begins with forecast quality: an optimiser can only be as reliable as the
prediction intervals it conditions on. This chapter presents the energy management instanti-
ation of the ORIUS forecasting layer. The methods—six model families, conformal quantile
regression, OQE reliability scoring, and RAC interval inﬂation—are domain-agnostic; their
instantiation here uses the locked DE ENTSO-E load dataset as the primary validation
surface. Chapter 7.1 applies the same forecasting layer across the tiered ORIUS runtime
package, with claim strength governed later by the universal validation tables.
5.1.1 Forecast Targets
Three operational targets are forecast at hourly resolution over a 48-step rolling horizon
(H = 48):
1. load_mw — net system load (MW).
2. wind_mw — aggregate wind generation (MW).
3. solar_mw — aggregate PV generation (MW).
A fourth target, price_eur_mwh, is forecast for the Germany surface only and used in the
dispatch objective (Section 3.4.5, Chapter 3.4). All targets are standardised to zero mean
and unit variance before model ingestion; predictions are de-standardised before interval
construction.
81

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 116 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 82
5.1.2 Model Families
Six model families span the spectrum from gradient-boosted trees to attention-based deep
architectures:
Table 5.1: Forecast model families and key hyper-parameters.
Family Type Key hyper-params Notes
GBM Gradient-boosted trees depth, lr, rounds Strongest on locked DE dataset
LSTM Recurrent (gated) hidden, layers, dropout Baseline sequential model
TCN Temporal convolution kernel, dilation, channels Parallelisable alternative to LSTM
N-BEATS Basis-expansion blocks stacks, width, sharing Interpretable decomposition
TFT Temporal fusion heads, hidden, quantiles Multi-horizon with variable selection
PatchTST Patch-based transformer patch len, heads, depth Channel-independent patching
GBM (LightGBM). Gradient Boosted Machines trained via LightGBM achieve the low-
est MAE on the locked Germany surface for load and wind targets. The model ingests 94 engi-
neered features including calendar dummies, rolling statistics, and weather forecasts. Quan-
tile regression heads at τ∈ { 0. 025, 0. 05, 0. 10, 0. 25, 0. 50, 0. 75, 0. 90, 0. 95, 0. 975} are trained
independently, providing native prediction intervals before conformal calibration.
LSTM. A two-layer LSTM with 128 hidden units and 10% dropout serves as the recurrent
baseline. Input sequences of 168 h (one week) are consumed to produce a 48-step ahead
forecast. The LSTM captures temporal dependencies but is prone to over-smoothing during
rapid ramp events.
TCN. A Temporal Convolutional Network with dilated causal convolutions (kernel size 7, 4
dilation layers) provides a parallelisable alternative to the LSTM. TCN achieves comparable
accuracy but trains 3–5 × faster due to the absence of sequential hidden-state propagation.
N-BEATS. The Neural Basis Expansion Analysis architecture decomposes the forecast
into trend and seasonality stacks using learnable polynomial and Fourier basis functions.
The interpretable variant is used, with 3 trend stacks (polynomial degree 3) and 3 seasonality
stacks (harmonics 5).
TFT. The Temporal Fusion Transformer combines variable selection, gated residual con-
nections, and multi-head attention for multi-horizon quantile forecasting. TFT is the only

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 117 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 83
architecture in the family that natively produces probabilistic forecasts without a separate
quantile-regression head.
PatchTST. The Patch Time-Series Transformer segments the input sequence into non-
overlapping patches of length 16, embeds each patch, and applies a standard transformer
encoder with 4 heads and 3 layers. Channel-independent processing avoids cross-variable
leakage and reduces parameter count.
5.1.3 Training Protocol and Leakage Control
Temporal leakage is the primary threat to forecast evaluation integrity. The following pro-
tocol prevents it:
1. Expanding-window split. Training data grows monotonically; the test window is
always strictly future. No shuﬄe split is permitted.
2. Embargo gap. A 48-hour embargo separates the end of the training window from
the start of the test window, ensuring that no feature derived from a lagged target can
leak future information.
3. Walk-forward evaluation. The test set is divided into non-overlapping 168-hour
(one-week) windows. Metrics are computed per window and then aggregated (mean,
P95).
4. Feature freeze. The feature engineering pipeline is frozen before model training
begins. No feature is constructed using information from the test period.
5. Seed locking. All random seeds (NumPy, PyTorch, LightGBM) are ﬁxed at 42 for
the primary run, with reproducibility veriﬁed across seeds {11, 22, 33, 44}.
5.1.4 Conformal Prediction Intervals
Point forecasts are converted to prediction intervals using Conformalized Quantile Regression
(CQR), following the split-conformal framework:
1. Train quantile regressors at levels α/2 and 1−α/2 on the training fold to obtain ˆqα/ 2(x)
and ˆq1− α/ 2(x).
2. On a held-out calibration fold of size n, compute the conformity scores:
si = max
(
ˆqα/ 2(xi) − yi, y i − ˆq1− α/ 2(xi)
)
, i = 1,...,n. (5.1)

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 118 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 84
3. Let ˆq be the ⌈(1 − α)(n + 1)⌉/n quantile of {s1,...,s n}.
4. The prediction interval for a new point xn+1 is:
C(xn+1) =
[
ˆqα/ 2(xn+1) − ˆq, ˆq1− α/ 2(xn+1) + ˆq
]
. (5.2)
CQR provides a ﬁnite-sample marginal coverage guarantee: Pr[yn+1 ∈C(xn+1)] ≥ 1 −α,
assuming exchangeability of the calibration and test data.
5.1.5 Marginal Coverage
On the locked Germany surface, the GBM + CQR pipeline achieves PICP@90 = 100% and
PICP@95 = 100% for load (marginal coverage). Wind and solar targets also achieve marginal
coverage above the nominal level. These aggregate numbers, however, mask conditional
failures.
5.1.6 Reliability-Conditioned Under-Coverage
When coverage is stratiﬁed by the DC3S reliability score wt (Chapter 4.1), the picture
changes substantially. Table 5.2 reproduces the locked group-coverage results.
Table 5.2: CQR group coverage at 90% nominal level, stratiﬁed by reliability group. Source:
reports/publication/cqr_group_coverage.csv.
Target Group PICP@90 Mean width (MW)
load_mw low 0.802 2 147
load_mw mid 0.825 2 305
load_mw high 0.807 2 345
wind_mw low 0.488 464
wind_mw mid 0.396 285
wind_mw high 0.410 381
solar_mw low 0.903 796
solar_mw mid 0.887 869
solar_mw high 0.816 853
Wind intervals are catastrophically under-covered in all three groups (39–49% vs. 90%
nominal), conﬁrming that marginal guarantees do not transfer to conditional subsets. Load
intervals under-cover by 8–20 percentage points; solar intervals are closest to nominal but
still under-cover in the high-variability group.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 119 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 85
5.1.7 Solar and Wind Calibration Weaknesses
Two structural factors drive the calibration gap:
Wind non-stationarity. Wind generation exhibits regime switches (storm fronts, lulls)
that violate the exchangeability assumption underlying CQR. The calibration fold, drawn
from a single contiguous period, may not contain suﬃcient representation of extreme regimes,
leading to narrow intervals that fail during the test period.
Solar bimodality. Solar generation has a deterministic diurnal envelope (sunrise–sunset)
overlaid with a stochastic cloud-cover component. During nighttime hours, the true gen-
eration is identically zero, making any nonzero interval width wasteful. During daytime,
cloud transients produce heavy-tailed residuals that require wide intervals. A single set of
conformity scores cannot eﬃciently cover both regimes simultaneously.
5.1.8 Why Calibration Alone Cannot Close the Gap
The group-coverage results demonstrate a fundamental limitation: post-hoc conformal cali-
bration on a static calibration fold cannot guarantee conditional coverage under distribution
shift or telemetry degradation.
Three pathways to closing the gap are pursued in subsequent chapters:
1. Reliability-adaptive inﬂation (DC3S, Chapter 4.1): the interval width is inﬂated
in real time as a function of the observation quality score wt, the drift ﬂag dt, and the
stale count st, compensating for the conditional under-coverage.
2. Fault-tolerant invariant tube (FTIT, Chapter 4.1): a robust state tube propagates
worst-case SOC bounds forward, providing a safety backstop that does not depend on
interval calibration.
3. Online conformal update (ACI baseline, Chapter 5.2): the conformal quantile is
updated online using the most recent residuals, partially adapting to distribution shift
but without observation-quality feedback.
5.1.9 Wind Coverage Gap: Diagnosis, Bound, and Partial Mitiga-
tion
Wind PICP@90 of 39–49% is the most prominent calibration failure in this thesis. This
section documents the root cause, the scope of the gap, and the partial mitigation already

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 120 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 86
in place.
Root cause: regime non-stationarity. Wind generation follows strongly seasonal and
synoptic regimes. The CQR calibration set is a ﬁxed temporal window; when the test set
contains a regime not represented in calibration (e.g. a prolonged high-pressure anticyclone
producing near-zero wind for days), the conformal quantile ˆqα is badly underﬁt. The residuals
on test are heavy-tailed relative to the calibration distribution, so 50% of test points fall
outside an interval sized for a stationary distribution.
Scope of the gap. Wind conditional under-coverage is a known limitation, not a hidden
failure. Table 5.2 documents the 39–49% values explicitly. The DC3S shield compensates via
two mechanisms: (1) the OQE reliability weight wt drops when the forecast residual exceeds
expected magnitude, triggering RUI inﬂation; (2) Mondrian CQR stratiﬁcation by reliability
group (detailed in Chapter 6.7) partially recovers conditional coverage within each stratum,
though marginal wind coverage remains below nominal.
Quantitative bound. From reports/publication/reliability_group_coverage_phase3.
csv, the wind stratum with wt ≥ 0. 8 achieves PICP@90 = 72%; the stratum with wt < 0. 5
achieves 31%. The RUI module inﬂates intervals by 1/w t for low-reliability steps, which
raises eﬀective coverage toward the target without requiring full marginal calibration. How-
ever, this is a safety argument (zero dispatch violations), not a coverage-equality argument:
the thesis claims zero true-SOC violations, not marginal wind coverage at 90%. The latter
is a known open problem in conformal prediction under distribution shift [ 12].
Consequence for safety. Critically, wind under-coverage propagates to the dispatch layer
only if the DC3S shield does not compensate. Empirically, zero SOC violations are observed
despite 39–49% wind coverage, conﬁrming that RUI inﬂation bridges the calibration gap for
the safety claim even when it cannot close the statistical coverage gap.
5.1.10 Chapter Summary
This chapter described the six forecast model families (GBM, LSTM, TCN, N-BEATS, TFT,
PatchTST), the leakage-controlled training protocol, and the CQR conformal calibration
layer. Marginal coverage meets or exceeds the nominal level on all targets, but reliability-
stratiﬁed analysis reveals severe conditional under-coverage, particularly for wind (39–49% vs.
90% nominal). Solar calibration is weakened by diurnal bimodality, and wind by regime non-

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 121 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 87
stationarity. These structural calibration failures motivate the observation-aware adaptive
inﬂation developed in the next chapter.
5.2 CPSBench Multi-Domain Evaluation Framework
Standard forecasting benchmarks evaluate prediction accuracy in isolation: they score point
forecasts or intervals against held-out actuals without ever closing the loop through a physical
plant. The CPSBench Battery Track closes that loop, coupling forecasts to a battery dispatch
controller and evaluating safety on the true (unobserved) state trajectory under replayable
telemetry faults.
5.2.1 Why Standard Evaluation Is Insuﬃcient
A forecast benchmark that reports MAE and PICP answers only half the question. Consider
two controllers:
• Controller A achieves PICP@90 = 92% with narrow intervals, maximising economic
value—but during the 8% of uncovered steps, the dispatched action drives the battery
SOC below the safe ﬂoor, triggering a BMS trip that takes the asset oﬄine for 30
minutes.
• Controller B achieves PICP@90 = 96% with wider intervals, sacriﬁcing 3% of revenue—
but never triggers a BMS trip.
Standard metrics rank A above B on interval sharpness and close to B on coverage. Yet in
operational terms, A is unsafe and B is safe. The missing ingredient is closed-loop evaluation
on the true plant state , which requires:
1. A plant simulator with deterministic SOC dynamics.
2. A fault injector that degrades telemetry between the plant and the controller.
3. Separate logging of truth-state and observed-state trajectories.
4. Safety metrics computed exclusively on the truth trajectory.
CPSBench provides all four.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 122 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 88
5.2.2 Dual-Trajectory Evaluation
The central architectural choice in CPSBench is the maintenance of two parallel trajectories
at every step t:
Truth trajectory {ztrue
t }. The plant simulator advances the true SOC using the executed
action and the true load/renewables signal. This trajectory is logged but never exposed
to the controller.
Observed trajectory {˜zt}. The fault injector corrupts the truth signal before delivering it
to the controller. The controller makes all decisions based on this degraded view.
Safety violations are deﬁned as events where the truth-trajectory SOC exits the safe
envelope [Emin,E max]. This is the true-SOC violation (as opposed to an observed-SOC
violation, which may be masked by sensor error).
The dual-trajectory architecture ensures that the benchmark answers the correct question:
“did the battery actually violate its SOC limits?” rather than “did the controller think it
violated?”
5.2.3 Truth-State and Observed-State Logging
At each step the CPSBench runner logs the following record to the episode trace (cf. reports/publication/dc3s_step_trace.csv):
Table 5.3: Per-step logging schema in CPSBench.
Field Source Trajectory
load_observed_mw Fault injector Observed
renewables_observed_mw Fault injector Observed
renewables_true_mw Plant simulator Truth
reliability_w OQE Observed
drift_flag Page–Hinkley Observed
inflation RAC-Cert Computed
proposed_charge_mw Optimiser Decision
proposed_discharge_mw Optimiser Decision
safe_charge_mw DC3S shield Decision
safe_discharge_mw DC3S shield Decision
intervened Shield Decision
soc_after_mwh Plant simulator Truth
guarantee_passed Certiﬁcate Computed
Every ﬁeld is deterministic for a given (scenario, seed, controller) triple, enabling exact
reproduction of any individual step.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 123 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 89
5.2.4 Fault Taxonomy and Replayable Schedules
CPSBench implements six fault types, each controlled by a severity parameter and applied
by a deterministic injector seeded from the episode RNG:
Table 5.4: Fault taxonomy with severity parameterisation.
Fault Parameter Sweep levels Eﬀect on ˜zt
Dropout Rate p 0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.20, 0.30 ˜zt = NaN w.p. p
Delay jitter Max delay (s) 0, 1, 5, 15 ˜zt =zt− δ, δ∼ U [0,d max]
Spike Scale σ 0, 1, 2, 3 ˜zt =zt ·(1 +ϵ), ϵ∼ N (0,σ2)
Stale sensor Window (steps) 0, 2, 4, 8 ˜zt =zt− s for s consecutive steps
Drift Rate (MW/step) 0, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0 ˜zt =zt +β·t
Reorder Rate r 0, 0.05, 0.15 Adjacent packets swapped w.p. r
Each fault schedule is a deterministic function of the episode seed, meaning that any
researcher with the same code and seed will reproduce exactly the same fault pattern. The
drift_combo scenario applies all six faults simultaneously at moderate severity, representing
a worst-case but ﬁeld-plausible operating condition.

truth trajectory:

5.2.6 Benchmark Invariants
The following invariants are checked programmatically after each episode and recorded in
the certiﬁcate stream:
1. Energy conservation. The truth-trajectory SOC at step t + 1 must equal the SOC
at step t plus the energy balance from the executed action, to within ﬂoating-point
tolerance (ϵ<10− 6 MWh).
2. Power bounds. The executed power must satisfy 0 ≤ P +
t ≤ P +
max and 0 ≤ P −
t ≤ P −
max
at every step.
3. Mutual exclusion. P +
t ·P −
t = 0 at every step (no simultaneous charge and discharge).
4. Certiﬁcate completeness. Every step must emit a certiﬁcate with zero missing ﬁelds
(tracked as certificate_missing_fields in the results CSV).
5. Determinism. Re-running the same (scenario, seed, controller) triple must produce
bit-identical results.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 125 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 91
5.2.7 Battery Track as Benchmark Foundation
The CPSBench Battery Track serves as the empirical foundation for all subsequent evaluation
chapters in this dissertation:
• Chapter 6.10 uses the drift_combo scenario to measure certiﬁcate half-life under
communication blackout.
• Chapter 5.3 extends the battery track into a public benchmark release with stan-
dardised controller interfaces and reproduction instructions.
• The locked sweep results in reports/publication/cpsbench_merged_sweep.csv con-
tain the full four-dimensional severity sweep (dropout rate × delay × reorder rate ×
spikeσ) across all eight controllers and ﬁve seeds.
• The fault breakdown in reports/publication/dc3s_fault_breakdown.csv isolates
the per-fault-type violation and intervention rates for each controller.
The battery track is intentionally single-asset: it evaluates one battery on one dataset
surface at a time. Fleet-level and cross-region extensions are addressed in Chapters 5.3
and 6.10.
5.2.8 Chapter Summary
This chapter introduced the CPSBench Battery Track, a closed-loop evaluation harness that
couples forecasts to battery dispatch under replayable telemetry faults. The dual-trajectory
architecture separates truth state from observed state, enabling safety metrics (TSVR, sever-
ity) to be computed on the actual plant trajectory rather than the controller’s degraded view.
Six fault types—dropout, delay jitter, spike, stale sensor, drift, and reorder—are swept across
conﬁgurable severity levels with deterministic episode seeds. Three metric families (safety,
economic, environmental) provide a comprehensive evaluation surface. Benchmark invariants
(energy conservation, power bounds, mutual exclusion, certiﬁcate completeness, determinism)
ensure result integrity. The battery track forms the empirical foundation for all subsequent
evaluation and extension chapters.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 126 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 92
5.3 ORIUS-Bench: Battery Track and Benchmark Con-
tract
This chapter promotes ORIUS-Bench from a mention of benchmark release into a defended
contract chapter. The battery track matters not because it proves universal superiority, but
because it deﬁnes the reusable API, replay protocol, metric schema, and artifact bundle that
make observation-aware safety evaluation reproducible.
5.3.1 Benchmark Scope and Design Goals
ORIUS-Bench (Battery Track) is an open, deterministic evaluation harness designed to an-
swer a single question: does a battery dispatch controller remain safe when its telemetry is
degraded?
Design goals:
1. Reproducibility: deterministic episode generation from a (scenario, seed) pair, elim-
inating run-to-run variance.
2. Fault isolation : each fault dimension (dropout, delay, spike, stale sensor, drift) is
independently controllable.
3. Truth separation : the harness maintains parallel truth and observed trajectories,
enabling unambiguous violation attribution.
4. Multi-controller comparison: any controller conforming to the benchmark contract
can be plugged in.
5.3.2 Episode Generation and Fault Replay
Episodes are generated by the battery benchmark scripts and replayed under named fault
schedules. The battery track carries independent severity knobs for dropout, delay-jitter,
out-of-order packets, spike magnitude, stale sensors, and drift combinations. This matters
because the replay protocol is part of the contribution: a score without a reproducible fault
schedule is not benchmark evidence.
5.3.3 Seven-Metric Schema
The ORIUS-Bench seven-metric schema (src/orius/orius_bench/metrics_engine.py) ex-
ports:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 127 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 93
1. True-State Violation Rate (TSVR),
2. Observation-Action Safety Gap (OASG),
3. Certiﬁcate Validity Accuracy (CV A),
4. Graceful Degradation Quality (GDQ),
5. Intervention Rate (IR),
6. Audit Completeness (AC), and
7. Recovery Latency (RL).
5.3.4 Schema and Adapter Contract
The benchmark exposes four schema groups through the locked artifact reports/publication/orius_bench_battery_schema_table.csv.
Table 5.5: ORIUS-Bench battery schema groups and representative ﬁelds.
Schema group Field Meaning
Controller API controller controller identiﬁer
Controller API safe_action_mw safety-repaired action power
Fault schema fault_dimension injected fault family
Fault schema severity severity bucket
Metric schema true_soc_violation_rate physical violation rate
Metric schema intervention_rate safety intervention rate
Artifact schema run_id execution lineage identiﬁer
Artifact schema source_script script that generated the run
This is the reusable contribution of ORIUS-Bench: a domain adapter can vary the plant
and constraints, but it must still conform to the controller API, fault replay contract, metric
schema, and artifact schema.
5.3.5 Controller Interface
A benchmark-compliant controller must expose a step() method returning at minimum:
• proposed_charge_mw, proposed_discharge_mw,
• safe_charge_mw, safe_discharge_mw,
• interval_lower, interval_upper, and
• certificate or an explicit null certiﬁcate.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 128 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 94
5.3.6 Fault Replay and Determinism
Battery leaderboard entries are only meaningful if replay is deterministic. ORIUS-Bench
therefore binds every run to:
1. a scenario name,
2. an explicit seed,
3. a named source script,
4. a run identiﬁer, and
5. a release-family manifest.
The supporting governance artifacts live in reports/publication/run_id_policy.json
and reports/publication/release_manifest.json. The benchmark result is therefore
not just a score table; it is a replayable artifact family.
5.3.7 Leaderboard Surface
Table 5.6 reproduces the locked battery leaderboard excerpt from reports/publication/orius_bench_battery_leaderboard.csv.

5.3.8 Ranking Under Degradation
The companion artifact reports/publication/battery_rank_reversal.csv shows that
degraded-observation ranking diverges from nominal ranking. In the current battery package,
dc3s_wrapped moves from nominal rank 7 to degraded rank 1, while robust_fixed_interval

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 129 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 95
falls from nominal rank 1 to degraded rank 7. That rank reversal is precisely why the battery
track needs truth-vs-observation separation instead of nominal scorecards.
5.3.9 What ORIUS-Bench Does and Does Not Prove
ORIUS-Bench proves three things well:
1. the battery evaluation protocol is reproducible,
2. the controller and adapter contract is explicit, and
3. degraded-observation ranking diﬀers materially from nominal ranking.
It does not by itself prove cross-domain superiority. The additional adapters and uniﬁed
harness are framework portability and auditability evidence; the locked battery theorem-
and-artifact surface remains the primary proof anchor of the thesis.
5.3.10 Summary
ORIUS-Bench (Battery Track) now appears in the thesis as a benchmark contract, not just
as a release note. Its defended value is replayability, schema discipline, and truth-separated
evaluation rather than universal dominance rhetoric.
5.4 Runtime Latency and Systems Footprint
Safety guarantees are worthless if the controller cannot compute them within the dispatch
cycle. This chapter benchmarks the wall-clock latency of every DC3S sub-component, char-
acterises the runtime memory footprint, and establishes that the full pipeline ﬁts comfortably
within a 30-second dispatch interval on commodity hardware.
5.4.1 Why Latency Matters for Real-Time Dispatch
Grid-scale BESS dispatch operates on intervals of 5–30 minutes for energy arbitrage and
2–4 seconds for frequency regulation. The DC3S pipeline must complete within the shortest
applicable interval. If the safety shield exceeds the cycle time, the controller either dispatches
uncertiﬁed actions or stalls, both unacceptable outcomes.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 130 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 96
5.4.2 Measurement Methodology
Latency is measured using Python time.perf_counter_ns() around each DC3S sub-component
during a 168-hour CPSBench episode under drift_combo (seed 42). The episode comprises
N = 168 hourly steps. All measurements are recorded in reports/publication/dc3s_latency_summary.csv
and reproduced below.
5.4.3 Per-Component Latency
Table 5.7 reports the wall-clock latency of each DC3S pipeline stage.
Table 5.7: Per-component DC3S latency statistics (milliseconds). Source:
reports/publication/dc3s_latency_summary.csv.
Component Mean Median P95 P99 Max
Reliability scoring (OQE) 0.026 0.021 0.043 0.130 0.712
Drift update (Page-Hinkley) 0.000 0.000 0.001 0.001 0.160
Uncertainty set build (RUI) 0.012 0.011 0.011 0.047 0.724
Action repair (SAF/Shield) 0.002 0.002 0.002 0.003 0.210
Full DC3S step 0.042 0.037 0.041 0.193 2.416
5.4.4 Statistical Summary
The full DC3S step completes in a median of 0.037 ms and a P99 of 0.193 ms . Even the
worst-case single step (2.416 ms) is three orders of magnitude below the 30-second dispatch
interval. The latency budget is dominated by reliability scoring (62% of mean time), followed
by the uncertainty-set build (29%). The action repair projection is negligible ( < 5%).
5.4.5 Runtime Memory Footprint
The DC3S pipeline maintains a sliding window of CQR residuals (default: 200 points), the
Page-Hinkley drift accumulator, and the current certiﬁcate state. Total resident memory is
approximately ∼ 12 MB for a single-battery instance, measured via tracemalloc snapshots:
• CQR residual buﬀer: ∼ 3.2 MB (200 points × 3 targets × quantile arrays).
• OQE feature history: ∼ 1.8 MB.
• Forecast model (LightGBM): ∼ 5.6 MB (pre-loaded).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 131 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 97
• Certiﬁcate and state overhead: ∼ 1.4 MB.
This footprint ﬁts within the 64–256 MB RAM available on typical edge gateways (e.g.,
NVIDIA Jetson Nano, Raspberry Pi 4).
5.4.6 Solver Infeasibility Rate
The LP solver (HiGHS via scipy.optimize.linprog) reports infeasible status when the
tightened SOC bounds leave no feasible dispatch. Across the full CPSBench sweep, the
solver-OK rate for DC3S+FTIT is ≥ 95.8%, with infeasibilities occurring only during extreme
drift episodes where the uncertainty set contracts the feasible region to near-zero width. In
these cases, the SAF module falls back to a safe-standby action (zero net power), preserving
safety at the cost of one lost dispatch cycle.
5.4.7 Observability Outputs
Each DC3S step emits a structured certiﬁcate containing:
• Proposed and safe actions (charge/discharge MW).
• Reliability score wt and its component features.
• CQR interval width and adaptive multiplier.
• Solver status and objective value.
• SHA-256 certiﬁcate hash for audit traceability.
These outputs are logged to reports/hil/hil_certificate_audit.csv (Chapter 7.2)
and can be streamed to an observability backend (e.g., Prometheus/Grafana) for real-time
monitoring.
5.4.8 Deployment Implications
The sub-millisecond median latency has three practical consequences:
1. Frequency regulation: DC3S can serve 4-second AGC signals with >99.99% on-time
delivery.
2. Fleet scaling : At 0.042 ms per battery, a single core can certify ∼ 700 batteries per 30-
second cycle, enabling ﬂeet-level deployment without parallelisation (cf. Chapter 6.12).
3. Edge deployment: The combined latency and memory footprint are compatible with
ARM-based edge gateways, eliminating cloud round-trip dependencies.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 132 ===
CHAPTER 5. SYSTEM DEPLOYMENT AND TESTBENCH ARCHITECTURE 98
5.4.9 Summary
The DC3S safety pipeline completes in a median of 0.037 ms per step, with worst-case latency
of 2.4 ms. The full pipeline including forecast, OQE, RUI, SAF, and certiﬁcate generation
ﬁts within ∼ 12 MB RAM. These ﬁgures conﬁrm that the safety guarantee is computationally
free in the context of grid-scale dispatch cycles, removing latency as a barrier to deployment.

at the price of a modest intervention rate.
6.1.2 Hidden True-State Violation Measurement
The TSVR metric captures violations that are invisible to the controller at decision time: the
observed SOC satisﬁes all constraints, but the true (plant-internal) SOC breaches a bound.
This hidden-violation phenomenon is the core motivation for the ORIUS safety layer.
For the deterministic LP under drift_combo:
• DE: 3.9% of steps violate in true state; P95 severity = 333.4 MWh.
• US: 4.2% of steps violate in true state; P95 severity = 298.1 MWh.
These violations occur silently—the LP reports feasibility at every step.
6.1.3 Zero-Violation Shield Result
The central empirical claim is:
Under the DC3S shield, the true-state violation rate is exactly zero across all four
fault scenarios, both regions, all dropout ratesd ∈ {0. 00, 0. 05, 0. 10, 0. 15, 0. 20, 0. 25, 0. 30},
and all seeds.
This zero-violation result holds because the shield’s tightened action set is constructed from
reliability-inﬂated conformal intervals that account for the gap between observed and true
state (cf. Theorem 4.14).
6.1.4 Intervention Rate and Conditional Conservatism
The DC3S shield intervenes—replacing the LP’s dispatch with a projected safe action—at a
rate of 2.8% (DE) and 3.1% (US) under drift_combo. Intervention is conditional: it occurs
only when the reliability score wt drops below its calibrated threshold, which correlates with
episodes of high telemetry degradation.
Proposition 6.1 (Conditional conservatism). Let I = {t :aLP
t ̸=asafe
t } be the intervention
set. Then
1
|I|
∑
t∈I
(1 − wt) ≥ 3 ¯wepisode,
where ¯wepisode is the episode-average reliability. In words, interventions concentrate in low-
reliability periods at more than three times the average unreliability level.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 135 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS101
6.1.5 Regional Value Surface
The economic impact of DC3S varies by region because the value of accurate dispatch de-
pends on price volatility and renewable penetration.
• DE: 7.11% cost savings relative to the LP baseline, and 0.30% carbon-intensity reduc-
tion.
• US (CAISO) : 0.11% cost savings and 0.13% carbon reduction.
The asymmetry reﬂects DE’s higher price spread and more volatile residual load. In both
regions the shield’s conservatism is more than oﬀset by the avoidance of penalty-inducing
constraint violations.
6.1.6 Robustness and Severity Surfaces
We sweep across dropout rates d ∈ [0, 0. 30] and measure TSVR, severity P95, and interven-
tion rate. The resulting severity surface (Figure 6.1) shows that:
1. Deterministic LP severity grows super-linearly with dropout.
2. CQR-only reduces severity but does not eliminate it.
3. DC3S maintains TSVR = 0 across the entire surface.
6.1.7 Operational Meaning
The results translate to operational guarantees as follows. A grid operator deploying DC3S
on a 100 MWh BESS can expect:
• No hidden SOC excursion beyond rated bounds, even under 30% packet dropout and
simultaneous sensor drift.
• Fewer than 3 interventions per 100 dispatch intervals on average.
• Net positive economics in high-volatility markets (DE) and roughly break-even in lower-
volatility markets (US CAISO).

6.1.9 Comparison with Alternative Safety Architectures
Table 6.3 positions DC3S against ﬁve major alternative approaches to safe control under
uncertainty. The central distinction is whether each method models observation quality as
a ﬁrst-class input to the safety shield.
Key insight. Every alternative method listed above achieves safety guarantees conditioned
on reliable observations . ORIUS is the ﬁrst framework that (a) diagnoses observation qual-
ity in real time, (b) propagates the quality score into the safety shield, and (c) provides
formal bounds that degrade gracefully as telemetry quality decreases. The No Free Safety
theorem (Chapter 4.6) proves that this quality-awareness is necessary, not optional: no
quality-ignorant controller can achieve target safety rates under adversarial fault sequences.
6.1.10 What Results Do and Do Not Prove
What the results establish. The empirical evidence demonstrates that DC3S achieves
zero true-state violations on the CPSBench battery plant under all tested fault combinations,
with bounded intervention cost.
What the results do not establish.
1. Out-of-distribution faults. The four fault families (dropout, delay, drift, stale) are
representative but not exhaustive.
2. Non-battery domains. Transfer to other CPS domains requires separate validation
(Chapter 4.7).
3. Optimality. DC3S is safe but not necessarily cost-optimal; the intervention rate is an
upper bound, not a lower bound, on the price of safety.
6.1.11 Chapter Summary
The main battery results establish three facts: (i) deterministic dispatch exposes signiﬁcant
hidden violations, (ii) DC3S eliminates them entirely, and (iii) the economic cost of safety
is modest and region-dependent. These results anchor the theoretical claims of Part III and
the extension claims of Part IV.
