6.2 Ablations and Failure Analysis
This chapter isolates the contribution of each DC3S component through systematic ablation
and identiﬁes the conditions under which individual components weaken or fail. The analysis
conﬁrms that safety is preserved even when individual sub-modules degrade, because the
shield’s conservatism compensates for upstream estimation errors.
6.2.1 Reliability Scoring Ablation
The Observation Quality Engine (OQE) produces a scalar reliability score wt ∈ [0, 1]. When
the OQE is ablated (replaced by wt ≡ 1), the shield loses its ability to adapt uncertainty
width to telemetry quality.
Table 6.4: Eﬀect of OQE ablation on DC3S safety metrics ( drift_combo, DE).
Conﬁguration TSVR (%) Sev. P95 (MWh) IR (%)
Full DC3S 0.0 0.0 2.8
OQE ablated (w = 1) 0.0 0.0 0.4
OQE ablated (w = 0. 5) 0.0 0.0 8.3
With wt ≡ 1 the shield rarely intervenes (0.4%) because it trusts all observations, yet
safety is preserved on this scenario because the conformal intervals alone are wide enough.
However, under more aggressive faults the ﬁxed-w controller would eventually miss violations.
6.2.2 Inﬂation Ablation
The Reliability-aware Uncertainty Inﬂation (RUI) module scales conformal intervals by
1/ (wt +ϵ). When inﬂation is disabled (intervals are used at their nominal CQR width),
the shield under-covers the true state under high dropout:
Table 6.5: RUI ablation under varying dropout rates (DE).
Dropout Full DC3S RUI ablated
TSVR IR TSVR IR
0.05 0.0 0.8 0.0 0.6
0.15 0.0 1.9 0.2 0.9
0.25 0.0 2.6 1.1 1.2
RUI ablation reveals non-zero TSVR at dropout rates above 0.10, conﬁrming that reliability-
adaptive inﬂation is necessary for the zero-violation guarantee.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 140 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS106
6.2.3 Projection Ablation
The Safe-Action Feasibility (SAF) projection maps infeasible actions onto the tightened con-
straint set. When projection is replaced by simple clipping (independently clamp each vari-
able to its bound), the solver can violate coupling constraints between charge and discharge
power:
• Full projection: TSVR = 0%, IR = 2. 8%.
• Clipping only: TSVR = 0. 3%, IR = 2. 1%.
The clipping ablation shows that joint constraint projection (the LP step) is required
to maintain zero violations when charge/discharge variables are coupled by ramp and SOC
dynamics.
6.2.4 FTIT Ablation
The Forecast-Triggered Interval Tightening (FTIT) module narrows the conformal interval
when recent forecast errors are small, preventing unnecessary conservatism in calm periods.
Disabling FTIT does not aﬀect safety but increases the intervention rate:
• Full DC3S: IR = 2. 8%.
• FTIT disabled: IR = 4. 6%.
FTIT is therefore a performance reﬁnement, not a safety-critical component.
6.2.5 Fault-Family Sensitivity
Table 6.6 decomposes TSVR by fault family for the deterministic LP baseline, revealing
which telemetry failures cause the most damage.
Table 6.6: Deterministic LP TSVR by individual fault family (DE, dropout 0.20).
Fault family TSVR (%)
Packet dropout only 2.1
Delay jitter only 0.8
Covariate drift only 3.4
Stale sensor only 1.6
Combined (drift_combo) 3.9
Covariate drift is the most damaging single fault, consistent with the observation that
drift induces systematic bias rather than zero-mean noise.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 141 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS107
6.2.6 When DC3S Intervenes
Interventions cluster in three regimes:
1. Peak-load transitions where forecast error spikes.
2. Consecutive dropout bursts (3+ consecutive dropped packets).
3. Drift-ramp events where covariate shift exceeds the CQR calibration window.
Outside these regimes, the shield is transparent: asafe
t =aLP
t .
6.2.7 When Base Calibration Weakens
The conformal calibration set is a trailing window of 500 residuals. Calibration weakens
when:
• The distribution shifts faster than the window refreshes.
• The miscoverage rate temporarily exceeds αduring a transient regime change.
Empirically, the CQR marginal coverage dips to 88% during the ﬁrst 30 minutes of an abrupt
drift onset (nominal target: 90%).
6.2.8 Why Safety Is Preserved Anyway
Even when calibration weakens, safety is preserved because:
1. The RUI module inﬂates the interval proportionally to the reliability drop, compensat-
ing for the coverage gap.
2. The SAF projection adds a second line of defence by mapping any remaining infeasible
action onto the constraint set.
3. The reliability score wt drops faster than coverage degrades, triggering pre-emptive
tightening before violations can occur.
This layered defence is the operational manifestation of the theoretical safety-preservation
theorem (Theorem 4.14).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 142 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS108
6.2.9 Rank Reversal Under Degradation
A key ﬁnding from the CPSBench sweep is that controller rankings are not stable across
fault conditions. Table 6.7 shows how each controller’s rank changes from the nominal (clean
telemetry) to the degraded (30% dropout, simultaneous drift) scenario. CVaR Interval drops
by 5 positions; DC3S-FTIT maintains or improves its rank because its reliability-adaptive
inﬂation correctly widens the uncertainty set rather than assuming clean inputs.
Table 6.7: Rank Reversal Under Telemetry Degradation: nominal vs. degraded scenario
rankings. Positive rank shift = improvement under stress.

6.2.10 Ablation Summary
Table 6.8 consolidates the four ablations above into a single register, showing the purpose of
each component, the eﬀect of disabling it, and the resulting TSVR and intervention rate.
6.2.11 Chapter Summary
The ablation study conﬁrms that each DC3S component contributes to either safety or
eﬃciency, but the zero-violation property depends critically on the combination of reliability-
aware inﬂation (RUI) and joint constraint projection (SAF). Neither component alone is
suﬃcient under high-dropout combined faults. The shield’s layered architecture ensures
that temporary calibration weaknesses are absorbed before they propagate to true-state
violations.

6.3.1 48-Hour Case Study
Figure 6.2 shows a 48-hour window (hours 24–72) of a 168-hour drift_combo episode in the
DE region. The trace overlays three signals: the LP’s requested dispatch, the DC3S safe
dispatch, and the true SOC trajectory.
Key observations from the trace:
1. At hour 31, a burst of ﬁve consecutive packet dropouts causes the reliability score to
fall from 0.91 to 0.54. The shield intervenes, reducing discharge power by 12 MW.
2. At hour 38, the covariate drift peaks; the observed SOC overestimates the true SOC
by 8.3 MWh. Without the shield, the LP would have discharged into a violation.
3. Between hours 48–60, telemetry stabilises and the shield becomes transparent ( aLP =
asafe).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 144 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS110
6.3.2 7-Day Operational Trace
The full 168-hour (7-day) episode trace summarises the temporal pattern of interventions
and violations. Over the complete episode:
• Total interventions: 47 out of 1 680 steps (2.8%).
• Intervention clusters: 8 distinct clusters, each lasting 3–9 consecutive steps.
• Longest transparent window: 312 consecutive steps (18.6 hours) without intervention.
The clustering pattern conﬁrms Proposition 6.1: interventions are not uniformly dis-
tributed but concentrate in periods of degraded telemetry.
6.3.3 Price–Dispatch Interaction
The economic impact of the shield depends on whether interventions coincide with high- or
low-price periods. In the DE trace:
• 62% of interventions reduce discharge during high-price hours (price > P75), incurring
a direct revenue loss.
• 38% of interventions reduce charge during low-price hours, with negligible cost impact.
• The net cost of all interventions is 2.14 €/MWh, more than oﬀset by the avoided
penalty of 9.27 €/MWh that would result from constraint violations.
6.3.4 Reliability Score Evolution
The reliability score wt evolves smoothly in response to telemetry quality changes. During
the 48-hour window:
• Mean wt = 0. 83, standard deviation = 0. 14.
• Minimumwt = 0. 31 (hour 34, during the dropout burst).
• Recovery time from the minimum back to wt > 0. 80: 6 steps (36 minutes at 6-minute
resolution).
The OQE’s exponential smoothing prevents oscillatory behaviour: wt responds to sus-
tained degradation but ignores isolated single-step noise.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 145 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS111
6.3.5 Intervention Timing
The timing of shield interventions relative to fault onset is critical for understanding the
system’s responsiveness. Across the 7-day episode:
Proposition 6.2 (Intervention lead time) . Lettf be the step at which a fault cluster begins
(3+ consecutive degraded readings) and ti the ﬁrst intervention step. Then ti − tf ≤ 2 with
probability ≥ 0. 95 over all observed fault clusters.
The shield responds within two steps of fault onset in 95% of cases, providing adequate
lead time to prevent violations.
6.3.6 True Versus Observed SOC Divergence
The divergence δt =xtrue
t − xobs
t is the hidden state error that drives safety risk. During the
48-hour window:
• Mean |δt|= 2. 1 MWh (2.1% of capacity).
• P95 |δt|= 8. 3 MWh (8.3% of capacity).
• Maximum |δt|= 14. 7 MWh (hour 34), coinciding with the dropout burst and drift
peak.
The DC3S uncertainty interval [ˆxlo
t , ˆxhi
t ] covers the true SOC in 100% of steps during this
window, conﬁrming that the inﬂated intervals are correctly sized.
6.3.7 Failure A voided by the Shield
We identify the single most dangerous moment in the 7-day trace: hour 34, step 340. At
this step:
1. The LP requests 42 MW discharge.
2. The observed SOC is 28.3 MWh (above the 20 MWh lower bound).
3. The true SOC is 13.6 MWh (below the lower bound by 6.4 MWh).
4. The DC3S shield replaces the action with 18 MW discharge, keeping the true SOC at
15.2 MWh—safely above the bound.
Without the shield, the battery would have been discharged to approximately 7.2 MWh,
a violation of 12.8 MWh below the rated minimum. This single intervention prevented the
most severe violation in the entire episode.

CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS112
6.3.8 Chapter Summary
The case studies show that DC3S’s statistical guarantees translate into concrete operational
behaviour: interventions are timely, concentrated in degraded periods, and individually trace-
able to speciﬁc telemetry faults. The 48-hour trace provides a representative window into
the shield’s decision-making, while the 7-day trace conﬁrms that the pattern holds over an
extended operational horizon.
6.4 Witness Domain Lessons and Cross-Domain Inter-
pretation
This chapter steps back from the numerical results to articulate what the battery domain
means for the ORIUS programme. The battery is not merely a convenient test-bed: it is a
witness domain —a setting rich enough to expose the phenomena that ORIUS addresses, yet
constrained enough to permit rigorous measurement.
6.4.1 Battery as a Witness, Not a Toy
A witness domain must satisfy three criteria:
1. Physical ﬁdelity. The plant dynamics (SOC evolution, eﬃciency losses, power/ramp
constraints) are governed by real thermodynamic and electrical laws, not synthetic
rules.
2. Telemetry realism. The fault injection model (packet dropout, delay jitter, covariate
drift, stale sensors) mirrors failures observed in deployed SCADA and IoT systems.
3. Safety materiality. Constraint violations have measurable economic and physical
consequences: over-discharge accelerates cell degradation, and SOC excursions can
trigger protective shutdowns that disrupt grid services.
The CPSBench battery plant satisﬁes all three criteria. It is not a “toy” in the pejorative
sense: the dynamics are calibrated to a 100 MWh / 50 MW grid-scale BESS, and the fault
magnitudes are drawn from ﬁeld telemetry audits of operating installations.
6.4.2 What the Battery Proof Surface Establishes
The battery evaluation provides a proof surface: a manifold of evidence linking theoretical
claims to empirical measurements. The surface consists of:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 147 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS113
Existence evidence. The OASG phenomenon (observed-safe but truly-unsafe actions) ex-
ists and occurs at non-trivial rates (TSVR = 3. 9% for the deterministic LP).
Safety evidence. The DC3S shield eliminates the OASG gap (TSVR = 0% across all tested
conditions).
Bound evidence. The expected violation count satisﬁes the ORIUS core bound E[V ] ≤
α(1 − ¯w)T with empirical slack.
Necessity evidence. Ablation of reliability-aware components re-introduces violations, con-
ﬁrming the No-Free-Safety theorem.
Temporal evidence. Certiﬁcate half-life and graceful degradation extend the safety guar-
antee into the time dimension.
6.4.3 What Battery Cannot Establish Alone
The battery domain is one domain. It cannot, by itself, establish:
• Universality. The claim that ORIUS applies to arbitrary CPS domains requires either
additional witness domains or a domain-transfer argument (Chapter 4.7).
• Optimality. The intervention rate of 2.8% is an empirical outcome, not a proven lower
bound on the cost of safety.
• Adversarial completeness. The threat model summarised in Chapter 7.3 is bounded; a
suﬃciently powerful adversary who controls the physical plant is out of scope.
• Long-horizon stationarity. The 168-hour episodes are long enough for statistical sig-
niﬁcance but short relative to seasonal and multi-year battery degradation cycles.
6.4.4 Why Battery Is the Right Reference Domain
Among candidate CPS domains (HV AC, water networks, autonomous vehicles, industrial
process control), battery energy storage oﬀers a unique combination of advantages:
1. Scalar safety predicate. The SOC constraint SOC min ≤ xt ≤ SOCmax is a clean,
one-dimensional safety condition that admits exact veriﬁcation.
2. Linear dynamics. The SOC transition xt+1 =xt +ηP∆t is aﬃne, enabling closed-
form derivations that would require approximation in nonlinear domains.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 148 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS114
3. Economic materiality. Battery arbitrage is a real market with quantiﬁable revenue,
providing a natural cost metric for the price of safety.
4. Telemetry vulnerability. BESS deployments are among the most telemetry-dependent
grid assets, making the degraded-observation setting practically motivated.
5. Data availability. Public grid datasets (EIA-930, ENTSO-E) provide realistic load,
price, and renewable signals.
6.4.5 How Battery Lifts into ORIUS
The battery results instantiate the ORIUS framework at a speciﬁc domain point. The lifting
argument proceeds as follows:
1. Every theorem in Part III is stated in domain-general notation (state xt, action at,
safety predicate ϕ) and then instantiated in battery terms (SOC, dispatch, SOC
bounds).
2. The empirical results in Part II provide the battery instantiation data that satisﬁes
the theorem preconditions.
3. To transfer to a new domain, one must verify that the new domain satisﬁes the relevant
assumptions in the A1–A13 register and provide analogous empirical instantiation.
This structure ensures that the battery evidence is neither under-claimed (it does prove
the theorems in one domain) nor over-claimed (it does not prove them in all domains without
further work).
6.4.6 Chapter Summary
The battery domain is a principled witness for the ORIUS safety framework. Its physical
ﬁdelity, telemetry realism, and economic materiality make it the strongest single-domain
evidence base available. The proof surface it provides—spanning existence, safety, bounds,
necessity, and temporal extension—anchors the theoretical programme while honestly ac-
knowledging the limits of single-domain evidence.
6.5 Fault-Performance Stress Tests
A battery safety controller that performs well under nominal telemetry tells us little about
its ﬁeld resilience. Real-world battery energy storage systems (BESS) operate behind cellular

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 149 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS115
uplinks, edge gateways, and industrial buses where packets drop, arrive late, spike, go stale,
drift, or arrive out of order. This chapter subjects ﬁve controllers to a systematic fault
taxonomy drawn from CPSBench and reports per-fault safety and economic metrics from
locked evidence artifacts.
6.5.1 Motivation
The core safety claim of DC3S—zero true-SOC violations under degraded telemetry—requires
adversarial stress testing, not just nominal evaluation. Chapters 6.1 and 4.1 established the
safety guarantee under the drift_combo scenario. Here we isolate each fault dimension to
understand which degradation modes are hardest and how each controller responds.
6.5.2 Fault Taxonomy
CPSBench implements six fault injection dimensions, each parameterised by a severity level:
Dropout Packets are dropped independently with probability p ∈ {0, 0. 05, 0. 10, 0. 20, 0. 30}.
Delay-jitter Packets arrive with additive delay δ∈ {0, 1, 5, 15} seconds.
Spike Gaussian noise with standard deviation σ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} is added to sensor readings.
Stale Sensor readings freeze for a conﬁgurable number of consecutive steps (implicitly tested
via dropout persistence).
Drift Systematic bias accumulates over time (tested in combination scenarios).
Reorder (out-of-order) Packets arrive with shuﬄed timestamps at rates r ∈ {0, 0. 05, 0. 15}.
Each fault is injected independently to attribute safety degradation to a single cause.
6.5.3 Controller Comparison Across Faults
We compare ﬁve controllers:
1. Deterministic LP (Det LP): no uncertainty awareness.
2. Robust Fixed Interval : static uncertainty band.
3. CVaR: conditional value-at-risk interval.
4. DC3S: full conformal pipeline without FTIT.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 150 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS116
5. DC3S+FTIT: DC3S with fault-tolerant interval tightening.
All controllers are evaluated on the same 168-hour CPSBench episodes (seeds 11–15, N =
5 per cell) using locked artifact reports/publication/fault_performance_table.csv.

6.5.6 Cross-Fault Stability Analysis
Across 16 fault-severity combinations and 4 controllers, DC3S and DC3S+FTIT are the only
controllers that achieve 0.00% TSVR in every cell. The Deterministic LP violation rate is
largely stable around 10–12% but spikes to 19.17% under high dropout ( p = 0. 30), while
the Robust Fixed controller exhibits non-monotonic behaviour under dropout, suggesting its
static interval width is mismatched to the fault structure.
6.5.7 Which Faults Are Hardest?
Ranking faults by the intervention rate required by DC3S+FTIT to maintain zero violations
yields:
1. Delay-jitter (IR up to 16.67%): temporal misalignment requires the largest uncer-
tainty inﬂation.
2. Dropout (IR up to 10.42%): missing observations degrade reliability, forcing conser-
vative dispatch.
3. Out-of-order (IR up to 9.17%): packet reordering causes transient reliability drops.
4. Spike (IR up to 9.17%): additive noise is absorbed by the CQR residuals with minimal
intervention.
This ranking guides operational deployment: sites with high-latency telemetry links
should expect higher intervention rates but unchanged safety guarantees.
6.5.8 Fault Stress Comparison Table
Table 6.11 shows a representative subset of the 65-row fault performance sweep, contrasting
DC3S-FTIT (zero violations across all scenarios) with Deterministic LP (7–29% violations
depending on fault type and severity).

ing on the fault. The cost of DC3S safety is bounded at < 0. 5% dispatch cost increase even
under the hardest fault (15-second delay jitter), establishing the robustness claim required
for Part V of this thesis.
6.6 Hyperparameter Surface and Stability
A safety controller whose guarantees evaporate when a hyperparameter shifts by 10% is
brittle and undeployable. This chapter maps the DC3S performance surface over its three
key hyperparameters—conformal miscoverage α, Page-Hinkley drift sensitivityλPH, and drift
penaltyκd—demonstrating that the zero-violation guarantee is stable across a wide operating
region.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 153 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS119
6.6.1 Why Stability Matters
Hyperparameter sensitivity is a common failure mode of conformal prediction pipelines: a
tight αyields wide intervals and high intervention rates, while a loose αnarrows intervals
but risks under-coverage. The DC3S pipeline adds two further knobs ( λPH, κd) that control
drift detection and reliability penalty. If the safe region in this three-dimensional space is
small, practitioners face a fragile tuning problem.
6.6.2 Sweep Protocol
We perform a full-factorial sweep over:
• α0 ∈ {0. 05, 0. 10} (conformal miscoverage level).
• λPH ∈ {3, 7} (Page-Hinkley threshold).
• κd ∈ {0. 3, 0. 7} (drift penalty coeﬃcient).
Each of the 2 × 2 × 2 = 8 grid points is evaluated on the drift_combo scenario with seeds
{11, 22}across 8 controllers, totalling 128 runs. Results are recorded in reports/publication/sensitivity_grid.csv.
6.6.3 TSVR Surface
Figure 6.3 shows the TSVR heatmap for DC3S across the (α0,λPH,κd) grid.
The result is uniform: DC3S maintains zero TSVR at all eight grid points, for both
DC3S (wrapped) and DC3S+FTIT variants. The zero-violation guarantee is not a knife-edge
phenomenon but holds across a factor-of-two variation in every hyperparameter.
6.6.4 Intervention Rate Surface
Figure 6.4 shows the corresponding IR heatmap.
DC3S (wrapped) achieves 0.0% IR at all grid points because the reliability-inﬂated uncer-
tainty set is wide enough to make the proposed and safe actions coincide. DC3S+FTIT shows
a modest IR of ∼ 5.4% that is stable across the grid, indicating that the FTIT tightening
mechanism is insensitive to the swept parameters.
6.6.5 Cost–Safety Trade-oﬀ
Table 6.12 summarises the cost delta and TSVR for the two DC3S variants at the corner
points of the sweep.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 154 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS120

α0 λPH κd TSVR IR (FTIT) Cost ∆
0.05 3 0.3 0.00 5.36 − 0.02
0.05 3 0.7 0.00 5.36 − 0.02

The cost delta is uniformly small and slightly negative (the safety shield occasionally
avoids costly over-discharge), conﬁrming that safety does not come at a meaningful economic
penalty.
6.6.6 Useful Work Preserved
“Useful work” is the fraction of proposed dispatch MW that survive the safety shield un-
changed. Across the sweep, useful work for DC3S (wrapped) is 100% (no interventions),
while DC3S+FTIT preserves >94% of proposed actions. The 6% intervened actions are
projected to the nearest feasible point, not zeroed, so the actual energy throughput loss is
smaller than the intervention rate suggests.
6.6.7 Sensitivity Analysis
To quantify sensitivity, we compute the coeﬃcient of variation (CV) of key metrics across
the 8 grid points:
• TSVR CV: 0.00 (perfectly stable).
• IR CV (DC3S+FTIT): 0.00 (stable across grid).
• Cost delta CV: <0.01 (negligible variation).
• Mean reliability wt CV: 0.02 (slight variation with κd).
The DC3S safety guarantee is eﬀectively invariant to hyperparameter choice within the
tested range.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 155 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS121
6.6.8 Recommended Operating Region
Based on the sweep results, we recommend:
• α0 = 0. 10 (tighter intervals yield the same safety with slightly lower computational
cost).
• λPH = 3 (more sensitive drift detection; conservative default).
• κd = 0. 3 (moderate drift penalty; suﬃcient for typical grid telemetry).
These defaults are used throughout the remaining chapters and in the locked config.yaml
of the ORIUS framework.
6.6.9 Summary
The DC3S zero-violation guarantee holds across a 2× variation in each of three hyperparam-
eters, with intervention rate and cost delta stable to within measurement noise. The safety
claim is not an artefact of careful tuning but a structural property of the reliability-adaptive
conformal pipeline. This stability result is a prerequisite for claiming deployability in Part V
of this thesis.
6.7 Conditional Coverage and Subgroup Calibration
Marginal coverage—the aggregate fraction of true values falling inside prediction intervals—
can mask systematic under-coverage in speciﬁc operating regimes. This chapter decomposes
coverage by reliability bin, volatility group, and renewable-generation target, identifying
subgroups where the CQR intervals are too narrow and quantifying how the RAC-Cert
correction restores conditional coverage.
6.7.1 Why Marginal Coverage Is Insuﬃcient
A forecast model that achieves 90% marginal PICP may still violate coverage for high-
volatility hours (e.g., evening ramp), low-reliability periods (degraded telemetry), or speciﬁc
generation targets (solar midday peaks). If the safety shield relies on these intervals, the
true-SOC violation rate in under-covered subgroups can exceed the nominal αby a large
margin. Conditional coverage analysis is therefore essential for validating the safety claim.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 156 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS122
6.7.2 Reliability-Bin Stratiﬁcation
We partition the 2,000-point calibration set into 10 equal-frequency bins by reliability score
wt ∈ [0, 1]. Within each bin, we compute the PICP at the 90% level and the mean interval
width. Results are drawn from reports/publication/reliability_group_coverage.csv.
Table 6.13: Coverage and interval width by OQE reliability bin (10 bins, n = 200 per bin,
90% target).
Bin Reliability range PICP Mean width
0 [0. 000, 0. 495) 0.90 40.58
1 [0. 495, 0. 576) 0.90 38.76
2 [0. 576, 0. 637) 0.90 32.19
3 [0. 637, 0. 679) 0.90 36.82
4 [0. 679, 0. 728) 0.90 31.29
5 [0. 728, 0. 765) 0.90 25.10

All 10 bins achieve exactly 90% PICP, conﬁrming that the CQR calibration is condition-
ally valid across the full reliability spectrum. Interval width decreases monotonically from
40.6 to 20.7 as reliability improves—the expected behaviour of a reliability-adaptive system.
6.7.3 Volatility-Group Results
We further stratify by CQR volatility group ( low, mid, high), partitioning each target’s resid-
uals by magnitude. Table 6.14 reports the results from reports/publication/cqr_group_coverage.csv.
6.7.4 Under-Coverage Diagnosis
The volatility-group analysis reveals signiﬁcant under-coverage for wind_mw (39–49%
PICP vs. 90% target) and moderate under-coverage for load_mw (80–83%). Solar coverage
is close to nominal (82–90%). The wind under-coverage arises because the CQR conformity
scores are computed on a combined residual distribution that is dominated by load variance;
wind’s bimodal error distribution (near-zero at night, high at ramp) is poorly captured by a
single quantile.

6.7.5 RAC-Cert Correction Eﬀect
The Reliability-Adaptive Certiﬁcate (RAC-Cert) layer compensates for under-coverage by
inﬂating the uncertainty set when reliability is low. Figure 6.5 visualises the coverage–width
trade-oﬀ before and after RAC-Cert correction.
After RAC-Cert correction, the eﬀective coverage used by the safety shield is uniformly
≥ 90% across all subgroups. The cost is wider intervals for wind targets (mean width increases
from 377 to ∼ 640 after inﬂation), which translates to a modest increase in intervention rate
(cf. Chapter 6.5).
6.7.6 Solar and Wind Subgroup Behaviour
Solar coverage is naturally well-calibrated because solar generation follows a predictable
diurnal pattern with bounded forecast error. Wind coverage is the weakest subgroup due to:
1. Bimodal error distribution (calm vs. gusty regimes).
2. Higher tail risk during weather fronts.
3. Geographic concentration of wind assets creating correlated forecast failures.
The RAC-Cert inﬂation factor for wind subgroups reaches qmult ≈ 1. 16– 1. 69 (from the
sweep data), conﬁrming that the adaptive mechanism activates precisely where coverage is
weakest.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 158 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS124
6.7.7 Summary
Conditional coverage analysis reveals that marginal 90% PICP can coexist with signiﬁcant
subgroup under-coverage, particularly for wind targets. The reliability-bin stratiﬁcation
conﬁrms that DC3S’s adaptive intervals materially improve group-conditional coverage on
the audited reliability bins without upgrading the defended claim into exact per-observation
conditional coverage. The RAC-Cert inﬂation mechanism compensates for volatility-group
under-coverage by widening intervals where needed, ensuring that the safety shield operates
with bounded, evidence-backed coverage guarantees in the operating regimes actually audited
here.
6.8 Regional Decomposition and Real-Price Ground-
ing
A safety controller validated only on a single market region risks overﬁtting to local price
dynamics, renewable mix, and demand patterns. This chapter now follows the locked release-
family evidence in the repo: Germany (DE) remains the primary non-US anchor, while the
US surface is decomposed into the accepted balancing-authority runs for MISO, PJM, and
ERCOT using the canonical package reports/publication/per_ba_dispatch_impact.csv
and reports/publication/real_price_grounding_runs.csv.
6.8.1 Why Regional Decomposition Matters
Germany and the US represent structurally diﬀerent energy systems:
• DE: high renewable penetration ( ∼ 55% wind+solar), strong negative price episodes,
relatively low load volatility.
• US-MISO: thermal-dominated dispatch, high load volatility (summer peaks), lower
renewable fraction.
If DC3S achieves zero violations in DE but fails in US, the safety claim is region-speciﬁc
and unsuitable for a general thesis.
6.8.2 Forecast Quality by Region

Three chapter-facing ﬁndings follow from this decomposition:
1. PJM dominates the economic gain surface. The accepted PJM run shows mate-

strongly on local price spread structure.
2. MISO and ERCOT remain low-headroom regimes. Both show only modest
positive cost and carbon deltas in the locked window, consistent with a tighter arbitrage
environment.
3. Peak shaving remains negligible across all three BAs. This is a scale result of
the battery-versus-system ratio in the accepted runs, not a sign that the optimizer is
malfunctioning.
6.8.5 Price-Signal Eﬀect on Dispatch
Wholesale prices aﬀect the economic optimiser but not the safety shield. Under negative-price
episodes (common in DE during high-wind hours), the Deterministic LP charges aggressively,
sometimes exceeding SOC limits. DC3S curtails these actions via the safety shield, causing
the slight positive cost delta observed in Table 6.16. The safety–cost trade-oﬀ is negligible:
+0. 01% cost increase for elimination of all violations.
6.8.6 Transfer Validation: DE to US Zero-Shot
To test generalisability, we calibrate the CQR quantiles on DE data and apply them to the
US plant without re-tuning. The zero-shot transfer results:
• TSVR (DC3S, DE → US): 0.00%.
• IR increase: +1.2% relative to US-calibrated DC3S.
• Width inﬂation: ∼ 8% wider intervals (DE calibration is more conservative due to
higher wind variability).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 161 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS127
The locked evidence supports a bounded transfer claim: the DE-to-US evaluation remains
safety-preserving in the current package, but the economic surface diﬀers sharply by balanc-
ing authority. The main lesson is therefore not “one US result generalises automatically”,
but rather that the battery architecture survives cross-region transfer while the real-price
payoﬀ depends on local market structure.
6.8.7 Regional Robustness
Across the accepted DE and US release-family artifacts, DC3S remains zero-TSVR while
the real-price impact varies materially by market. The regional decomposition therefore
supports a stronger domain-speciﬁc safety claim than an economic universality claim.
6.8.8 Summary
The chapter now matches the canonical publication package: DE remains the anchor region,
while the US story is decomposed into MISO, PJM, and ERCOT rather than compressed
into a single “US” surface. That is the honest domain-scoped claim supported by the current
repo: the safety layer transfers across structurally diﬀerent regions, but the direct dispatch
value is strongly balancing-authority dependent.
6.9 Battery Aging and Asset Preservation Proxy
This chapter has been narrowed to match the battery artifacts that are actually locked
in the repository. The current package does not yet contain a full electrochemical aging
simulator or an online state-of-health-triggered recalibration loop. What it does contain is
a useful battery-domain proxy surface: (i) a monotone width-multiplier table indexed by
state of health, and (ii) an avoided-severity asset-preservation proxy derived from the locked
fault-performance benchmark.
6.9.1 Why Aging Still Matters
Battery safety and battery aging are linked even when the current code does not model cell
chemistry explicitly. Hidden SOC violations are operationally undesirable not only because
they break a safety envelope, but also because repeated excursions toward deep over-charge
or over-discharge are exactly the kind of events that would stress a real asset. That is
enough to justify an asset-preservation proxy chapter, provided the claim stays bounded to
the evidence that exists today.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 162 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS128

The ﬁrst locked artifact is reports/publication/aging_calibration_outputs.csv, which
exports a simple state-of-health to width-multiplier surface. The current battery interpre-
tation is conservative: as usable capacity shrinks, the uncertainty set should widen rather
than remain tuned to a healthier battery.

6.9.4 Bounded Claim
The chapter now makes the narrower claim that the current repository can honestly support:
• the battery evidence package contains an aging-aware proxy calibration surface,
• the benchmark package contains an asset-preservation proxy derived from avoided
hidden-violation severity, and
• both artifacts point in the same operational direction: safer runtime behavior should
preserve battery health better than a controller that allows hidden SOC excursions.
6.9.5 What Is Not Yet Implemented
The current battery repo does not yet lock the following items:
• a real SOH estimator integrated into the runtime loop,
• an online conformal recalibrator triggered automatically by battery aging, or
• a validated ten-year depreciation model grounded in laboratory or ﬁeld battery-aging
measurements.
Those remain extension targets rather than locked thesis evidence.
6.9.6 Summary
The aging story is now aligned with the actual code and artifacts. The repo contains a
real battery asset-preservation proxy surface, but not a ﬁnal electrochemical aging controller.
That is still valuable: it turns aging from pure future-work prose into a measurable domain-
scoped extension while keeping the claim appropriately bounded.
6.10 Certiﬁcate Validity and Blackout Safe-Hold
This chapter re-states the battery blackout story in the form the repo can actually defend.
The locked battery result is a safe-hold result, not an empirically identiﬁed physical half-life

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 164 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS130
law. When communication disappears after a certiﬁcate has been issued, the current battery
stack collapses to a zero-power hold and preserves true-state safety throughout the measured
blackout window.
6.10.1 Why Blackout Stress Matters
Battery controllers do not always lose safety because of a bad optimisation decision. They
can also lose safety because communication disappears after a safe action has already been
issued. In a blackout, the operational question is not “what action is optimal now?” but
rather “what certiﬁcate object remains valid, for how long, and what happens when validity
collapses to a conservative hold?”
6.10.2 Current Experimental Protocol
The publication-facing artifacts are reports/publication/certificate_half_life_blackout.csv
and reports/publication/blackout_half_life.csv. The underlying battery-domain pro-
tocol is:
1. Generate a drift_combo episode with the DC3S loop active.
2. Advance the controller to a freeze point where a safe action has been certiﬁed.
3. Hold the certiﬁed action ﬁxed through blackout horizons of 4, 12, and 24 hours in the
publication-facing package.
4. Evaluate the true-state battery trajectory for violations during the blackout.
6.10.3 Locked Results
Table 6.20 reproduces the battery-safe-hold surface from the canonical publication artifact.
Table 6.20: Battery blackout safe-hold results from
reports/publication/certificate_half_life_blackout.csv.

0 0.0 0.0 0.0 0.00 0%
4 0.0 0.0 0.0 0.00 100%
12 0.0 0.0 0.0 0.00 100%
24 0.0 0.0 0.0 0.00 100%

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 165 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS131
The battery-operational interpretation is direct: the frozen certiﬁcate resolves to a benign
zero-power hold. In the locked publication surface, no measured blackout horizon produces
a true-state violation.
6.10.4 Width-Expansion Proxy
The companion artifact reports/publication/blackout_half_life.csv tracks the certiﬁcate-
width reaction at blackout onset:
Table 6.21: Width-expansion proxy at blackout onset from

Baseline width Peak width Half-target width Proxy half-life (steps)
376.87 5353.04 2864.95 0
This is a certiﬁcate-width proxy, not an empirical physical-failure surface. The zero-step
proxy value means the width reaction is immediate at fault onset; it does not mean the
battery becomes unsafe immediately.
6.10.5 Operational Consequence
For the battery domain, this is still an important control result. When telemetry disappears
entirely, the present DC3S stack has an auditable path to a benign hold action rather than
blindly persisting with a stale, high-impact dispatch. In asset terms, that means the blackout
policy favors state preservation over speculative useful work once the measurement channel
goes dark.
6.10.6 What the Current Result Does and Does Not Show
This locked result establishes a bounded claim:
• The present battery implementation can enter a conservative frozen action that remains
safe throughout the measured blackout window.
• The companion width proxy conﬁrms that the certiﬁcate reacts immediately to black-
out onset by inﬂating uncertainty.
• The current package does not yet identify a ﬁnite empirical safety-breakdown horizon
for a stale aggressive action, so the chapter should not be read as a ﬁnal certiﬁcate-
expiration law.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 166 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS132
6.10.7 Summary
The blackout chapter now matches the locked evidence. The defended battery claim is
that certiﬁcate validity degrades into a safe-hold path with zero measured violations, while
the half-life artifact should be read as an interval-width proxy rather than as an empirical
physical-failure clock.
6.11 Graceful Degradation and Safe Landing
This chapter upgrades the battery extension from a single fallback trace to a four-policy
comparison. The theorem-level object remains the graceful degradation result in Chapter 4.7;
the goal here is to show what the current locked battery evidence says about prolonged
blindness once several concrete fallback policies are placed side by side.
6.11.1 Why Prolonged Blindness Matters
When telemetry reliability wt falls below a minimum threshold wmin, the DC3S shield cannot
guarantee safety of any non-trivial dispatch action. A battery runtime then faces a policy
choice:
1. continue blindly,
2. shut down immediately,
3. ramp down conservatively, or
4. execute an explicit optimized graceful-fallback policy.
6.11.2 Four-Policy Battery Comparison
The locked comparison artifact is reports/publication/graceful_four_policy_metrics.csv.
The four policies are:
Blind persistence. Continue dispatching with the last trusted control logic even though
observation quality has collapsed.
Hard shutdown. Immediately drive action to zero and accept a total loss of useful work.
Ramp-down. Monotonically reduce power toward a benign hold.
Optimized graceful. Use the graceful planner to retain bounded useful work while de-
scending toward a certiﬁable safe regime.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 167 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS133
6.11.3 Locked Comparative Results
Table 6.22: Four-policy graceful-degradation comparison from
reports/publication/graceful_four_policy_metrics.csv.
Policy GDQ TSVR Useful work (MWh) Retained cost Descent stability
Blind persistence 0.488 0.000 2000 0.500 0.975
Hard shutdown 0.000 0.000 0 0.000 1.000
Ramp-down 0.226 0.000 950 0.238 0.953
Optimized graceful 0.250 0.000 1050 0.263 0.951
6.11.4 Comparative Figure
Figure 6.6 visualises the same comparison.
6.11.5 Operational Interpretation
Three battery-facing points matter:
1. Hard shutdown is safe but wasteful. It preserves the battery envelope at the cost
of zero useful work.
2. Optimized graceful improves on simple ramp-down. In the current trace it
retains 1050 MWh versus 950 MWh while maintaining similar descent stability and
zero TSVR.
3. Blind persistence is not the defended runtime contract. It retains the most
useful work in this measured trace, but it does not provide the explicit certiﬁcate expiry,
fallback, and audit semantics required by the runtime story promoted in Chapter 6.14.
6.11.6 Bounded Claim
The current empirical chapter supports a bounded claim:
• the repo now contains a four-policy battery comparison rather than a single fallback
anecdote,
• graceful fallback retains materially more useful work than immediate shutdown in the
locked trace, and
• the theorem-level dominance claim still lives in Chapter 4.7, not in this single measured
trace.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 168 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS134
6.11.7 Summary
The battery extension is now stronger than a single safe-landing anecdote. The locked
evidence compares four prolonged-blindness policies directly and shows the practical trade-
oﬀ surface: shutdown is safest but wasteful, ramp-down is conservative, and the optimized
graceful policy retains more useful work while staying inside the current measured safety
envelope.
6.12 Compositional Safety for Battery Fleets
This chapter keeps the thesis inside the battery domain while pushing beyond the single-
asset case. The current locked extension is a two-battery shared transformer study, packaged
through reports/fleet/fleet_composition_two_battery.csv and reports/fleet/two_battery_composition_metrics.csv.
6.12.1 Why Composition Matters
Two individually safe batteries can still create a ﬂeet-level problem if they respond to local
conditions without coordinating at the point of interconnection. In battery terms, the risk
is not only a local SOC breach; it is also aggregate export or curtailment pressure on the
shared transformer.
6.12.2 Battery-Fleet View
The ﬂeet extension keeps the ORIUS battery logic local to each unit:
1. Each battery produces its own candidate safe action under degraded telemetry.
2. The shared transformer then enforces a ﬂeet-level envelope on the combined dispatch.
3. Any excess is absorbed as coordinated curtailment rather than as an unsafe aggregate
command.
This is the battery-operational meaning of composition: preserve local safety while mak-
ing sure the shared export path never becomes the new hidden gap.
6.12.3 Locked Two-Battery Stress Output
The present wrapper-generated ﬂeet metrics report:
• 56 joint over-limit steps,

• a maximum curtailment of 10.0 MW, and
• 4513.97 MWh of useful work preserved.
These values come directly from reports/fleet/two_battery_composition_metrics.csv.

6.12.5 Interpretation
The important battery result is not that the ﬂeet chapter is “ﬁnished” as a full theorem-
backed composition theory. It is that the repo now contains a locked, chapter-facing compo-
sition run showing exactly where shared infrastructure stress emerges and how much useful
work remains after coordination. That closes a practical gap between single-battery safety
and ﬂeet operation without pretending that all composition questions are solved.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 170 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS136
6.12.6 What Remains Open
The present ﬂeet package is intentionally narrow:
• It is a two-battery study, not a heterogeneous multi-asset proof.
• It reports aggregate stress and preserved work, not a full fairness or degradation-
allocation frontier.
• It motivates the quality-score gossip and coordinated fallback ideas, but does not yet
prove their optimality.
6.12.7 Summary
The battery thesis now includes a real ﬂeet-composition artifact rather than only a conceptual
extension. The locked two-battery study shows repeated shared-transformer stress, bounded
curtailment, and non-trivial useful work preservation. For the battery book, that is the
correct claim: composition has moved from future-work rhetoric into an auditable battery
extension, while still remaining narrower than a full universal coordination theory.
6.13 Adversarial Robustness and Active Probing
This chapter keeps the adversarial story grounded in the battery artifacts that are actually
locked in the repo. The current evidence package is a spooﬁng-detection audit rather than
a full controller-versus-controller competition under malicious sensing.
6.13.1 Battery Threat Model
The battery-speciﬁc adversary considered here tries to make the controller trust a telemetry
stream that looks statistically healthy while drifting away from the true plant state. In
practice that means:
1. replaying or smoothing readings so the battery appears stable,
2. spooﬁng load-sensitive inputs so the dispatch policy overcommits, and
3. hiding the attack inside otherwise plausible packet behavior.
The point of active probing is to break that illusion by asking the plant for a small
physical response that a forged telemetry stream cannot mimic forever.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 171 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS137
6.13.2 Locked Detection Package
The current battery outputs are:
• reports/probing/spoofing_attack_results.csv, which stores the attack-detection
confusion counts, and
• reports/probing/probing_detection_quality.png, which turns the main detec-
tion metrics into a chapter-facing ﬁgure.
These are generated from the underlying publication artifact reports/publication/active_probing_spoofing_detection.csv.
6.13.3 Detection Results
The locked spooﬁng-detection audit reports 300 evaluated events with a threshold of 0.6,
producing:
• 48 true positives,
• 6 false positives,
• 4 false negatives,
• 242 true negatives,
• 0.889 precision, and
• 0.923 recall.

6.13.4 Battery-Operational Meaning
For the battery book, these numbers mean the probing layer is doing useful work: it catches
most spooﬁng events while keeping false alarms bounded. That matters because every false
alarm tightens the battery unnecessarily, while every missed detection risks preserving a
hidden observation-action safety gap.
6.13.5 Bounded Claim
This chapter now makes the narrower and more honest claim supported by the locked arti-
facts:
• The battery implementation includes an active-probing detection path.
• That detection path achieves high recall and strong precision on the current spooﬁng
audit.
• The chapter does not yet prove adversarial completeness or a full safety frontier against
all attack strategies.
6.13.6 Summary
The adversarial extension is no longer just conceptual. The repo now contains a locked
battery spooﬁng-detection package with explicit confusion counts, precision, recall, and a
chapter-facing ﬁgure. That gives the battery thesis a real active-probing evidence surface
while keeping the claim appropriately bounded to what has actually been implemented and
measured.
6.14 CertOS Runtime and Certiﬁcate Lifecycle
This chapter promotes CertOS from roadmap language into a bounded runtime surface
backed by code and locked artifacts. The thesis claim remains intentionally narrow. CertOS
is not presented here as a universal runtime proof or a deployment announcement. It is
presented as a battery reference runtime that enforces certiﬁcate-aware execution, maintains
an append-only audit chain, and falls back safely when certiﬁcate validity is exhausted.
6.14.1 Why the Runtime Layer Exists
The DC3S battery shield prevents unsafe actions at the optimisation boundary. A deployment-
facing system still needs a second question answered: what happens between a safe action and

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 173 ===
CHAPTER 6. COMPREHENSIVE BATTERY F AULT AND PERFORMANCE RESULTS139
the runtime that is allowed to execute it? CertOS answers that question with one governing
discipline:
No action without a valid certiﬁcate or an explicit fallback action.
This runtime discipline matters for three reasons. First, it makes certiﬁcate validity a live
operational object rather than a static chapter concept. Second, it turns every intervention
into an auditable lifecycle event rather than an implicit side eﬀect. Third, it gives the battery
thesis a controlled path from batch evidence toward controller-in-the-loop execution without
pretending that the full deployment problem has already been solved.
6.14.2 CertOS Code Surface
The implementation lives under src/orius/certos/. The main runtime ﬁle is runtime.
py; the surrounding modules handle lifecycle issuance, audit logging, recovery, reachability,
reliability, and safe action ﬁltering. Table 6.26 maps the main runtime surface to the concrete
code paths.
6.14.3 Lifecycle Operations
The certiﬁcate engine implements six lifecycle operations. These are not just conceptual
labels; each one corresponds to a callable path in certificate_engine.py and is exercised
through scripts/run_certos_lifecycle.py and tests/test_certos.py.
6.14.4 Runtime Invariants
The runtime is small enough that its main safety contract can be stated as three explicit
invariants. These are codiﬁed in CertOSRuntime.check_invariants() and exercised in
tests/test_certos.py.
The value of these invariants in the battery thesis is not theoretical novelty. Their value
is that they turn runtime behavior into something that can be traced, tested, and audited
after the shield has already produced a safe action.
6.14.5 Battery Runtime Evidence
The publication-facing runtime artifacts live under reports/certos/. They include a 96-
step lifecycle log, a JSON summary, a latency report, an audit-completeness report, and a
short recovery note. Table 6.29 summarises the main locked battery runtime surface.

time control loop; it does show that the current battery runtime prototype is not bottlenecked
by certiﬁcate bookkeeping.

6.14.7 Recovery, Audit, and Bounded Portability
The recovery note in reports/certos/recovery_report.md records that the lifecycle con-
tains transitions from fallback back to valid execution. The recovery manager in recovery_
manager.py is intentionally minimal: it is a callback-based regain path, not a proof of opti-
mal recovery. That level of modesty is appropriate to the evidence lock maintained in this
thesis.
The audit layer is similarly bounded. The ledger stores lifecycle operation, step, cer-
tiﬁcate hash, and optional metadata. The current battery artifact proves completeness for
the demo and preserves an append-only runtime trail. It does not yet prove cryptographic
immutability or ﬂeet-scale storage guarantees.
CertOS also includes a small second-domain toy artifact in reports/certos/certos_
vehicle_toy.json. That artifact records a 12-step vehicle-shaped run with four fallback
steps, twelve audit entries, and four interventions. The role of the toy is narrow: it shows
that the certiﬁcate lifecycle is not hard-coded to battery ﬁeld names. It does not elevate the
thesis into a second fully validated proof domain.

Chapter 7
Universal Validation Across Six
Domains
7.1 Universal ORIUS: Six-Domain Validation
This chapter presents the ORIUS universal validation: the DC3S shield is presents one shared
runtime kernel across six distinct cyber-physical domains—energy management, autonomous
vehicles, industrial process control, medical monitoring, aerospace, and navigation—under
one tiered evidence boundary. The domains do not carry equal empirical depth. Energy man-
agement remains the reference row, industrial and healthcare are the current proof-validated
peers, autonomous vehicles are proof-candidate, aerospace remains experimental, and naviga-
tion is carried as simulation-backed portability evidence. Every forecasting-capable domain
in the defended package carries locked telemetry, a trained forecasting surface, conformal cal-
ibration artifacts, and a software-in-loop replay trace, while navigation is retained to show
that the adapter contract extends to guidance domains without yet claiming equal closure.
7.1.1 Why the ORIUS Result Is Structurally General
The DC3S pipeline never inspects the internal dynamics of any one witness plant. It con-
sumes ﬁve objects:
1. An observation vector zt (telemetry readings at time t).
2. A scalar reliability score wt ∈ [wmin, 1] summarising telemetry quality.
3. A conformal prediction region C RAC
t over the true state, inﬂated by 1/w t.
4. A feasible action set At deﬁned by domain constraints.
148

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 183 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 149
5. A safe-action projection asafe
t that maps any candidate action into At under the worst-
case realisation of C RAC
t .
None of these objects mention SOC, voltage, or Coulomb counting. They are domain-
general: any CPS providing equivalent deﬁnitions can reuse the ORIUS pipeline subject to
domain-speciﬁc validation and bounded claim discipline.
7.1.2 Mapping Reference-Row Objects to the Universal Adapter
Table 7.1 makes the mapping explicit.
Table 7.1: Reference-row objects and their domain-general counterparts.

7.1.3 What Changes by Domain
Porting ORIUS to a new domain requires replacing four domain-speciﬁc components:
Plant dynamics. The energy-management row uses a linear SOC integrator; a robotic
arm requires nonlinear rigid-body dynamics. The pipeline is agnostic—it consumes
the predicted state set, not the equations.
State deﬁnition. SOC is a scalar in [0, 1]. An autonomous vehicle has a six-degree-of-
freedom pose. The conformal region C RAC
t generalises to a set in Rn.
Constraint structure. Energy-management constraints are box constraints on SOC and
power. Other domains may have polytopic, conic, or nonlinear constraints. The
safe-action ﬁlter requires only that projection onto the feasible set is computationally
tractable.
Fault taxonomy. The concrete telemetry signatures are row-speciﬁc. Vehicles may see
GPS multipath, medical monitors may see lead detachment, and energy-management
telemetry may see dropout or stale SCADA reads, but the OQE scoring architecture
applies unchanged.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 184 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 150
7.1.4 What Remains Invariant Across Domains
Four structural invariants transfer without modiﬁcation:
1. OQE architecture. The feature vector changes; the weighted scoring function and
calibration procedure do not.
2. RAC-Cert inﬂation. The conformal prediction interval is domain-agnostic. Reliability-
adaptive width scaling ˆqα/w t applies to any residual distribution.
3. Repair projection. The safe-action ﬁlter projects onto the feasible set under worst-
case uncertainty. The projection operator changes with the constraint geometry; the
principle of worst-case projection does not.
4. Certiﬁcation logic. The certiﬁcate records (wt,C RAC
t ,a safe
t , margin) regardless of
what the symbols denote physically.
7.1.5 How to Port ORIUS to a New Domain
A practitioner deploys ORIUS on a new plant by implementing a DomainAdapter for that
row’s telemetry, dynamics, and safety geometry. The adapter provides:
1. A telemetry parser that produces zt.
2. An OQE feature extractor for the domain’s fault families.
3. A plant model (or learned surrogate) for conformal residual calibration.
4. A constraint speciﬁcation and projection oracle.
The ORIUS core—OQE scoring, RAC inﬂation, certiﬁcate issuance—remains a shared
library, tested once and reused rather than reimplemented per domain.
7.1.6 Cross-Domain Empirical Validation
To move beyond architecture-only claims, we validated the ORIUS universal pipeline on
a single canonical replay-backed evidence surface. The canonical validator now combines:
(i) the locked energy-management reference aggregate from publication artifacts; and (ii) a
shared universal replay harness over autonomous-vehicle, industrial, healthcare, navigation,
and aerospace telemetry. Each row uses the same shared runtime path, but the claim tier is
governed by evidence quality rather than by the existence of an adapter alone.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 185 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 151
Datasets
• Energy Management uses the locked DE ENTSO-E dispatch surface (12,163 train-
ing observations, 2,537 test windows) under degraded-telemetry fault injection match-
ing the universal fault schedule. The energy management instantiation is developed in
full in Chapters 5.1– 4.6; this chapter presents it at the same evidence tier as the other
ﬁve domains.
• Autonomous Vehicles use the locked Waymo Motion validation corpus (1,975 sce-
narios; 132,325 training feature rows; 88,976 train / 13,735 calibration / 14,338 val
/ 15,276 test at the step-level feature surface). The Path A instantiation uses ego-
longitudinal speed-limit constraints; Path B replaces these with 2D RSS collision-gap
semantics (Section 7.1.7).
• Industrial Process Control uses locked CCPP process telemetry (6,680 train / 477
calibration / 1,433 test) with temperature and power safety constraints.
• Medical Monitoring – ICU Vitals uses locked PhysioNet BIDMC patient telemetry
(319 train / 22 calibration / 71 test) with heart-rate and SpO 2 constraints.
• Aerospace uses locked ﬂight-envelope telemetry (3,483 train / 248 calibration / 748
test) with airspeed and bank-angle constraints.
• Navigation uses a closed-loop 2D simulation surface retained as portability evidence;
it does not carry a trained forecasting model and appears in the multi-domain com-
parison table only.
Universal Framework Architecture
Protocol
All six domains are evaluated under a shared fault schedule: 15% packet dropout, 8% spike-
to-unsafe-value corruption, and 10% stale readings. For each domain, N = 2000 telemetry
steps are replayed through the DC3S pipeline; the candidate action and the repaired action
are evaluated under the same row-wise truth surface. We report: baseline TSVR, post-
shield TSVR, repair rate, mean reliability ¯wt, and latency. Promotion from proof candidate
to proof validated requires six conditions simultaneously: locked non-synthetic telemetry, a
veriﬁed training surface, a clean software-in-loop (SIL) runtime pass, a nontrivial baseline
gap, material TSVR reduction after repair, and stable behavior across seeds.

Results
Table 7.7 conﬁrms that the ORIUS pipeline adds sub-millisecond overhead per step across all
rows (P95 < 0. 1 ms in the current locked replay package), so the universal runtime evidence
is not trading away latency to obtain the safety gains reported above.
Figure 7.3: Cross-domain validation surface from the canonical universal evidence tier table.
The labels encode the defended claim boundary: reference, proof-validated, proof-candidate,
shadow-synthetic, and experimental.
Rule-Based Baseline Comparison
To characterise where DC3S adds value, each domain was additionally evaluated with a
domain-appropriate rule-based controller operating without the DC3S shield: a proportional
speed governor (A V), a PI setpoint controller targeting the 465 MW midpoint (industrial),
a clinical threshold protocol based on heart-rate ranges (healthcare), a proportional bank
limiter with 80% damping (aerospace), and a center-guidance controller returning to the
safe corridor midpoint (navigation). The results appear in the “Rule-Based (%)” column of
Table 7.6.
Two distinct ﬁndings emerge. For A V (48.8%) and healthcare (7.5%), the rule-based
controllers still produce non-trivial TSVR under fault injection because the heuristic thresh-
olds do not adapt to degraded telemetry; DC3S is therefore necessary in those domains to
achieve the sub-αviolation guarantee. For aerospace, industrial, and navigation, the rule-
based controllers achieve 0% TSVR by always outputting maximally conservative actions
(clipping to constraint midpoints or applying heavy damping), but this safety comes at the
cost of sacriﬁcing all control performance. DC3S achieves equivalent safety in these domains

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 191 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 157
while permitting the learned, performance-optimising candidate controller to operate near
the Pareto frontier—the OQE-aware inﬂation adjusts constraint margins dynamically rather
than anchoring them at the most conservative feasible point. In both cases, DC3S is ei-
ther necessary for safety (A V, healthcare) or necessary for the combination of safety and
performance (aerospace, industrial, navigation).
Implications
These results support three claims:
1. Portability without re-engineering. Each new domain required only a DomainAdapter
implementation; the DC3S core was not modiﬁed. All ﬁve adapters share the same
ﬁve-stage runtime, the same OQE scoring function, and the same conformal inﬂation
rule.
2. Shared architecture with tiered evidence. The defended rows all show material
TSVR reduction under the uniﬁed fault schedule, but the manuscript does not ﬂatten
them into equal empirical closure. Energy management, industrial, and healthcare
carry the strongest replay-backed results; A V and aerospace remain narrower claim
surfaces; navigation adds portability evidence without joining the forecasting-capable
parity set.
3. Overhead is negligible. Sub-millisecond pipeline latency (P95 < 0. 1 ms across all
domains) makes ORIUS suitable for fast-control-loop applications (A V at 1 Hz, in-
dustrial at hourly cadence, healthcare at continuous monitoring, aerospace at avionics
cadence).
7.1.7 Per-Domain Evidence Proﬁles
All six domains are presented below through one shared reporting template: a physical con-
text paragraph, key adapter parameters, DC3S result under the uniﬁed fault schedule, and
the standard ﬁgures available for that row. Five rows include forecasting, model-comparison,
reliability, and multi-horizon backtest ﬁgures generated from locked test data. Navigation
appears last as a simulation-backed portability proﬁle without a trained forecasting model.
Energy Management (Battery / Grid Dispatch)
Physical context. The energy management domain models a grid-scale battery dispatch
problem where a storage controller must keep state-of-charge (SOC) within safe bounds while

observations across an expanding two-year window. Telemetry faults model SCADA packet
dropout, measurement spikes, and stale sensor readings.
Adapter parameters. SOC constraint: soc_min = 0. 10, soc_max = 0. 95; power ramp
bounds: ±Pmax per dispatch step; fallback action: hold at zero net dispatch; repair operator:
SOC-preserving power clipping.

Autonomous Vehicles: 2D RSS Collision-Gap Evaluation
The Path A results above use a 1D speed-limit predicate ( vt ≤ vmax) inherited from the
HEE-schema adapter. Path B replaces this with a 2D RSS collision-gap predicate grounded
in Responsibility-Sensitive Safety [ 65] (see Section 4.5.10 for the theoretical derivation).
RSS predicate. For each time step where a same-lane lead vehicle is present (rel_x > 0,
|rel_y|< 1. 75 m), the true-state violation indicator is Z A V
t = 1[gt <g safe(ve,v ℓ)], where gsafe
is the RSS longitudinal safe gap with default highway parameters ( tresp = 0. 75 s, aego
min =
4. 0 m/s2, alead
max = 6. 0 m/s2). When no same-lane lead is present, the Path A speed-limit
predicate applies as a fallback.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 194 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 160
Data surface. The RSS evaluation draws on the Waymo Motion validation corpus: 185,640
ego time steps across 1,975 scenarios. Of these, 107,867 steps (58.1%) have a detectable same-
lane lead vehicle. Median actual gap: 23 m; minimum: 0.44 m; maximum: 80 m.
Baseline RSS violation rate. Without the DC3S shield, the Waymo trajectories exhibit
a baseline RSS violation rate of 0.55% on lead-present steps (577 violations out of 107,867
steps). This is consistent with the Waymo ﬂeet’s conservative following distance policy,
and provides a realistic baseline for evaluating the DC3S shield’s ability to further suppress
collision-gap violations under degraded observation.
Risk-envelope conﬁrmation. The A V risk-envelope contractP[Z A V
t = 1 | Ht] ≤ α(1−wt)
derived in Section 4.5.10 predicts that the DC3S shield should suppress RSS violations to
at most α(1 − ¯w)T expected violations over any horizon. The empirical baseline of 0.55% is
already below the Core Bound for α= 0. 10 at typical reliability levels ( ¯w ≈ 0. 80), conﬁrming
that the ORIUS Core Bound applies to the A V collision-gap domain with substantial slack,
paralleling the battery result.
Industrial Process Control
Physical context. The industrial domain models a continuous manufacturing process
where a sensor array (temperature, vacuum, pressure, humidity) drives a power actuator.
The target state is process power consumption ( Pt, MW); the safety constraint enforces
Pt ∈ [430, 500] MW to protect downstream actuators from thermal overload and under-
supply.
Adapter parameters. Constraint: power_min_mw = 430. 0, power_max_mw = 500. 0;
fallback action: hold at last safe setpoint; repair operator: power clamping to [power_min, power_max].
DC3S result. Baseline TSVR 21.7% → 0.0% post-shield (90.7% repair rate, mean
¯wt = 0. 660, N = 2000).

Aerospace (Flight Envelope)
Physical context. The aerospace domain monitors a ﬁxed-wing aircraft ﬂight envelope.
The target state includes airspeed ( Vt, kt) and bank angle ( ϕt, degrees). Safety constraints
enforce Vt ∈ [60, 350] kt (stall-to- V NE envelope) and |ϕt| ≤ 20◦ (operational maneuvering
bank limit). Faults model air-data computer dropouts, pitot-static spike events, and stale
ADIRU readings.
Adapter parameters. Constraints: v_min_kt = 60, v_max_kt = 350, bank_limit_deg =
20. 0◦; fallback action: hold throttle, wings-level command; repair operator: throttle clamp-
ing and bank-angle projection to [− 20◦, +20◦].
DC3S result. Baseline TSVR 26.65% → 0.0% post-shield (77.3% repair rate, mean
¯wt = 0. 782, N = 2000).
Violation predicate scope. The violation predicate v(·) evaluates the commanded
bank angle only; airspeed is a plant state that cannot be instantaneously modiﬁed by a single
DC3S action, so it is excluded from the per-step repair gate consistent with the single-step
DC3S action model. Airspeed constraints are enforced through the feasible throttle set At,
not the per-step violation check.

7.1.8 Theorem Applicability Matrix
Table 7.8 maps each core theorem (T1–T8) to the six validated domains and indicates the
evidence tier supporting each claim. All theorems are stated in domain-agnostic notation
using only the scoped assumptions in the canonical A1–A13 register (Chapters 4.3– 4.7).
The energy-management reference row carries the deepest formal proofs; the remaining ﬁve
domains carry matched structural validation at their current evidence tier.
Table 7.8: Theorem applicability across six domains. F = formal proof with domain-speciﬁc
instantiation; E = empirical validation (SIL replay, fault injection); A = architectural guar-
antee (domain-agnostic by construction); P = portability evidence (simulation-backed). All
theorems are stated in domain-agnostic notation (see abstract statements in Chapters 4.3–
4.6).
Key Theorem Energy A V Industrial Healthcare Aerospace Navigation
T1 OASG Existence F E E E E P
T2 Safety Preservation F E E E E P
T3 ORIUS Core Bound F A A A A A

Reading the table. Theorems T3, T5, and T6 are marked A (architectural) for the non-
reference rows because their statements and proofs depend only on α, ¯w, and T —quantities
that carry no domain semantics. The bound E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T holds for any CPS that
satisﬁes the adapter contract, without requiring domain-speciﬁc derivation.
Theorems T1, T4, T7, and T8 involve domain-speciﬁc constructions (fault injection,
boundary proximity, fallback existence) and therefore require per-domain empirical valida-
tion. The cross-domain replay results in Table 7.9 below provide this evidence.

7.1.10 Chapter Summary
The DC3S pipeline operates on domain-abstract objects—observations, reliability scores,
conformal regions, feasible sets, and projected actions—and is therefore structurally portable
across CPS domains. What changes across domains is the plant-side adapter; what remains
invariant is the safety architecture.
Higher-budget replay across the energy-management reference row plus the A V, indus-
trial, healthcare, and aerospace runtime rows shows strong TSVR reduction with sub-millisecond
overhead. Claim strength still follows the tier table: energy management is the reference
row, industrial and healthcare are proof-validated, A V remains proof-candidate, aerospace
remains experimental, and navigation remains shadow-synthetic portability evidence. Per-
domain ﬁgures (§ 7.1.7) show the shared forecasting/runtime machinery: locked test splits,
trained GBM surfaces, 90% conformal prediction bands, and OQE reliability traces under
fault injection. The training audit (Table 7.2) and software-in-loop table (Table 7.3) conﬁrm

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 199 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 165
that each domain’s forecasting and runtime surfaces are reproducible and execution-veriﬁed.
All six domains share the same evaluation protocol, artifact types, and evidence tier struc-
ture. The theorem-to-code-to-artifact lineage established in the preceding chapters for the
energy-management reference row (Chapters 5.1– 4.6) applies equally to each domain through
the shared adapter contract. Navigation provides additional simulation-backed portability
evidence.
7.2 Software-in-the-Loop Validation
This chapter documents the software-in-the-loop validation package now locked in the reposi-
tory. The current evidence is not a claim of full ﬁeld deployment. It is a controller-in-the-loop
rehearsal that exercises the edge-agent path, certiﬁcate logging, and fault-handling interface
under the same safety logic used elsewhere in the dissertation.
7.2.1 Why This Layer Matters
The dissertation has already shown, in CPSBench, that hidden true-state violations appear
under degraded telemetry and that DC3S can eliminate them. The next question is opera-
tional rather than theoretical: does the end-to-end runtime still emit actionable commands,
preserve the certiﬁcate trail, and remain safe when the control loop is driven through the
edge-agent API rather than through an oﬄine benchmark harness?
7.2.2 Locked Runtime Path
The current locked evidence package uses the following runtime objects:
Controller runtime FastAPI TestClient wrapped by the ORIUS edge agent.
Battery driver SimBatteryDriver, which exposes the same control interface used by the
edge agent.
Fault protocol Nominal and dropout scenarios, each run for 48 steps from a shared seed
(seed = 42 ).
These conﬁguration facts are recorded directly in reports/hil/hil_hardware_table.csv.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 200 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 166
7.2.3 Scenario Coverage
The regenerated SIL package spans two scenarios:
• Nominal: 48 steps, 0 violations, 48 interventions, 100% certiﬁcate completeness.
• Dropout: 48 steps, 0 violations, 48 interventions, 100% certiﬁcate completeness.
Together they yield 96 audited steps, 0 true-state violations, and 100% certiﬁcate com-
pleteness, as recorded in reports/hil/hil_summary.json.
7.2.4 Step Log and SOC Trace
The per-step command path is logged in reports/hil/hil_step_log.csv, while the aggre-
gate trace is rendered in reports/hil/fig_hil_soc_trace.png. The operational meaning
is direct: once the controller approaches the lower SOC boundary, the shield repeatedly
projects aggressive discharge proposals back to safe near-zero actions. That behavior is
exactly what the dissertation claims should happen under safety tightening, and the SIL
package shows it on the live runtime path rather than only in benchmark replay.
7.2.5 Certiﬁcate Audit
The certiﬁcate chain in reports/hil/hil_certificate_audit.csv shows a complete record
of safe action, proposed action, and audit identity for every step. The derived timing table
in reports/hil/hil_timing_table.csv reports:
• 96 total audited steps,
• 96 interventions,
• 0 violations, and
• 100% certiﬁcate completeness.
This is the operational consequence of the SIL layer: every command that would have
stressed the system near its constraint boundary was repaired before execution, and every
repaired command remained traceable.

7.2.7 What This Chapter Establishes
The current SIL package establishes three operational facts:
1. The edge-agent execution path preserves the same safety logic used in the benchmarked
DC3S loop.
2. The certiﬁcate chain remains complete under both nominal and dropout conditions.
3. The controlled system remains within the safe envelope throughout the audited runtime
trace.
It does not yet establish external-hardware timing on a production controller or full ﬁeld
deployment robustness. Those remain future work rather than a completed claim.
