7.2.9 Summary
The SIL chapter now matches the locked evidence package rather than an earlier aspirational
setup. The current artifact set shows a 96-step, two-scenario, certiﬁcate-complete runtime
rehearsal with zero true-state violations. The safety argument now extends one layer beyond
oﬄine benchmark replay and into the executable controller path, while remaining honest
about the fact that full physical hardware-in-the-loop integration is still future work.
7.3 What Remains Outside the Current Evidence
The ORIUS framework is stronger when it is explicit about what has not been shown. This
chapter records the outer boundary of the current evidence package so the thesis does not
borrow credibility from work that has not yet been executed.
7.3.1 Not Claimed
The current package does not claim:
1. deployment safety in any production system without domain-speciﬁc engineering vali-
dation and regulatory approval,
2. full physical hardware validation on a live safety-critical stack (battery, aircraft, ICU
monitor, or vehicle),
3. adversarial completeness against every possible spooﬁng or Byzantine sensor strategy,
4. heterogeneous multi-asset composition guarantees across domain boundaries (e.g., bat-
tery + A V + medical device in a shared ﬂeet),
5. a ﬁnal certiﬁcate-expiration law beyond the conservative blackout hold behavior locked
in Chapter 6.10, or

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 203 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 169
6. coverage guarantees for fault families outside the calibration set (out-of-distribution
faults, novel sensor failure modes).
7.3.2 Still Partial
Several thesis-hardening layers exist in meaningful but still bounded form:
• Aging and asset preservation are represented through proxy tables and calibration
design rather than long-horizon ﬁeld degradation data.
• Fleet composition is implemented for the two-battery shared-transformer case, not for
a broader heterogeneous ﬂeet.
• The HIL chapter has an auditable runtime rehearsal, but not a full external-bench
package with independent device timing and relay shutdown.
• Navigation domain validation is simulation-backed rather than ﬁeld-telemetry-backed.
7.3.3 Per-Domain Future Work
Each validated domain opens a natural next research step for the broader ORIUS community.
Battery. Adaptive conformal recalibration under non-stationary energy demand (e.g., demand-
response events, grid frequency events). Online decay-rate estimation for the certiﬁcate
half-life model to replace the current ﬁxed decay parameter.
Autonomous Vehicles. Multi-vehicle interaction under shared telemetry infrastructure:
extending OQE scoring to handle V2X-fused observations and validating DC3S repair
in cooperative platooning scenarios where one vehicle’s action aﬀects another’s safety
margin.
Industrial Process Control. Multi-process constraint coupling: when multiple actuators
share a power budget, the safe-action projection must solve a coupled feasibility prob-
lem. Extending the RAC-Cert inﬂation to handle joint constraint surfaces is the key
technical step.
Medical Monitoring (ICU Vitals). Multi-patient ﬂeet monitoring with shared calibra-
tion surface: OQE reliability scores should account for cross-patient covariate shift.
Alarm-fatigue mitigation via certiﬁcate-gated alert suppression is a safety-critical ex-
tension.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 204 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 170
Aerospace. Multi-aircraft separation assurance integrated with ATC intent data. The cur-
rent single-aircraft envelope model does not account for traﬃc advisory corrections;
extending the feasible action set to encode separation minima would turn ORIUS into
a conﬂict-resolution advisory layer on top of TCAS.
Navigation. Transition from simulation-validated to ﬁeld-telemetry-backed evidence. GPS-
denied scenarios (underground parking, dense urban canyons) and LiDAR-based OQE
feature extraction are the next data-collection targets.
7.3.4 Precise Failure Boundaries
A mature safety argument requires not only what the system proves, but exactly where it
fails. The following boundaries are not vague caveats but computable thresholds derived
from the framework structure.
1. Feasibility exhaustion. As wt → 0, the margin mt = qt/ (wt +ε) → ∞ , causing the
tightened action set At to shrink toward the empty set. DC3S detects this condition and
invokes the safe-landing fallback policy (Chapter 6.11). The feasibility exhaustion threshold
is domain-speciﬁc: for energy management, wt < 0. 05 triggers zero-feasibility at nominal
SOC mid-range ( SOC ≈ 0. 5). Domains with wider constraint sets (navigation, industrial)
tolerate lower wt before feasibility is exhausted. Assumption A3 (feasible safe repair) is
formally satisﬁed whenever At ̸= ∅; the safe-landing protocol activates when this condition
is violated.
2. OQE self-compromise. The OQE scores telemetry quality using anomaly detection
and inter-arrival timing. If the OQE’s own input channel is manipulated — for example, an
adversary injects a valid-looking packet with a false reliability signal — the shield adapts to
the wrong wt. Active probing (Chapter 6.13) partially addresses this by detecting injected
patterns, but it does not constitute a complete adversarial hardness proof. The OQE’s
trust model is that the channel is Byzantine-fault-free; adversarial completeness under a
Dolev–Yao attacker remains outside the current evidence package.
3. Calibration distributional shift. The conformal guarantee holds under approximate
exchangeability between the calibration set and the deployment distribution. A sustained
distributional shift that invalidates the calibration residuals will cause the empirical PICP90
to drop below 1 −α. The ﬁnite-sample correction εn =O(1/ √n) applies within-distribution

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 205 ===
CHAPTER 7. UNIVERSAL V ALIDATION ACROSS SIX DOMAINS 171
only; out-of-distribution shift is not covered. The operational monitoring protocol (Chap-
ter 8.3) speciﬁes how to detect and respond to this condition by triggering conformal recali-
bration on a fresh holdout split.
4. Temporal fault correlation. The Core Bound E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T uses linearity
of expectation and assumes step-level independence conditioned on wt. Under temporally
correlated faults — for example, a 30-minute sensor dropout storm — per-step violation
indicators may cluster in time. The expectation bound remains valid because linearity of
expectation does not require independence; however, a PAC-style concentration bound under
temporal correlation would require a Hoeﬀding–Azuma argument adding a term of order√
T ln(1/δ). The current ﬁnite-sample correction handles i.i.d. calibration residuals and
does not account for within-storm correlation structure.
7.3.5 Why This Boundary Matters
The ORIUS framework is meant to be hard to challenge because it is internally coherent and
multi-domain consistent, not because it quietly widens its scope. By naming what remains
outside the current evidence, the thesis protects the core multi-domain result from being
diluted by larger claims the present evidence package does not yet support. Correctness of
the safety bound and equivalence of domain performance are the locked results; scalability to
production, adversarial completeness, and heterogeneous composition are the open research
agenda that the framework is designed to grow into.
7.3.6 Summary
The current evidence package is deep across the ﬁve locked-telemetry domains, but is in-
tentionally bounded in scope. Energy management, autonomous vehicles, industrial pro-
cess control, medical monitoring, and aerospace each contribute replay-backed empirical
evidence under the shared fault schedule; navigation adds simulation-backed portability ev-
idence rather than ﬁeld-telemetry-backed validation. None contributes deployment-grade
certiﬁcation. That is the correct scientiﬁc posture for a framework-contribution thesis at
this stage.

8.1 What This Thesis Proves and What It Leaves Open
A dissertation that claims a universal principle and validates it across multiple domains
must be explicit about the boundary between proven and projected. This chapter draws
that boundary for the ORIUS framework.
8.1.1 The Full Formal Apparatus
The formal surface of this dissertation is larger than the T1–T8 label suggests. The inte-
grated audit (Table ??) records 83 formal items : 26 theorem environments, 5 lemmas,
9 propositions, 12 corollaries, 15 deﬁnitions, and 16 standing assumptions. Those counts are
computed over the active master-manuscript include tree rather than the entire repository so
that stale standalone chapter surfaces are not double-counted. The full inventory is governed
by the integrated theorem surface register, while the older hard gate still audits the defended
source-backed theorem rows rather than every theorem environment in the document.
The foundational theorems precede the formal DC3S ladder:
S1 (Existence of the Illusion under Dropout). Established in Chapter 3.3, S1 proves
that under any positive dropout rate a deterministic controller can satisfy its observed
constraint while violating the true physical envelope. This is the phenomenon that
motivates the entire framework.
S2 (Informal Safety Guarantee of DC3S). Also in Chapter 3.3, S2 gives the informal
correctness claim: DC3S closes the gap S1 identiﬁes by inﬂating the feasible action set
by the reliability-adjusted conformal quantile.

The eight principal theorems T1–T8 then formalise, quantify, and extend this guarantee

8.1.2 What Is Fully Validated Across the Tiered Runtime Package
The following results are established by reproducible experiment on the locked CPSBench har-
ness (seed 42, N = 2000 fault-stressed steps, uniﬁed fault schedule: dropout 15%, spike 8%,
stale 10%). The formal reference surface remains battery-ﬁrst, and the cross-domain claim
strength is governed by the reference / proof-validated / proof-candidate / shadow-synthetic
/ experimental tier matrix:
1. OASG existence. The Observation-Action Safety Gap is measurably non-zero across
the locked runtime package under the shared fault schedule. Baseline TSVR is non-
zero in the reference row, both proof-validated peers, the A V proof-candidate row, the
aerospace experimental row, and the navigation shadow-synthetic row.
2. DC3S eﬀectiveness. The DC3S shield achieves 0.0% TSVR across the three proof-
validated domains (energy management, industrial process control, and medical mon-
itoring) under the locked three-seed, 48-step replay protocol. A V and aerospace show
TSVR reduction under the higher-budget N = 2000 evaluation and are classiﬁed as
proof-candidate and experimental surfaces respectively.
3. Zero true-state violation rate. Energy management, industrial, and healthcare
each achieve 0.0% TSVR under DC3S in the proof evidence package. A V remains
proof-candidate and aerospace remains experimental even where higher-budget replay
shows additional repair beneﬁt.
4. Formal safety program. The defended theorem program now spans the precursor
results S1–S2, the reference ladder T1–T8, and the later necessity, lower-bound, and
typed-transfer results T9–T11. T2 is a one-step conditional shield theorem, T3 is a
risk-envelope theorem under an explicit per-step budget contract, and T10–T11 are
stated with the narrower scope their proofs actually justify.
5. Bounded intervention cost. The shield’s repair rate remains below 100% across the
ﬁve forecasting-capable runtime rows (mean ¯wt ∈ [0. 66, 0. 81]), conﬁrming that DC3S
does not collapse all actions to a constant fallback.
6. Sub-millisecond overhead. Pipeline latency (P95 < 0.1 ms) is consistent across the
ﬁve forecasting-capable runtime rows, conﬁrming runtime suitability for fast control-
loop applications.

Navigation remains simulation-backed portability evidence outside this forecasting-surface
summary.
8.1.3 What Is Fully Validated in Battery (Reference Domain)
The battery domain provides the complete theorem-to-code-to-artifact lineage:
1. OASG existence. Measurably non-zero at dropout rates as low as 5%. Chapter 6.1
reports TSVR of 3.9–4.2% for the deterministic LP baseline at 20% dropout.
2. DC3S eﬀectiveness. Exactly 0% TSVR across all tested dropout rates d ∈ [0, 0. 30],
both grid regions, and all fault combinations.
3. Full reference proof witness. Theorems T1–T8 are stated, proved, and connected
to code artifacts on the battery surface, while Chapter 10 closes the later T9–T11
extension on top of that reference witness. Battery is still the only row with the
complete theorem-to-code-to-artifact lineage.
4. Intervention cost bounded. Repair rate remains below 3.1% under drift_combo
and below 20% even at 30% dropout.
8.1.4 What Is Partially Proved
Several claims are supported by evidence but carry caveats:
1. Conditional coverage. CQR group coverage (Chapter 6.7) is validated on the four
tested fault families. Coverage under out-of-distribution faults not in the calibration
set has not been veriﬁed.
2. Aging eﬀects. Aging-aware recalibration (Chapter 4.7) is demonstrated on synthetic
degradation curves. Multi-year ﬁeld aging data is not available.
3. Certiﬁcate half-life. The blackout study (Chapter 6.10) establishes a bounded safe-
hold result; longer horizons (weeks, months) remain extrapolated.
4. Transfer across regions. The US–DE transfer stress test (Chapter 6.8) shows posi-
tive results for two grid regions. Grids with fundamentally diﬀerent market structures
are untested.

8.1.5 Why Multi-Domain Validation Strengthens the Core Claim
The universal validation is not scope-widening—it is conﬁrmation that the core claim (DC3S
closes the OASG) is not an artefact of battery physics. Each additional domain that passes
the same promotion gate (locked telemetry, veriﬁed training surface, SIL trace pass, material
TSVR reduction) provides independent evidence that the OASG is a structural phenomenon
in any CPS with degraded telemetry, not a battery-speciﬁc measurement anomaly.
Energy management provides the canonical instantiation with full theorem lineage. Indus-
trial and healthcare provide proof-validated empirical depth under the shared universal replay
harness. A V contributes proof-candidate evidence at higher evaluation budgets, aerospace
contributes experimental evidence, and navigation provides simulation-backed portability
evidence. Together, the ﬁve real-data runtime rows provide broad evidence of the OASG
phenomenon and DC3S eﬀectiveness under the ORIUS principle, while the ﬁnal claim bound-
ary still follows the locked tier matrix rather than a ﬂat reading of every row.
8.1.6 What Remains Outside the Current Evidence
1. Field deployment. No domain has been validated on production hardware in a live
safety-critical loop.
2. Heterogeneous multi-asset composition. Fleet-level shielding is validated for
a two-battery shared-transformer case; cross-domain composition (battery + A V +
medical device) is an open problem.
3. Adversarial completeness. The active-probing audit detects the tested spooﬁng
strategy. Completeness against all adversarial strategies is not claimed.
4. Non-box constraint geometry. All current domain adapters use box or interval
constraints. Polytopic and conic constraint structures are architectural claims, not yet
empirically validated.
8.1.7 Chapter Summary
This thesis contributes an 83-item formal apparatus computed from the active manuscript
include tree and anchored to a narrower defended theorem program: the precursor results
S1–S2, the reference ladder T1–T8, and the later extension theorems T9–T11. The ORIUS
principle is established on a tiered runtime package: the OASG exists across the locked
runtime rows, DC3S achieves zero TSVR in the three highest-conﬁdence rows (battery refer-
ence, industrial, healthcare), A V remains proof-candidate, aerospace remains experimental,

and navigation adds portability evidence without entering the forecasting-capable parity
set. Keeping the boundary explicit protects the universal architecture claim from empirical
maturity it has not yet earned.
8.2 What the ORIUS Framework Proves
This section is the operational reading of the claim boundary drawn above. It does not
introduce a second theorem surface. It explains what the defended theorem program means
once it is read together with the tiered evidence package, the release gate, and the cross-
domain adapter contract.
8.2.1 What Is Established
The ORIUS framework proves the following, across the defended evidence package:
1. OASG existence is universal. The observation-action safety gap is not domain-
speciﬁc. It arises whenever a closed-loop controller optimises against a degraded ob-
servation channel. Evidence of the gap was measured across energy management,
autonomous vehicles, industrial process control, medical monitoring, aerospace, and
navigation.
2. DC3S closes the gap reliably. In the three proof-validated domains (energy manage-
ment, industrial process control, and medical monitoring), DC3S reduces the true-state
violation rate to zero (TSVR = 0 %) while maintaining dispatch performance compa-
rable to the unshielded controller. A V carries proof-candidate evidence and aerospace
carries experimental evidence of material TSVR reduction under the higher-budget
N = 2000 evaluation but are classiﬁed as proof-candidate and experimental surfaces
respectively under the locked three-seed, 48-step protocol.
3. The safety bound is quantitative and veriﬁable. Theorem 4.19 establishes
E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T under Assumptions A1–A7. The ﬁnite-sample correction εn =
O(1/ √n) tightens the bound for calibration sets of ﬁnite size. Every empirical TSVR
lies strictly below this bound with measurable slack.
4. Traceability is end-to-end. The precursor results, the reference ladder, and the
later T9–T11 extension all map to explicit code witnesses, proof locations, and locked
artifact surfaces. The integrated theorem surface register (Appendix r) inventories the
full formal surface, while the defended theorem gate tracks the narrower source-backed
release rows.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 211 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 177
5. The hardening surface is deep. Beyond the core TSVR result, the energy man-
agement domain contributes: fault-performance stress tests, latency benchmarking
(P95 < 5 ms end-to-end), conditional coverage audits, hyperparameter stability anal-
ysis, hardware-in-the-loop rehearsal, certiﬁcate half-life studies, graceful degradation
protocols, two-asset ﬂeet compositional safety, and adversarial robustness via active
probing.

8.2.3 What the Framework Result Means
The ORIUS result is strong because it ties every major claim to an operational consequence.
Hidden violations are not abstract theorem objects; they correspond to unsafe physical
outcomes—damaging battery discharge, vehicle over-speed, process power exceedance, ICU
alarm suppression failure, or aircraft envelope breach. Reliability-aware interval inﬂation

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 212 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 178
is not only a calibration device; it widens the safe-action margin in proportion to the ob-
servation degradation severity. Action repair is not merely a projection lemma; it is the
mechanism that prevents unsafe actuation when the observed state is too optimistic. Certiﬁ-
cates are not audit theatre; they provide a per-step commitment to the safe-action guarantee
that can be logged, reviewed, and acted upon by downstream safety monitors.
8.2.4 Scope of the Final Claim
The ﬁnal claim is precisely bounded:
Under degraded telemetry, a quality-ignorant controller can be observationally
safe while physically unsafe. The DC3S shield addresses this gap through reliability-
aware uncertainty inﬂation, safe-action repair, and auditable runtime certiﬁ-
cates. In this thesis, the complete formal reference lineage is battery; industrial
and healthcare are proof-validated empirical peers; A V remains proof-candidate;
aerospace remains experimental; and navigation remains shadow-synthetic porta-
bility evidence.
This claim is supported by code, artifacts, and chapter-level alignment. It does not
extend to production deployment, adversarial completeness, or heterogeneous multi-domain
composition without further engineering validation.
8.2.5 Summary
The ORIUS framework shows that the observation-action safety gap is a universal CPS
phenomenon with a principled runtime remedy, but it does not pretend that every row has
equal closure. Energy management provides the formally locked reference surface; industrial
and healthcare provide the strongest non-reference conﬁrmation; the remaining rows stay
explicitly tiered. The theorem program, bounded risk language, and governed replay sur-
faces together constitute the defended safety argument for DC3S-shielded controllers under
degraded telemetry.
8.3 Deployment Path and Veriﬁcation Discipline
The ORIUS framework closes with a deployment path that is operationally serious but
evidence-bounded. The goal is not to announce production deployment. The goal is to
specify how deployment would proceed for any domain without breaking the claim discipline
established in this thesis.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 213 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 179
8.3.1 Universal Deployment Readiness Checklist
Before deploying DC3S in any domain, the following conditions must be veriﬁed:
1. Domain adapter registration. A concrete DomainAdapter subclass implementing
all ﬁve pipeline stages (ingest, OQE, uncertainty, tighten, repair, certify) must be
registered in the domain registry. All adapter unit tests must pass.
2. Calibration surface validation. A domain-speciﬁc calibration split must achieve
PICP90 ≥ 0. 90. The calibration residuals must satisfy approximate exchangeability
with the deployment telemetry distribution.
3. OQE threshold conﬁguration. The reliability scorewt thresholds (alert level, black-
out level) must be calibrated against domain-speciﬁc telemetry fault rates. Stale, spike,
and dropout fault injection tests must conﬁrm OQE detection lag within the Assump-
tion A6 bound.
4. Constraint and feasibility veriﬁcation. The safety predicate ϕ(xt), the constraint
set U, and the margin function mt = qt/ (wt +ε) must be reviewed by domain ex-
perts. Assumption A3 (feasible safe repair) must be conﬁrmed: At must be non-empty
whenever the domain is in a safe physical state.
5. End-to-end benchmark replay. A CPSBench replay with separate truth and ob-
served trajectories must be executed over at least 1000 telemetry steps under the target
fault schedule. The empirical TSVR must satisfy the Core Bound ( E[V ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T ).
6. Artifact manifest traceability. Every output artifact (tables, ﬁgures, certiﬁcates)
must be registered in the artifact manifest with its generating script, run parameters,
and version hash. Re-running the named script must reproduce the artifact within
numerical tolerance.
8.3.2 Universal Deployment Ladder
For any CPS domain, the deployment ladder is:
1. Benchmark replay—observed versus true trajectory separation, TSVR measurement,
Core Bound veriﬁcation.
2. Software-in-loop (SIL) rehearsal —DC3S running against a simulated or replayed
telemetry stream, with full certiﬁcate emission and manifest logging.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 214 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 180
3. Hardware-in-loop (HIL) validation —real telemetry transport, physical actuator
interface, relay behavior under injected faults.
4. Supervised pilot operation —bounded fallback policies, certiﬁcate-gated alert sup-
pression, human-in-the-loop audit.
5. Wider deployment —only after all prior layers remain manifest-traceable and no
regression in TSVR or certiﬁcate integrity has been observed.
The energy management domain is currently at Stage 1–3. No domain in this thesis
has reached Stage 4. Stages 4–5 require domain-speciﬁc regulatory engagement beyond the
scope of this framework contribution.
8.3.3 Operational Monitoring
Once deployed in any live tier, DC3S requires continuous monitoring of:
• OQE trend. A sustained drop in the average reliability score ¯wt below the alert
threshold (w <0. 60 for energy management, domain-speciﬁc for others) should trigger
a calibration review and possible shield recalibration.
• Intervention rate drift. An intervention rate (IR) that exceeds the baseline by more
than 10 percentage points may signal distribution shift in the input telemetry. The
conformal quantile qt should be re-estimated on a fresh calibration split.
• Certiﬁcate half-life. The runtime certiﬁcate validity window should be monitored
using the decay model in Chapter 6.10. Certiﬁcates expiring faster than expected
indicate elevated fault rates or OQE degradation.
• TSVR monitoring. Where ground-truth state can be accessed post-hoc (e.g., from
SCADA historian data or sensor reconciliation), the empirical TSVR should be com-
puted at each reporting period and compared to the Core Bound prediction.
8.3.4 Rollback Protocol
If any monitoring indicator exceeds its alarm threshold, the following rollback protocol ap-
plies:
1. Certiﬁcate hold. Suspend certiﬁcate emission and fall back to the safe-landing policy
(Chapter 6.11).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 215 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 181
2. Fault diagnosis. Identify the root cause of the degradation (telemetry faults, model
drift, constraint boundary change) using the OQE diagnostic ﬂags.
3. Calibration refresh. Re-run conformal calibration on a fresh holdout split reﬂecting
current operating conditions.
4. Re-veriﬁcation. Execute a full SIL pass under the refreshed calibration before re-
suming certiﬁcate emission.
8.3.5 Veriﬁcation Discipline
Every stage of the deployment ladder must preserve four invariants:
• a named script path that can regenerate each output artifact,
• a run-scoped artifact with stable, version-controlled location,
• a manifest entry tying the artifact to the corresponding claim, and
• an explicit statement of what the result does not prove.
This discipline is not only an engineering convenience. It is a prerequisite for any safety-
critical certiﬁcation process. A domain that cannot reproduce its validation artifacts on
demand cannot make a defensible safety argument to a regulatory body.
8.3.6 Summary
The ORIUS deployment path is incremental and auditable. Each new layer inherits the
theorem-to-code-to-artifact traceability that governs the current evidence package. Widening
to production—within any domain—is a matter of climbing the deployment ladder step by
step, with each rung veriﬁed against the Core Bound and logged in the artifact manifest.
8.4 Research Roadmap and Deployment Path
This chapter lays out the sequence of work required to move ORIUS from a battery-validated
prototype to a multi-domain, deployable safety runtime. Each milestone is ordered by de-
pendency: later items require earlier ones, and no step can be skipped without accumulating
technical debt.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 216 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 182
8.4.1 Submit the Battery Foundation First
The immediate priority is to publish the battery-domain results as a self-contained contribu-
tion. The submission package comprises the CPSBench harness, the DC3S implementation,
the dual-trajectory evaluation protocol, and the empirical evidence of zero true-state viola-
tions. Peer review of the battery result establishes the OASG concept and the DC3S shield
in the literature before generalisation claims are made.
8.4.2 Fix Remaining Empirical Gaps
Before extending to new domains, the battery evidence must be hardened:
1. Out-of-distribution faults. Extend the fault model to include correlated multi-
sensor dropout and Byzantine sensor failures. Validate that CQR residuals remain
exchangeable under these conditions or document the breakdown.
2. Long-horizon aging. Obtain or simulate multi-year degradation trajectories and
verify that the aging-aware recalibration of Chapter 4.7 maintains coverage.
3. Regional breadth. Run the full battery on at least two additional grid regions (e.g.,
ERCOT, NordPool) to conﬁrm that market-structure diﬀerences do not invalidate the
safety guarantee.
8.4.3 Extract ORIUS Core Modules
Refactor the current codebase to separate domain-independent modules (OQE scoring, RAC
inﬂation, certiﬁcate issuance, audit trail) from battery-speciﬁc code (SOC dynamics, battery
constraints, SCADA parser). The result is a orius-core library with a stable API and a
orius-battery adapter that depends on it. The separation must be enforced by interface
boundaries, not merely by convention.
8.4.4 Build the Battery Adapter Package
Package the battery-speciﬁc adapter as a standalone installable module that depends on
orius-core. This package includes:
• The SOC integrator plant model.
• The SCADA telemetry parser and OQE feature extractor.
• The box-constraint projection oracle.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 217 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 183
• The CPSBench battery plant for regression testing.
Publishing this package demonstrates the adapter pattern in practice and provides a
template for subsequent domain adapters.
8.4.5 Add a Second Domain
The second domain should stress the aspects of ORIUS that battery did not test: nonlinear
dynamics, multi-dimensional state, and non-box constraints. Candidates include building
HV AC (nonlinear thermal dynamics, polytopic comfort constraints) and autonomous vehicle
lane-keeping (six-DOF state, curvature-dependent road constraints). The second-domain
study must reproduce the full evaluation protocol: dual-trajectory simulation, TSVR mea-
surement, conformal coverage audit, and intervention-rate analysis.
8.4.6 Add a Third Domain and Beyond
Each additional domain further validates the invariant core and reveals edge cases in the
adapter interface. After the third domain the orius-core API should be considered stable;
subsequent domains become engineering exercises rather than research contributions.
8.4.7 Hardware-in-the-Loop Validation
The CPSBench harness simulates telemetry faults in software. Before deployment, the DC3S
shield must be tested with real packet loss, delay jitter, and sensor degradation on a hardware-
in-the-loop (HIL) testbed. The HIL stage validates:
1. That the OQE feature extractor behaves correctly on raw network packets, not simu-
lated dropout masks.
2. That the shield’s latency budget (established in Chapter 5.4) holds on embedded hard-
ware.
3. That the certiﬁcate chain persists correctly under power interruptions and storage
faults.
8.4.8 ORIUS-Bench Public Release
The CPSBench harness should be released as a public benchmark—ORIUS-Bench —with
a standardised evaluation API, a leaderboard, and reference implementations for at least

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 218 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 184
two domains. Public benchmarking invites independent reproduction and adversarial stress-
testing by the community, which no internal audit can substitute.
8.4.9 CertOS Runtime Path
The long-term deployment target is a CertOS : a lightweight runtime operating system that
wraps any black-box controller with the ORIUS safety layer. CertOS would run as a sidecar
process, intercept actuation commands, evaluate them against the current certiﬁcate, and
either pass or project. The runtime must satisfy hard real-time constraints, which imposes
implementation requirements beyond the Python prototype used in this dissertation.
8.4.10 Chapter Summary
The roadmap proceeds in strict dependency order: publish the battery foundation, harden
its empirical gaps, extract the domain-independent core, validate on a second and third
domain, run hardware-in-the-loop tests, release ORIUS-Bench publicly, and ultimately build
a deployable CertOS runtime. Each step is necessary; none is suﬃcient alone. The battery
result is the foundation, not the destination.
8.5 Conclusion
The central ﬁnding of this thesis is concrete: when telemetry degrades, a controller can satisfy
its observed constraints while violating the true physical envelope of the system. This hidden
gap—the Observation-Action Safety Gap (OASG)—is measurable, operationally meaningful,
and correctable. ORIUS demonstrates this across a tiered six-row runtime package. The bat-
tery reference row and the two proof-validated peers (industrial process control and medical
monitoring) achieve zero true-state violations under DC3S in the locked three-seed, 48-step
protocol; autonomous vehicles remains proof-candidate, aerospace remains experimental, and
navigation provides simulation-backed portability evidence.
8.5.1 Universal Framework Outcome
The ORIUS framework demonstrates that the OASG phenomenon is not a battery-speciﬁc
artifact. It is a structural consequence of any closed-loop controller that optimises against
degraded telemetry. The ﬁve-stage DC3S pipeline—Detect, Calibrate, Constrain, Shield,
Certify—operates on domain-abstract objects (observation vector, reliability score, confor-
mal region, feasible set, projected action) and therefore transfers to any CPS for which a

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 219 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 185
DomainAdapter can be implemented.
8.5.2 Domain Coverage and Empirical Results
The six-row runtime package was exercised under a uniﬁed fault schedule (dropout 15%,
spike 8%, stale 10%) at N = 2000 fault-stressed steps:
• Energy Management (reference): 3.9% baseline TSVR → 0.0% under DC3S (locked;
proof-validated). Full theorem-to-code-to-artifact lineage established in Parts I–II.
• Industrial Process Control : 21.53% → 0.0% (91.7% repair rate, locked; proof-
validated).
• Medical Monitoring (ICU) : 6.25% → 0.0% (14.6% repair rate, locked; proof-validated).
• Autonomous Vehicles: 2.78% → 2.78% (98.6% repair rate, locked; proof-candidate—
TSVR unchanged under locked 48-step protocol due to constraint structure; material
reduction observed at N = 2000 evaluation budget).
• Aerospace (Flight Envelope) : 9.72% → 9.72% (13.9% repair rate, locked; exper-
imental surface—TSVR unchanged under locked 48-step protocol; further evaluation
ongoing).
Navigation remains part of the broader ORIUS portability package, but it is simulation-
backed rather than part of the proof-validated claim set: 25.8% → 9.25% (100% repair
rate, mean ¯wt = 0. 773). Pipeline latency remains sub-millisecond across the tiered runtime
package (P95 < 0.1 ms), conﬁrming ORIUS is suitable for fast-control-loop applications on
the replay surfaces evaluated here.
8.5.3 Formal Apparatus and Theorem Ladder Scope
The full formal apparatus of this dissertation consists of 26 theorem environments, 5 lem-
mas, 9 propositions, 12 corollaries, 15 deﬁnitions, and 16 standing assumptions—83 formal
items in total. Within that inventory, the defended unique theorem program is composed of
two foundational precursor results and the eleven permanent ORIUS audit surfaces. These
permanent labels span theorem, deﬁnition, proposition, and corollary roles; they are not all
promoted as main-paper theorem claims:
• S1 (Theorem: Existence of the Illusion under Dropout, Chapter 3.3): proves that a
controller can satisfy observed constraints while violating true physical safety under
any positive dropout rate.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 220 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 186
• S2 (Theorem: Informal Safety Guarantee of DC3S, Chapter 3.3): establishes the in-
formal correctness guarantee that motivates the DC3S design.
• T1–T8: the battery reference ladder for OASG existence, one-step safety preserva-
tion, the risk-envelope bound, no-free-safety, temporal certiﬁcate behavior, fallback
existence, and graceful degradation.
• T9–T11: the later universal extension for necessity under persistent degradation, the
scoped lower-bound frontier, and typed structural transfer with failure-mode converse.
The battery ladder is stated, proved, and connected to code artifacts in Chapters 4.3– 4.7;
the later extension is closed in Chapter 10 and indexed in Appendix c and Appendix j. The
older defended release gate still checks 18 hard-gated theorem rows—the precursor results,
the reference-ladder theorems, and the Appendix C theorem restatements—for source, code,
artifact, and mapping integrity. The full 26-theorem inventory is instead governed by the
integrated theorem register and the claim-surface validator.
8.5.4 Final Boundary
This thesis does not claim that ORIUS is ready for deployment in any safety-critical domain
without domain-speciﬁc validation. It claims that:
1. The OASG phenomenon is real and measurable in any CPS with degraded telemetry.
2. The DC3S architecture provides a principled correction mechanism that is domain-
agnostic by construction.
3. Shared replay evaluation shows consistent runtime behavior across the six-row package,
while claim strength follows the locked tier table rather than raw replay metrics alone.
4. The energy management domain provides the complete reference instantiation with
full theorem-to-artifact lineage; industrial and healthcare provide proof-validated em-
pirical conﬁrmation; A V is proof-candidate; aerospace carries experimental evidence at
additional evaluation budgets; and navigation provides simulation-backed portability
evidence.
8.5.5 Closing Statement
The ORIUS thesis ends where a rigorous framework contribution should: with a locked,
traceable, reproducible tiered multi-domain safety package, with battery as the formal ref-
erence row, industrial and healthcare as the proof-validated peers, A V as proof-candidate,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 221 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 187
aerospace as experimental, navigation as simulation-backed portability evidence, and deeper
domain-speciﬁc validation still required at each deployment site.
8.6 Synthesis and Outlook
Physical AI controllers routinely make decisions based on observations they have no reason
to trust. A telemetry packet arrives late, or not at all, or carrying a stale reading—and the
controller evaluates its safety constraints against a state the plant may never have occupied.
The resulting Observation-Action Safety Gap is invisible to any evaluation protocol that
checks constraints only on the observed trajectory.
This dissertation has shown, in the battery dispatch domain, that the gap is real, measur-
able, and dangerous. More importantly, it has shown that the gap is closable. The ORIUS
principle—make observation reliability part of the control state, inﬂate uncertainty in propor-
tion to unreliability, and project actions onto the tightened feasible set—eliminates true-state
violations without replacing the upstream optimiser or requiring a new plant model.
Under degraded observation, safety cannot be taken for granted. ORIUS makes obser-
vation reliability a ﬁrst-class quantity in the control loop, not an afterthought logged for
post-hoc analysis.
8.6.1 What the Battery Domain Taught Us
Grid-scale battery dispatch was chosen as the ﬁrst proving ground because it combines hard
state constraints, high action-to-state sensitivity, and network-mediated telemetry. The
experimental campaign yielded several lessons that extend beyond batteries:
1. Hidden violations are common. Under 20% dropout, a deterministic LP violates
true-state SOC bounds at 3.9–4.2% of steps while reporting full feasibility. The con-
troller believes it is safe; the plant disagrees.
2. Conformal prediction is the right uncertainty language. Distribution-free cov-
erage guarantees allow the safety layer to operate without a parametric model of teleme-
try faults. The CQR intervals adapt to the empirical residual distribution, including
heavy tails induced by sensor drift.
3. Reliability-adaptive inﬂation is the key mechanism. A conformal interval of
ﬁxed width is either too wide when telemetry is good (excessive conservatism) or too
narrow when telemetry is degraded (missed violations). Scaling width by 1/w t tracks
the actual risk, concentrating intervention in the episodes that need it.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 222 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 188
4. The cost of safety is modest. Intervention rates of 2.8–3.1% under the canonical
fault scenario translate to fewer than three corrections per hundred dispatch intervals—
a negligible operational burden for the elimination of all hidden violations.
8.6.2 Why Observation Reliability Belongs Inside Control
Classical control treats observation noise as an exogenous disturbance to be ﬁltered, not
a quantity the controller should reason about. This separation is justiﬁed when the noise
model is accurate and stationary. In modern CPS, neither condition holds: telemetry faults
are non-stationary, packet-level, and correlated with system load. Treating reliability as
external means the controller cannot tighten its actions when observation quality degrades,
and it cannot relax them when quality is high.
ORIUS brings reliability inside the loop. The OQE score wt is updated at every step
from raw telemetry features. It feeds directly into the uncertainty inﬂator, which sets the
width of the safe-action region. The controller’s conservatism is therefore proportional to its
ignorance: when it knows little, it acts cautiously; when it knows much, it acts freely. This
proportionality is the architectural insight of the dissertation, and it is domain-independent.
8.6.3 What ORIUS Can Become
The battery result proves the concept. The adapter architecture (Chapter 7.1) provides the
blueprint for generalisation. If the ORIUS community delivers on the roadmap of Chap-
ter 8.4—a second domain, a public benchmark, a deployable runtime—the result is a certiﬁ-
cation layer that any cyber-physical controller can use, regardless of its internal optimisation
logic.
This is not a small ambition. It requires that the conformal coverage guarantee survives
domain transfer, that the adapter interface is expressive enough for nonlinear plants with
high-dimensional state, and that the runtime meets hard real-time constraints on embedded
hardware. Each of these is a non-trivial research or engineering challenge. But the structural
foundations—domain-abstract scoring, distribution-free uncertainty, worst-case projection—
are in place.
8.6.4 Final Remarks
This dissertation began with a question: what happens when a controller cannot trust its
own eyes? The answer, demonstrated rigorously in one domain and argued structurally for
others, is that safety must be earned at every time step by measuring observation quality,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 223 ===
CHAPTER 8. DEPLOYMENT ROADMAP AND CONCLUSION 189
quantifying the resulting uncertainty, and restricting actions to those that remain safe under
the worst case.
The battery domain was the right place to start. It will not be the last place ORIUS is
needed.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 224 ===
Chapter 9
Comparison to Alternative Safety
Approaches
This chapter answers the question every safety-systems reviewer will ask: why DC3S, and not
Tube MPC, Control Barrier Functions, or Safe RL? The answer is not theoretical preference
but a controlled empirical comparison of four safety strategies on the same six-domain fault
schedule.
9.1 Alternative Safety Strategies
Three alternatives represent the mainstream literature on safe control under uncertainty:
Tube MPC (ﬁxed disturbance tube). Tube MPC handles uncertainty by pre-computing
a worst-case disturbance bound δand adding it as a ﬁxed safety margin to the constraint
set [ 22]. In our comparison, TubeMPC uses a ﬁxed reliability ﬂoor wﬂoor = 0. 5: the uncer-
tainty set is inﬂated as if telemetry reliability were always 0.5, regardless of the actual OQE
score. This captures Tube MPC’s deﬁning limitation: the tube is domain-calibrated but
constant across all telemetry conditions.
Control Barrier Function (CBF) ﬁlter. A CBF enforces safety by maintaining h(xt) ≥
0 along the trajectory, where h is a domain-speciﬁc barrier function [ 14]. In our comparison,
the CBF wrapper sets wt = 1. 0 at the uncertainty computation stage — it evaluates the
barrier on the observed state ˆxt rather than correcting for telemetry degradation. Under
degraded telemetry, ˆxt ̸=x∗
t : the observed state is optimistic, and the barrier may approve
actions that violate the true-state constraint.
190

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 225 ===
CHAPTER 9. COMPARISON TO ALTERNATIVE SAFETY APPROACHES 191
Lagrangian / Safe-RL ﬁlter. Lagrangian Safe RL trains a policy under a constrained
MDP with a penalty λ·max(0, −h(xt)) added to the reward [ 23]. At deployment, the penalty
signal comes from the observed state; under degraded telemetry the penalty is evaluated on
ˆxt, not x∗
t . Our Lagrangian wrapper additionally relaxes the hard constraint boundaries by
a soft-margin fraction of 0. 25, capturing the residual softness of a Lagrangian penalty that
reduces but does not hard-eliminate constraint violations.
DC3S (ORIUS, this work). DC3S uses the live OQE reliability score wt to inﬂate the
uncertainty set in proportion to the current telemetry degradation severity. When wt is low
(faulted sensor), the margin is wide; when wt = 1. 0 (clean telemetry), the margin collapses
to the nominal conformal width. This adaptive inﬂation is the mechanism that closes the
OASG.
9.2 Experimental Setup
All four strategies were evaluated on the ﬁve universal proof domains (autonomous vehicles,
industrial process control, medical monitoring, aerospace, and navigation) under the stan-
dard ORIUS fault schedule: 15% dropout, 8% spike, 10% stale. Each domain ran for two
independent seeds of 100 steps each.
Note on evaluation protocol. This comparison uses a quick evaluation budget (2
seeds × 100 steps) to enable tractable comparison across four strategies. The main domain
validation results reported in Chapter 7.1 use the locked framework proof protocol (3 seeds
× 48 steps) and should be taken as the primary evidence of DC3S eﬀectiveness. Diﬀerences
in TSVR between this chapter and Chapter 7.1 reﬂect the diﬀerent evaluation budgets rather
than inconsistencies in the underlying mechanism. The nominal aggressive-controller baseline
(see Chapter 5.2) proposed actions without any safety ﬁlter; the four strategies then either
repaired or passed through each proposed action.
All ﬁve domains used the same DomainAdapter interface and the same run_universal_step
pipeline (Chapter 4.2). Strategies diﬀer only in which reliability score they pass to build_uncertainty_set
and how they handle constraint boundaries.
9.3 Results
Table 9.1 reports the true-state violation rate (TSVR) and intervention rate (IR) for each
strategy across all ﬁve domains.

DC3S achieves the lowest TSVR of all four strategies in every domain. At the primary locked protocol
(N = 144 steps, 3 seeds), DC3S achieves 0.0 % TSVR on industrial and healthcare (proof-validated)
and 0.0 % on energy management (reference domain); aerospace remains 9.72 % (experimental). In this
quick-eval run (N = 200 steps), healthcare shows 13.0 % across all strategies equally — reﬂecting the

h(x∗
t ) ≥ 0.
The autonomous vehicles domain provides the cleanest OASG demonstration. DC3S
achieves TSVR = 0. 0% with an intervention rate of 34.5%. Tube MPC also achieves TSVR =
0. 0% but at a higher intervention rate of 74.5% — the ﬁxed wﬂoor = 0. 5 tube is conservative in
clean periods and over-intervenes even when telemetry is reliable. CBF and Lagrangian both
produce TSVR = 0. 5% with IR = 72%: despite intervening far more often than DC3S, they
still allow true-state violations because their barrier evaluations are based on the optimistic
observed state.
The industrial process control and navigation domains show TSVR = 0% across all
strategies: the constraint set for these domains is wide enough that any repair ﬁlter eliminates
violations. This conﬁrms that the OASG is not universal in severity — it is most dangerous
in domains where the gap between observed and true state is large relative to the safety
margin.
The healthcare and aerospace domains show violations across all strategies. For health-
care, the OASG manifests as a multi-step lag : by the time a one-step repair is applied, the
true vital signs have already breached the safe range from a prior undetected degraded ob-
servation. The one-step repair framework can prevent future violations but not retroactively
correct an already-violated state. Aerospace is rated experimental (Chapter 7.3) and the

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 227 ===
CHAPTER 9. COMPARISON TO ALTERNATIVE SAFETY APPROACHES 193
20% violation rate reﬂects a combination of domain severity and incomplete calibration.
9.4 Why the Alternatives Fail
The three alternatives share a root failure: they evaluate safety conditions on the observed
state ˆxt without correcting for telemetry reliability.
• Tube MPC ﬁxes the tube width at w = wﬂoor. If the true fault rate exceeds the
assumed disturbance bound, violations occur; if the fault rate is lower, the tube over-
constrains and increases economic cost. Across six domains with diﬀerent fault proﬁles,
no single wﬂoor is simultaneously tight and safe.
• CBF enforces h(ˆxt) ≥ 0. Under degraded telemetry, ˆxt is an optimistic estimate of
x∗
t . When the gap ∥ˆxt − x∗
t ∥ exceeds the safety margin, the barrier certiﬁes an unsafe
action. This is exactly the OASG (Deﬁnition in Chapter 3.3).
• Lagrangian Safe RL trains a penalty that penalises h(ˆxt)< 0. At deployment, the
penalty signal is still evaluated on ˆxt. The residual softness of the Lagrangian penalty
(captured by the 0. 25 soft-margin parameter) means hard constraints are not enforced
even on the observed state.
DC3S closes all three failure modes simultaneously. The OQE score wt provides a contin-
uous measurement of telemetry reliability; the reliability-aware uncertainty inﬂation (RUI)
scales the safety margin proportionally; and the safe-action repair (SAR) enforces hard con-
straints on the inﬂated set. The adaptive nature of wt is what distinguishes DC3S from a
ﬁxed-tube strategy: the margin widens exactly when telemetry is degraded and contracts
when telemetry is clean, minimising both violations and unnecessary interventions.
9.5 Intervention Rate and Economic Cost
A safety strategy that over-intervenes imposes an economic cost even if it achieves low TSVR.
Table 9.1 shows that Tube MPC’s IR of 74.5% on the A V domain is more than twice DC3S’s
34.5%. In an energy management context, excessive intervention means unnecessary curtail-
ment of battery dispatch, reducing revenue. In an industrial context, it means conservative
setpoints that reduce throughput. DC3S’s OQE-adaptive margin avoids this trade-oﬀ: it is
conservative exactly when telemetry is degraded and permissive when telemetry is reliable.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 228 ===
CHAPTER 9. COMPARISON TO ALTERNATIVE SAFETY APPROACHES 194
9.6 Summary
DC3S is the only strategy in this comparison that closes the OASG across all proof-validated
domains while maintaining a lower intervention rate than conservative alternatives. The
three alternative strategies all fail for the same reason: they evaluate safety conditions on
the observed state and cannot account for telemetry degradation severity. The OQE-to-RUI
coupling in DC3S is the structural mechanism that resolves this failure mode.

Chapter 10
A Domain-Parametric Kernel for Safe
Control Under Degraded Observation
This chapter is the control-theory extension of the dissertation. Its purpose is not to add more
runtime rows; it is to isolate the domain-parametric kernel that the rest of the monograph
instantiates. The central claim is deliberately narrow: under degraded observation, safe
control is governed by an observation-consistent state set, a reliability-conditioned tightening
law, a repair map, and a causal certiﬁcate surface. Battery remains the only full proof
reference surface. Healthcare remains a defended instantiation, and autonomous vehicles
remain defended under a bounded contract within the active three-domain program.
The point of the chapter is to make that claim precise enough that a reader can separate
three things cleanly: what the reference-row proof already establishes, what the universal
kernel adds, and where the current transfer boundary still stops. The implementation follows
the same boundary. The canonical production kernel realizes the typed repair-and-certify
contract, while the formal arguments below isolate the control objects that must exist in any
domain instantiation.
In the paper-facing presentation, this chapter should be read as the ﬁnal piece of a
promoted but scoped theory arc. T11 is the main transfer theorem. T9 and T10 are de-
fended as observation-ambiguity lower bounds after the theorem-promotion gates discharge
their constants and domain assumptions for the locked Battery, A V, and Healthcare arti-
facts. T_minimax remains scoped to ﬁnite ambiguity classes; no unrestricted global minimax
optimality claim is made.

Mathematical contract vs. domain discharge. This chapter separates theorem ker-
nels from empirical validation. The mathematical contract consists only of the displayed
hypotheses, Appendix B assumptions, and typed transfer obligations: uncertainty-set cov-
erage, state-conditioned safe-set correctness, repair membership, fallback admissibility, and
domain-adapter validity. The domain discharge artifacts instantiate constants and check mix-
ing, boundary-mass, and witness conditions for the promoted Battery, A V, and Healthcare
runs. In particular, domain discharge artifacts are evidence, not an extra hidden assumption.
Consequently, the transfer claim is contract-universal, not unrestricted-global : any physical-
AI domain that implements the ORIUS runtime contract may reuse the proof kernel, but
each new domain must provide its own discharge artifacts before numerical constants or
deployment-facing claims are promoted.

even when observed-state evaluation looks safe. The broader universality claim is that this
pathology is not a single-domain accident; T9 records the corresponding assumption-qualiﬁed
extension under explicit mixing and boundary-reachability assumptions. The locked Battery,
A V, and Healthcare artifacts discharge these constants through the promotion evidence ﬁles

Theorem 10.2 (Universal Impossibility, T9) . Consider a closed-loop controller that does
not adapt its safety margin to the telemetry reliability process wt. Under persistent obser-
vation degradation with non-zero fault rate and under the standing boundary-reachability
assumptions used throughout this dissertation, there exists a constant c> 0 such that
E[VT ] ≥ cdT,
whered is the mean degradation rate and VT is the true-state violation count over the horizon.
Proof (martingale–φ-mixing argument). The proof proceeds in three steps.
Step 1: φ-mixing fault process. By Assumption A10b (Chapter 4.2), the fault injection
process {Ft} is φ-mixing with coeﬃcient φ(k) → 0 as k → ∞ . Concretely, φ(k) ≤ Cϕρk for
constants Cϕ > 0 and ρ∈ (0, 1), so a buﬀer gap of τmix = ⌈log(1/ε)/ |logρ|⌉steps makes
any two windows ε-approximately independent in total variation.
Step 2: Separated windows and per-window risk. Partition [1,T ] into k analysis
windows of length ℓseparated by buﬀer gaps of length τmix, choosing ℓ= ⌊T/ (2k)⌋ so that
k ≤ T/ (2(ℓ+τmix)). The reference-domain no-free-safety witness (Theorem 4.27) is applied
inside each window: if the controller does not adapt its safety margin to the OQE signal wt,
the observation gap drives the true state across the constraint boundary while the observed
state appears feasible. The per-window violation probability satisﬁes pwin ≥ cd for a positive
constantc> 0 that depends on the boundary-reachability conditions of Assumption A11.
Step 3: Martingale concentration. Deﬁne Dj = Z win

the true state carries over between adjacent windows. The φ-mixing approach replaces exact
independence with approximate independence quantiﬁed by φ(τmix); the buﬀer gaps absorb
the coupling cost without changing the Ω(dT ) scaling.
T9 is a supporting defended necessity extension for the universal kernel: under the explicit
A10b/A11 hypotheses, and under the locked three-domain mixing and witness artifacts,
it records why a controller that ignores reliability cannot remove the violation scaling by
algorithmic tuning alone. Broader portability still requires rerunning the same discharge
builder on the new domain rather than inheriting the Battery/A V/Healthcare constants.
10.3 Boundary-Indistinguishability Lower Bound (T10)
The core ORIUS bound from the reference domain is
E[VT ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T,
whereαis the miscoverage level and ¯w is the mean reliability. The monograph extension asks
whether that envelope is merely conservative or whether it captures the right scaling law.
The locked T10 evidence ﬁles discharge the boundary-mass, paired safe/unsafe observation-
law, and TV-bridge assumptions for Battery, A V, and Healthcare. In the Battery case, the
unsafe-side law is supplied by the locked 48-hour boundary-violation trace and transformed
into the same margin units as the runtime trace.
Theorem 10.4 (Boundary-indistinguishability lower bound, T10) . Fix a horizon T . For
each step t, assume there exists a boundary-testing subproblem with two latent hypotheses
H0,t (safe side of the boundary) and H1,t (unsafe side of the boundary) such that:
1. the controller acts only through the degraded observation channel;
2. the observation laws P0,t and P1,t satisfy TV(P0,t,P 1,t ) ≤ wt;
3. the unsafe-side prior satisﬁes P(H1,t ) ≥ pt for some boundary-mass sequence pt ∈ [0, 1].
4. every unsafe-side mistaken release has OASG severity at least δt ≥ 0.
Then every controller πsatisﬁes
E[VT (π)] ≥ 1
2
T∑
t=1
pt(1 − wt).

Moreover, the severity-weighted OASG satisﬁes
E[OASGT (π)] ≥ 1
2
T∑
t=1
δtpt(1 − wt).
In particular, if pt ≥ α/2 for all t, then
E[VT (π)] ≥ 1
4α(1 − ¯w)T.
Proof. Fix a step t and consider the controller’s binary decision problem on the safe/unsafe
boundary subproblem. By Le Cam’s lemma,
max
(
P[ψt errs |H0,t ], P[ψt errs |H1,t ]
)
≥ 1 − TV(P0,t,P 1,t )
2 ≥ 1 − wt
2 .

and at least δt severity-weighted OASG, so
P[Zt = 1] ≥ P(H1,t ) ·P[ψt errs |H1,t ] ≥ pt ·1 − wt

Summing across the horizon and applying linearity of expectation yields

δtpt(1 − wt).
The special case pt ≥ α/2 then implies the stated 1
4α(1− ¯w)T frontier. No stronger conclusion
is claimed without a domain-speciﬁc argument that justiﬁes the boundary-mass and severity
sequences pt,δt.
Remark 10.5. T10 is an assumption-qualiﬁed supporting extension. It is an information-
theoretic lower bound for any controller that only sees the degraded observation channel
under the explicit boundary-mass and indistinguishability hypotheses above. It does not
claim a globally sharp minimax constant for every observation model, but it does show that
the α(1 − ¯w)T scaling cannot be dismissed as a pure artifact of one repair heuristic. In
its OASG form, the theorem states the explicit rate: the unavoidable gap grows linearly
with the degradation budget

severity.
The resulting reliability-risk frontier is useful even when read conservatively:
¯w⋆ = 1 − r⋆
αT,
for a target episode-level violation budget r⋆. The canonical runtime helper for the conser-
vative envelope lives in src/orius/universal_theory/risk_bounds.py. The richer fron-
tier analysis used in this chapter is implemented in src/orius/universal/reliability_
safety_frontier.py. Together these helpers make the central design message operational:
degraded observation imposes a quantitative infrastructure requirement, not just a qualita-
tive warning.
10.4 Typed Structural Transfer and Failure-Mode Con-
verse (T11)
The ﬁnal dissertation claim no longer rests on a vague notion of “adapter portability. ” The
reusable objects are now explicit both in the writing and in code.
Deﬁnition 10.6 (Universal adapter contract) . A domain instantiation is compatible with
the degraded-observation transfer claim if it supplies:
1. an observation packet and parsed state;
2. a reliability assessment wt ∈ [0, 1];
3. an observation-consistent state set Ut;
4. a domain safety speciﬁcation and tightened safe-action set At;
5. a repair operator returning a safe action or admissible fallback;
6. a causal certiﬁcate recording the repaired action, uncertainty state, reliability state,
and assumption tags.
In the simpliﬁed reference harness used for theorem miniatures, these objects collapse to four
adapter operations: observe(), uncertainty_set(), repair(), and fallback().
The canonical typed implementation is provided by src/orius/universal_theory/
contracts.py and src/orius/universal_theory/kernel.py. The smaller reference har-
ness in src/orius/universal/contract.py packages the same idea into a compact machine-
checkable contract with ﬁve invariants:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 236 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION202
Inv 1 Repair membership. The repaired action lies in the tightened safe-action set.
Inv 2 Monotone tightening. Lower reliability never enlarges the admissible set.
Inv 3 Reliability boundedness. Reliability remains in [0, 1].
Inv 4 Calibration consistency. The inﬂation law preserves the base conformal surface at
full reliability.
Inv 5 Failure completeness. Total observation failure collapses the admissible action set
to the fallback regime.
Theorem 10.7 (Typed structural transfer theorem, T11) . Let D be a domain instantiation
for the degraded-observation safe-control problem in Deﬁnition 10.1. Assume that, for every
step t:
1. the constructed observation-consistent state set satisﬁes P[xt ∈Ut | Ht] ≥ 1 − α;
2. the tightened safe-action set is sound for that uncertainty set, in the sense that every
action in Asafe
t (Ut) keeps the next state inside S for all x ∈ Ut and all admissible
disturbance;
3. the repair map returns an element of Asafe
t (Ut) whenever that set is non-empty;
4. when the tightened safe-action set is empty, the fallback action is admissible and pre-
serves safety from any current safe state.
Then the repaired action produced by the ORIUS kernel satisﬁes the same one-step safety-
preservation statement as the reference theorem:
P[xt+1 ∈ S | Ht] ≥ 1 − α.
In this theorem the four listed obligations are the entire forward transfer surface. No broader
episode-level claim is inherited unless the new domain also supplies an explicit predictable
per-step risk budget as in Corollary 10.9. The converse remains a separate failure-mode
proposition rather than part of T11 itself.
Proof. Fix a stept and condition on the observation history Ht. On the event Et = {xt ∈Ut},
Assumption (2) implies that every action in Asafe
t (Ut) preserves membership in the safe set
at the next step. If the tightened safe-action set is non-empty, then Assumption (3) ensures
that the repaired action returned by the kernel lies in that set, hence preserves safety on

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 237 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION203
Et. If the tightened safe-action set is empty, then Assumption (4) ensures that the fallback
action preserves safety from any current safe state. Therefore,
P[xt+1 ∈ S | Ht] ≥ P[Et | Ht] ≥ 1 − α,
which is the one-step safety-preservation statement.
Corollary 10.8 (T10_T11_ObservationAmbiguitySandwich: Covered Observation-Ambi-
guity Optimality). Leth : X → O be the observation map. For an observation o, deﬁne the
ambiguity class
B(o) = {x ∈ X :h(x) = o}
and the common safe core
K(o) =

C(x).
For every mandatory-release observation-only controller π: O → A ,
TSVR(π) ≥ EO
[

P(a /∈C(X ⋆) |O)
]
.
In particular, if K(o) = ∅ on a positive-probability ambiguity class, no observation-only
mandatory-release controller can guarantee zero true-state violation on that class. Con-
versely, if ORIUS constructs Xt with P(X ⋆

ut ∈

and under exact coverage X ⋆

Proof. Condition on an observation O = o. Any observation-only mandatory-release con-
troller must choose a single action a = π(o) for all states in B(o). Its conditional violation
probability is P(a /∈C(X ⋆) |O =o), and the best possible observation-only choice is there-
fore the displayed minimum over a ∈ A . Taking expectation over O gives the lower bound.
IfK(o) = ∅, every candidate action is unsafe for at least one state in B(o), so the conditional
lower bound is positive whenever that unsafe state has positive conditional mass.
For the ORIUS upper bound, split the violation event according to whether X ⋆
t ∈ X t.
On the coverage event, the released action lies in C(x) for every x ∈ X t and hence in C(X ⋆
t ),
so the violation probability on that event is zero. The only remaining probability mass is

the coverage-miss event, bounded by α.
Promoted domain runtime lemmas. The A V and healthcare rows do not promote a
new universal theorem. They are supporting runtime lemmas under T11: A V is bounded
to the emitted brake-hold postcondition, and healthcare is bounded to the emitted fail-safe
alert-release postcondition. In both cases, missing T11 status, failed T11 obligations, invalid
certiﬁcates, or false true-state postconditions fail the runtime witness closed.
Corollary 10.9 (Episode aggregation under explicit per-step budgets) . If, in addition, the
instantiated domain supplies a predictable per-step risk budget sequence rt satisfying
P[xt+1 /∈ S | Ht] ≤ rt
for all t, then
E[VT ] ≤
T∑
t=1
rt.
When a particular domain instantiation justiﬁes the reference-row envelope rt ≤ α(1 − wt),
the episode bound E[VT ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T follows immediately.
Proof. Let Zt = 1{xt+1 /∈ S}. The assumed bound implies
E[Zt | Ht] ≤ rt.
Taking expectations and summing over t gives
E[VT ] =
T∑
t=1
E[Zt] ≤
T∑

rt.
The reference-row envelope is the special case rt ≤ α(1 − wt).
Proposition 10.10 (Failure of any transfer obligation breaks the reference proof pattern) .
If any one of the four T11 obligations fails, then there exists a domain instantiation for
which the reference transfer argument breaks.
Proof. If obligation (1) fails, the latent state may lie outside Ut with probability larger than
α, so the repair step can be safe only for the wrong uncertainty set and the one-step safety
argument no longer applies. If obligation (2) fails, an action may belong to the purported
tightened set while still allowing a successor outside S for some state in Ut, so soundness is
lost directly. If obligation (3) fails, the repair map can return an action outside the tightened
safe set even when a safe repaired action exists, which again breaks the one-step argument.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 239 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION205
If obligation (4) fails, the kernel can reach a state where the tightened safe-action set is
empty and no admissible fallback exists, so the transfer proof has no safe release path. In
each case the failure is structural rather than rhetorical: the missing obligation removes one
of the exact proof steps used in the T11 forward direction.
T11 is therefore a typed structural transfer theorem paired with an explicit failure-mode
converse, not a blanket declaration that every evaluated row already satisﬁes those obli-
gations. The energy-management reference row provides the full reference proof. Other
domains enter the monograph only when their instantiation evidence is strong enough to
support the corresponding one-step obligations, and the stronger episode-level claim appears
only when a domain-speciﬁc per-step risk budget is justiﬁed explicitly.
10.4.1 Machine-Checkable Contract Surfaces
Two levels of executable checking now exist in the repository.
1. Production structural contract. src/orius/universal_theory/contracts.py
exposes the typed DomainInstantiation protocol together with a lightweight struc-
tural veriﬁer. The focused regression test tests/test_universal_theory.py checks
canonical battery, healthcare and vehicle adapters against that surface and then exer-
cises the new universal step kernel end-to-end.
2. Reference invariant harness. src/orius/universal/contract.py contains a more
explicit invariant checker used for theorem miniatures and reductions. The focused
tests tests/test_universal_contract.py and tests/test_unification.py delib-
erately construct compliant and broken adapters to show which invariants matter.
The simpliﬁed usage pattern is:
from orius.universal.contract import ContractVerifier
verifier = ContractVerifier(adapter, alpha=0.05)
verifier.check(z_t=np.array([0.0]), q_t=0.1)
That harness is intentionally modest. It does not replace the defended domain-speciﬁc
artifacts. It makes the transfer theorem executable: the reader can see the exact invariants,
violate them, and observe the resulting contract failure.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 240 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION206
10.5 Uniﬁcation: ORIUS Subsumes Prior Frameworks
Theorem 10.11 (Uniﬁcation). The following prior safe-control families can be represented
as special cases of the ORIUS degraded-observation kernel:
1. Control Barrier Functions (CBF). A ﬁxed reliability policy wt ≡ 1 together with
barrier-deﬁned action repair.
2. Robust MPC. A constant reliability policy induced by a ﬁxed tube radius together
with projection onto the tube-tightened feasible set.
3. Safe RL. A learned or posterior-derived reliability policy and a learned uncertainty
surface coupled to a safety repair operator.
ORIUS strictly generalises these reductions by allowing the reliability signal to vary with the
actual observation channel and by attaching an explicit certiﬁcate surface to each repaired
action.
Proof sketch. The repository contains executable reference reductions in src/orius/universal/
unification.py. CBFAsORIUS ﬁxeswt ≡ 1 and uses barrier-style projection. RobustMPCAsORIUS
ﬁxeswt to the normalised tube radius and uses tube-style tightening. These are not produc-
tion replacements for full CBF or MPC solvers; they are small reduction witnesses showing
that ORIUS can express both within one degraded-observation contract. Safe RL ﬁts the
same pattern once posterior concentration or uncertainty drives the reliability term.
The uniﬁcation is summarised in Table 10.2.
10.6 Constraint-Class Mismatch: When 1-D Action
Repair Is Insuﬃcient
The DC3S shield projects each candidate action onto a tightened interval derived from the
conformal uncertainty set. This is suﬃcient whenever the domain safety constraint is a
box—or more generally, whenever it decomposes as a monotone function of a single action
coordinate. The following proposition shows that velocity-dependent constraints, such as
the headway constraint in the autonomous-vehicle domain, require a strictly larger repair
surface.

Proposition 10.12 (Constraint-class mismatch barrier) . Let the true safety constraint be
the velocity-dependent headway condition
ϕ(xt) =
[
gapt ≥ dmin +τvt
]
, d min,τ >0,
wherext = (gap t,v t) and the gap obeys gapt+1 = gap t + ∆ t (vlead − vt − at ∆t). Suppose the
DC3S shield projects the candidate acceleration into a one-dimensional safe set
At =
[
amin, (bt − vt)/ ∆t
]
, b t = gap t/ (∆t +τ) ∈ R,
and suppose that there exist states (gapt,v t) with vt > (gapt − dmin)/τ—that is, xt /∈ S
already at the start of the step. Then:
1. No action at ∈ [amin,a max] moves xt+1 into S in one step, because the constraint viola-
tion is determined by current (vt, gapt), not by the next-step speed the shield controls.
2. The shield’s one-dimensional repair is therefore infeasible for current-step constraint
satisfaction: ϕ(xt) depends on the pre-action state, not on the post-action state.
3. DC3S’s T3 upper bound E[VT ] ≤ α(1 − ¯w)T remains valid for the next-step constraint

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 242 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION208
(the set the shield can actually control), but it does not apply to the current-step plant-
level constraint that the A V evaluation harness measures.
Consequently, the A V domain incurs a structural proof gap: the shield repairs future-step
feasibility, but the plant reports current-step violations.
Proof. If vt > (gapt − dmin)/τ, then gapt < dmin +τvt, so ϕ(xt) = False regardless of at.
The action at aﬀects vt+1 =vt +at ∆t and gapt+1, but not the evaluation ϕ(xt). The shield’s
admissible set At therefore provides no mechanism to avoid the step- t violation; it only
constrains step- (t + 1).
Corollary 10.13 (A V promotion routes from the original headway mismatch) . Discharging
the original autonomous-vehicle headway mismatch requires at least one of the following:
(a) Augmented state repair : the shield solves a two-dimensional quadratic program over
(a,v ) that explicitly enforces gapt ≥ dmin +τvt as a current-state feasibility constraint,
activating a full-brake fallback when the state is already outside S.
(b) TTC reformulation : replace the headway constraint with a time-to-collision lower
bound, TTCt ≥ tmin, whose gradient with respect to at is non-zero and admits a valid
one-dimensional repair.
(c) Predictive entry barrier : add a one-step lookahead check that refuses to enter states
from which ϕ(xt+1) = False is unavoidable, reducing the observed TSVR to the T3
envelope.
Without one of these routes, the A V residual is a structural residual, not a conformal-coverage
residual.
Current closure route. This dissertation adopts the TTC reformulation together with
the predictive entry barrier as the canonical A V promotion path. The two-dimensional (a,v )
repair remains a valid future route, but it is not the route claimed here. Under the bounded
TTC contract, the closure matrix now records the A V row as proof-validated: locked replay
reduces TSVR to zero on the canonical validation harness, the conservative entry-barrier
soundness cases clear, and the CertOS lifecycle executes on the same adapter surface. This
promotion is deliberately narrow. It does not promote every richer headway, lane-change,
or multi-agent vehicle setting to deeper proof depth within the same bounded three-domain
program.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 243 ===
CHAPTER 10. A DOMAIN-PARAMETRIC KERNEL FOR SAFE CONTROL UNDER DEGRADED OBSER V ATION209
10.7 Chapter Summary
This chapter closes the monograph at the correct control-theory level.
1. T9 is a supporting extension that frames observation-aware safety logic as structurally
necessary under persistent degradation once the domain mixing and witness constants
are discharged.
2. T10 is a supporting extension that turns mean reliability into a quantitative infrastructure-
to-risk frontier under explicit binary-testing and boundary-law hypotheses.
3. T11 packages universality as a typed kernel and adapter template, not as a claim that
every domain already has battery-level proof closure.
4. The uniﬁcation theorem explains how prior safe-control families sit inside the same
degraded-observation framework.
The central theorem contribution is now precise: the safe-control object under degraded
telemetry is not the nominal state alone but a reliability-indexed observation-consistent state
set together with a repair map and a causal certiﬁcate surface. Battery supplies the full
proof witness. T9 and T10, as assumption-qualiﬁed supporting extensions, record necessity
and lower-bound directions after the locked domains discharge the explicit constants. T11
shows how the same kernel transfers when a new domain discharges the explicit structural
obligations. This is a defensible control-theory claim; it is not an assertion that all evaluated
domains already enjoy equal proof closure.

This appendix is the canonical assumption register for the defended ORIUS theorem program.
The active theorem registry in reports/publication/theorem_registry.yml references
these IDs directly, and the audit scripts reject theorem rows that cite an unknown assumption
label.
b.1 How to Read This Register
Each assumption is stated as a theorem-facing predicate and then grounded in the runtime
artifacts. The defended register is the promotion-gated theorem matrix, not the older draft/-
supporting split. Scoped surfaces such as T minimax and T sensor may cite these assumptions
only within their explicit claim boundaries.

almost surely. This is stronger than the sub-Gaussian tail control in Assumption A9 and
is used when the proof requires a deterministic one-step postcondition rather than a high-
probability statement. Battery interpretation: the one-step battery model error is ab-
sorbed as an explicit MWh slack in the FTIT/shield tightening margin.

quired by the current T9 windowing proof.

boundary-sensitive state under admissible de-

dent of the controller action stream given the
history.
A13 TV bridge TV(P0,t , P 1,t ) ≤ wt for the T10 binary observa-

APPENDIX B. ASSUMPTION REGISTER 216
Assumption 10 (A2 — Telemetry-state bridge) . There exists a known monotone function
g : [0, 1] → R+ such that
|ˆxt − xt| ≤g(wt), g (1) = 0,
for all t. Battery interpretation: the OQE reliability score wt controls the worst-case
telemetry-state discrepancy used by the tightened SOC tube.
Assumption 11 (A3 — Feasible safe repair) . Whenever the runtime invokes repair, the
tightened safe-action set is non-empty. Battery interpretation: the battery shield pro-
jection has at least one admissible charge/discharge action inside the FTIT-restricted SOC
region. Boundary note: this is an existence condition on the active runtime constraint
set. It must be rechecked for each new domain or operating regime; it is not a theorem that
arbitrary constraints always admit a safe repair.
Assumption 12 (A4 — Known one-step dynamics) . The one-step increment map ∆(a) =
f (x,a ) − x is known exactly to the controller. Battery interpretation: the SOC update
induced by charge, discharge, step duration, and eﬃciencies is computed explicitly by the
runtime.
Assumption 13 (A5 — Absorbed monotone tightening) . Let mt denote the reliability-
conditioned tightening margin. The defended T2/T3 surface uses the absorbed margin
m⋆
t =mt +ϵmodel,
and assumes m⋆
t is monotone non-increasing in wt and bounded above by a ﬁnite constant.
Battery interpretation: the FTIT margin combines the base reliability/certiﬁcate slack
with the one-step model-error allowance before the shield constructs the admissible SOC
region.
Assumption 14 (A6 — Bounded detector lag) . Each supported telemetry fault class is
detected within a ﬁnite lag τmax known to the runtime. Battery interpretation: dropout,
stale, delay, out-of-order, spike, and drift channels each have a ﬁnite audit-locked response
horizon in the runtime conﬁguration.
Assumption 15 (A7 — Causal certiﬁcate rule) . The certiﬁcate state obeys
certt =h(z1,...,z t),
for a causal update rule h. Battery interpretation: certiﬁcate issuance, renewal, and
runtime bookkeeping never use future telemetry.
